#### Abstracts
##### **DiaCap: Jointly Learning Who Speaks When for Audiovisual Dialogue Captioning**
2611.33153v1 by Binfei Ji, Zhiwen Cao, Anqi Yang, Prem Seetharaman, Shuo Yang, Ratheesh Kalarot, Yingyan Celine Lin, Mingze Xu

We present DiaCap, an audiovisual LLM for comprehensive dialogue captioning, which produces the transcript, timestamps, and a visual description of the speaker for every utterance in a video. Considering that utterance timing and audiovisual diarization are tightly coupled, DiaCap unifies the tasks in a single model, trained in two complementary stages. First, we train DiaCap on 225K curated clips, using synthetic captions that densely label every utterance in detail. Second, we further refine the model via reinforcement learning on 2.9K human-corrected clips, using fine-grained feedback that directly evaluates utterance timing, speaker grouping, and speaker binding. On DiaDemBench, which we extend with utterance-level timestamps and subtitle redaction, both DiaCap with off-the-shelf transcripts and its end-to-end variant DiaCap-E2E achieve strong results. Specifically, DiaCap performs on par with Gemini 4 Argon in audiovisual diarization while clearly outperforming it in timestamp accuracy (76.59 vs. 42.76 TIME). It also maintains strong diarization on multilingual and minute-long videos, and transfers to audiovisual question answering.

摘要：

##### **CoT-Proxy: Cross-Format Transfer for Distilling Long-Horizon Policies**
2611.33148v1 by Dawid J. Kopiczko, Tijmen Blankevoort, Yuki M. Asano

Reasoning models become long-horizon agents through reinforcement learning or, more cheaply, through fine-tuning on a stronger teacher's trajectories, which contain the teacher's chain-of-thought as well as its actions. The standard fine-tuning approach imitates both, yet the strongest teachers are closed models whose chain-of-thought is hidden behind an API. We show that the chain-of-thought can be left out altogether and that the long-horizon policy can be distilled from the teacher's actions alone. The student is trained on these trajectories with its reasoning block left empty, while a KL loss on a replay buffer of its own generations limits drift from its initial model. Surprisingly, the policy learned from these trajectories with an empty reasoning block carries over to inference with thinking enabled, where the student reasons in its own style while acting like the teacher, an effect we call cross-format transfer. Distilling the actions of Qwen3.6-27B raises the task success of Qwen3-4B on AppWorld from 7% to 18% and that of Qwen3-32B from 12% to 25%. We further introduce CoT-proxies, short summaries of the teacher's reasoning placed after the empty reasoning block, which raise Qwen3-4B further, to 35%. Since neither actions nor CoT-proxies need the hidden chain-of-thought, the recipe applies unchanged to closed teachers. Distilling GPT-5.6 Luna from its visible outputs and the reasoning summaries its API returns raises Qwen3-4B from 7% to 48% on AppWorld and from 3% to 28% on SWE-bench Verified.

摘要：

##### **BriConstrain: Geometrically Constrained Decoding for Autoregressive Brick Assembly Generation**
2611.33135v1 by Nadav Magar, Amir Hertz, Ariel Shamir, Or Patashnik

Language models can generate LEGO$^\circledR$ assemblies from text, but their builds often contain parts that collide or attach to nothing. The usual approach of rejecting and resampling invalid placements discards decoded tokens without guaranteeing a valid build. We present BriConstrain, a constrained decoding method that restricts a pretrained language model's next-token probabilities before sampling, steering it towards valid placements. Because the validity of a brick placement depends on the non-predetermined build state, it cannot be compiled into a fixed grammar and must be checked dynamically. However, checking the validity of a brick before sampling is challenging as the space of valid placements is combinatorially large and cannot be enumerated exhaustively. Our key idea is to narrow the search space by constraining how parts attach rather than which parts attach to each other. Concretely, given the model's predictions of the currently placed part, and the previously placed parent it wants to connect to, we constrain the model's choice of how they attach. This allows us to span the space of part attachments, using a precomputed geometric oracle, and to effectively filter out colliding options. With valid attachment candidates in hand, we bias the model's next-token distribution towards the remaining valid candidates. We evaluate our method on two LEGO$^\circledR$ formats and models: BrickNet, which annotates connectivity explicitly, and our proposed Implicit Connectivity LDraw Format (ICLF), which uses numeric values for placements and leaves connectivity implicit. Our method raises collision-free builds from 31% to 91% on BrickNet and from 15% to 65% on ICLF, and outperforms rejection sampling on both formats at about 60% of its decoding cost.

##### **A Bias in Disguise: Tiny Gradient Edits Stop a Small Language Model's Thinking Loops and Complement a Frontier Model's Weight Edit**
2611.33133v1 by Hussamuddin Syed

Qwen3.5-0.8B rarely finishes reasoning in thinking mode: at a 4,096-token budget only 1 or 2 of 500 MATH-500 answers close their think block (two seeds), and accuracy is 15.4%. A recent demonstration had GPT-6 Astra edit 131,072 of the model's weights without gradients and reported MATH-500 rising from 7.9% to 48.1% at a 32,768-token budget. We provide the missing gradient baseline, then combine the two edits. Fine-tuning only the 140 to 139,750 weights with the largest gradient, on the model's own correct answers to MATH training problems, raises thinking-mode MATH-500 at 4k tokens to 39.0-45.5% after 2-9 minutes of training on one GPU; 50 training examples suffice, and 1,398 random positions give only 16.3%. The gradient concentrates the edit on the most nearly constant channel of each of the first three layers. Because the model has no bias terms, an edit confined to those channels acts as a constant vector added to the residual stream: adding three fixed vectors to the unedited model matches the accuracy of such an edit. Under the Astra protocol (32k tokens, presence penalty 1.5), the Astra edit scores 52.1% (finished and correct) in our reproduction and our full-strength edits reach 45.6-45.7%, but ours win at every budget up to 16k tokens (39.5% against 6.7% at 4k). The two edits are complementary. The Astra edit plus our 139,750-weight edit at half strength scores 62.2% at 32k, 10 points above the Astra edit alone (p = 3e-9), with half as many tokens; our half-strength edit alone scores 46.7%.

##### **SAG-Mem-1.0 Technical Report**
2611.33124v1 by Yuchao Wu, XingCheng Liang, Xuanze Wang, Yini Huang, Yue Wu

Long-term conversational memory requires retrieving complementary facts scattered across dialogue sessions, including intermediate evidence with limited similarity to the original question. Existing approaches often rely on derived memory representations or persistent relational structures, introducing construction costs and potentially losing source details. We present SAG-Mem, a conversational memory framework that preserves complete dialogue messages in non-overlapping chunks and discovers complementary evidence through query-time lexical expansion. Memory construction requires no model inference, using a lexical index without LLM-based extraction, summarization, or semantic relation construction. At query time, BM25 retrieves initial candidates, an evidence evaluator identifies useful chunks, and lexical cues from selected evidence guide an additional retrieval round. Retrieved dialogue is further annotated with calendar interpretations of supported relative-time expressions, preserving the original wording and temporal granularity. We also introduce SAG-Mem-Lite, an alternative configuration that replaces evidence scoring with embedding-based candidate scoring, achieving lower operational cost than Full Context and all memory-based baselines across both benchmarks with low query latency. On LoCoMo and LongMemEval, SAG-Mem achieves overall scores of 83.08 and 80.20, respectively, the highest among the evaluated methods on both benchmarks. On LongMemEval, it exceeds the strongest compared baseline by 7.20 points. SAG-Mem-Lite achieves scores of 77.09 and 77.20, respectively. These results support dialogue preservation and evidence-guided retrieval expansion as an effective design for long-term conversational memory.

##### **MetaFORGE: A Meta-Learning Framework for Harness Optimization in Automated Code Generation for Parallel Computing**
2611.33119v1 by Scott Piersall, Yang Gao, Shenyang Liu, Liqiang Wang

Large language models (LLMs) can generate parallel programs, but their correctness and performance depend strongly on the instructions that guide generation.   Across six MPI tasks and three workload sizes, \textsc{MetaFORGE} improves static-analysis quality, runtime, and load balance relative to zero-shot generation and generally exceeds the evaluated \textsc{TextGrad} iterative baseline. A model-matched refinement ablation shows that the MetaFORGE advantage persists when its refinement model is reduced to \texttt{gpt-4o}. ##### **Draft Calibration for Speculative Decoding: Accelerating LLM Serving by Matching Draft and Target Distributions**
2611.33111v1 by Yuxing Liu, Wei Zuo, Walid Krichene, Tong Zhang, Li Zhang

Speculative decoding accelerates large language model (LLM) inference by employing a lightweight draft model to propose draft tokens and allowing the target model to verify the new tokens in parallel to improve inference-time compute utilization. The speedup relies on the acceptance probability of draft tokens in target verification, which is directly related to the gap $\|p-q\|_1$ between the target distribution $p$ and the draft distribution $q$. In this paper, we study draft calibration of speculative decoding to improve the speedup of speculative decoding, where we employ post-hoc methods to calibrate the draft distribution to mitigate the distribution gap. We observe that though current community open-source draft models may learn the top candidate token correctly, they are generally underconfident compared to the target model, suggesting a consistent distribution mismatch. To mitigate the effect of this mismatch on the acceptance rate of the draft models, we try various post-hoc calibration methods, including standard temperature scaling and token-wise calibration. Our results suggest that a standard temperature scaling applied to the draft distribution improves the acceptance length by up to approximately 10\% with almost no additional cost across different tasks. Throughput analysis further verifies that this improvement in serving efficiency. Analysis of more general calibration methods reveals further potential room for improvement.

##### **Delegent: A Multi-Agent Harness That Reduces Cost without Sacrificing Performance**
2611.33098v1 by Lingwei Gu, Jimmy Lin

Agents built on frontier language models are expensive. At every step, the frontier model re-reads its entire context and executes every subtask regardless of its difficulty. To test it, we build Delegent, a simple and effective agent harness built on Claude Code, in which the frontier model stays in charge of every task but hands focused subtasks, such as investigating code, running tests, and searching a corpus, to SLM subagents. Existing approaches such as cascades and model routers usually choose which model executes the entire task, whereas in Delegent, the frontier model always leads the execution and delegates easy subtasks to the SLM. No router or model is trained, so either model can be swapped freely. With the help of a 27B FP8 SLM, Delegent reduces the frontier cost of long-horizon coding by 67% and of agentic search by 59%, with no loss in task performance, and even a 2B SLM that fits on a laptop reduces the coding cost by 66%. The savings grow with the legwork that the frontier model would otherwise do itself, and a short instruction, which leaves the main coding tasks to the frontier model, accounts for most of the saving and keeps the 2B SLM from losing tasks. Across 9,136 attempts by four coding agents, the difficulty lies in stored state. Feedback that points to the first state mismatch raises acceptance over output-only feedback in all 12 agent--task cells of a matched study, by 25.0 to 46.9 percentage points, and halves the agent-hours per accepted design. Regraded on new sequences and conditions, 45 of 69 accepted designs fail within the 32 steps their tests required. More than a third of attempts to speed up a correct design end without one. ##### **Uncertainty-Aware Candidate Generation for Entity Linking in Cyber Threat Intelligence**
2611.33086v1 by Nasser Alkhulaifi, Direnc Pekaslan, Christian Wagner

Entity Resolution (ER) pipelines often collapse ambiguity into early hard decisions: upstream Named Entity Recognition (NER) selects a single span and type, and downstream Entity Linking (EL) treats that choice as ground truth. This discards valuable information about the ambiguity of the underlying data (e.g., span boundary disagreement and type confusion), leading to brittle systems that fail unpredictably when faced with uncertainty in inputs. Therefore, this work presents a proof-of-concept for uncertainty-aware ER that treats uncertainty as native data that should be preserved and exploited throughout ER pipelines. We (i) capture uncertainty signals in the NER stage and (ii) leverage these signals into the EL stage to improve candidate retrieval under ambiguity. These signals are used to adapt the fuzzy cutoff in candidate generation (CG) for EL. Because CG is treated as a blocking step, we evaluate retrieval recall. On the AnnoCTR dataset linked to the MITRE ATTCK knowledge base, the boundary-aware and dual-signal fuzzy linking approaches improve Recall@10 from 0.6186 to 0.7966. The contribution lies not in an end-to-end optimised ER pipeline but in establishing uncertainty preservation as a viable and beneficial component of ER pipelines, enabling downstream EL to make better-informed decisions under uncertainty.

##### **FOL-Proof: Verifying LLM Text-to-Logic Translations via Deterministic Semantics**
2611.33078v1 by Yisrael Clark, Guy Katz

Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used to translate natural-language statements into first-order logic (FOL) to improve reasoning. However, LLMs are prone to errors, and so a semantically unfaithful FOL formula could corrupt downstream reasoning. We present FOL-Proof, a semantic firewall pipeline that validates LLM-generated FOL translations against independently derived linguistic interpretations. FOL-Proof operates by deterministically obtaining a pool of candidate translations via semantic analysis tools, such as the English Resource Grammar and Minimal Recursion Semantics; then applying an SMT solver to check each derived reading for logical equivalence to the LLM-generated formula. If the translation is faithful it is accepted; otherwise, it is rejected, or optionally repaired via repeated invocations to the LLM. The procedure is conservative: incomplete search, unsupported constructions, or inconclusive checks yield abstention rather than false rejection. We evaluated FOL-Proof on established benchmarks (ProofWriter, RuleTaker and PrOntoQA), and also on a new adversarial benchmark (LongFaithCheck). Our experiments show that FOL-Proof attains near perfect selective accuracy, while direct LLM judges and round-trip baselines show substantially worse performance.

Voice activity detection (VAD) is one of the first steps in speech processing pipelines, from transcription and diarization to speech language models. 2611.33050v1 by Zihan Chen, Di Zhu, Lei Nico Zheng

##### **On Potentiality and Actuality Of Foundation Models**
2611.32991v1 by Fabian Gröger, Shuo Wen, Maria Brbić

Foundation models are built through large-scale pretraining, often followed by post-training, and are then adapted to specific tasks and behaviors. But, does the adaptation add truly new capabilities, or the base model already holds it? We decompose the update induced by different adaptations into a component shared across all inputs and a residual that varies with the input. We find that the shared component suffices for most of the adaptation gain and can be learned directly: a shift adapter that learns a constant vector per layer, identical for every input, reproduces the behavior of LoRA and full fine-tuning. We call this the potentiality principle of foundation models: the base model already contains the capacity needed for task-specific expertise. To adapt to a new task, the model just needs to be actualized, i.e., pushed in a certain direction via a constant, input-independent shift. We show the implications of this principle on parameter-efficient adaptation, steering, and convergence across models, and how it explains the neural thickets phenomenon.

2611.32982v1 by Ang Qi, Yue Wang

##### **Attestation is All You Need: Toward a Zero-Trust Architecture for HPC AI Agents**
2611.32958v1 by Jie Li, Chenxu Niu, Tongyang Wang, Yong Chen

AI agents are beginning to automate data analysis, orchestration, and simulation steering in HPC environments, but they introduce a security problem that existing cluster defenses do not address: the hijacked authorized agent. Controlled experiments show full attack-suite coverage, stronger detection than representative baselines, and no observed false positives on the evaluated benign workflows.

2611.32957v1 by Megan Squire

Online fraud is traditionally limited by the cost of the human labor needed to talk to potential victims. We ask whether hosted large language models will now supply that labor. We prompted ten off-the-shelf models to play the seller in the "free item, just pay delivery" advance-fee scam common on buy-and-sell sites, using a plain system prompt with no jailbreak. Each model played 30 conversations against each of four buyer personas, from credulous to guarded, with the same model playing the buyer (1,200 conversations). We label each conversation by what happened to the buyer's money, and validate the labels against two human coders. 737 conversations (61%) ended with the buyer paying in advance. Eight of the ten models played in almost every conversation, and running both sides cost about 2.5 cents per payment. Only the guarded buyer, who wanted a findable identity, resisted, and the most willing sellers got past it by inventing names and phone numbers. Sellers that recognized the scam still took the money in 133 conversations, usually reasoning about their own honesty rather than the buyer's risk. Several models protected the buyer without refusing, by conceding payment on delivery, so refusal rates alone misstate safety. We release the benchmark, prompts, code and labels.

##### **LoomKV: Lossless KV Cache Compression from GPU Memory to SSD for LLM Serving**
2611.32954v1 by Wentao Zhu, Ziao Sheng, Peng Deng, Huiqi Hu, Xuan Zhou, Xing Wei, Zhuang Wang

Large language model (LLM) serving engines offload the key/value (KV) cache from GPU memory to host DRAM and SSD so multi-turn and agent requests can reuse prompt prefixes. Capacity then limits service at two levels: external tiers govern prefix reuse before eviction, while the GPU KV pool bounds concurrent long requests, and lossy compression alters KV values and outputs. Using a calibrated per-head two-level 3-bit code over BF16 high bytes (sign and upper seven exponent bits), LoomKV stores BF16 KV at 0.71 of its raw size across nine models and FP8 KV at 0.85-0.87. In pool mode, FragPack packs variable-length records into smaller shared-budget GPU pages read in place by a fused decode-attention kernel faster than FlashAttention-3 on BF16. When tiers hold the working set, pool mode fits 1.31-1.34x as many GPU tokens, admitting 12 long requests with roughly raw's queue and goodput at 8. On an AI IDE trace where both tiers and the GPU pool are constrained, tier and pool modes raise goodput by 1.24x and 1.59x. Where raw capacity suffices, compression costs at most 2.2% throughput; tier mode is bit-exact with raw tiers, and pool mode matches FlashAttention-3's numerical accuracy.

##### **Do Vision-Language Models Know Their Own Visual Weaknesses? Probing Capability-Level Metacognition**
2611.32940v1 by Dohoon Kim, Taesup Moon

Metacognition includes knowing one's own strengths and weaknesses across kinds of tasks, not only judging whether a single answer is correct. We test whether vision-language models (VLMs) know in advance which kinds of visual tasks and image conditions are hard for them, across a wide range of open and proprietary models. Because some tasks are hard for every model, we separate what a model knows about this shared difficulty from what it knows about itself. We find that VLMs know little in advance about their visual weaknesses. They misjudge their overall accuracy and do not even know which kinds of tasks are hard for every model, and most misjudge how degraded images affect them. We further train VLMs to predict their own accuracy, which teaches them their own level of accuracy on kinds unseen in training but not which kinds are hard for them. However, information about these weaknesses appears once a model starts solving. On a few unlabeled questions of a kind, the probability a model assigns to its own answers follows weaknesses particular to the model, across kinds of tasks and image conditions. VLMs thus hold information about their own weaknesses that their stated predictions do not yet reflect. By separating what is particular to a model from what holds for every model, our evaluation offers a test for methods that aim to teach VLMs their own weaknesses, and the answer probability offers them a signal to start from.

World models support embodied decision-making by predicting future environment states. ##### **Residual Guidance for Real-Time Interactive Video Generation**
2611.32820v1 by NaHyeon Park, Kyeong-Hwan Moon, Youjin Jeon, Hyunjung Shim

Real-time interactive video generation allows users to change the prompt during generation to steer the evolving video. However, existing streaming models, which generate videos chunk by chunk, often keep following the previous prompt after a switch. We trace this failure to context inertia, where the previous chunk acts as a strong prior that suppresses the new prompt. Furthermore, we find that this inertia persists even when the cache retains only the previous chunk, yet discarding the context breaks visual continuity, which limits approaches that edit the cache. Rather than editing the cache, we propose Residual Guidance (RG), a simple, training-free method that counteracts context inertia with a correction measured outside the cache. Specifically, RG denoises the re-noised previous chunk once under the new prompt without the cache and adds the resulting residual to the early denoising steps of the next chunk, where its content is largely decided and the new prompt is most suppressed. Across settings ranging from a single switch to long multi-switch generation, RG substantially improves prompt switching capability on existing streaming models, including those with dedicated switching mechanisms, while largely retaining visual quality. As it requires no training or per-model tuning and adds only one forward pass per chunk, RG can be easily integrated into existing real-time video generation pipelines.

##### **Interactive Neural Computers**

Computers and their interfaces are usually treated as fixed, so a person, or increasingly an AI agent, must adapt to whatever the operating system presents. This is limiting when someone needs a different workflow, or an interface suited to hands-free use or a disability. We explore an overlooked alternative. Rather than training an agent to operate an existing computer, we train a generative model to be the computer, predicting each next screen from past screens and the user's mouse and keyboard actions. Treating a desktop as an interactive world model raises problems that natural-video world models largely avoid. Without a camera pose to index memory, the model must remember content that has left the screen without any geometric cues. Arithmetic in a terminal has to be exactly right, not merely plausible. Small interface text must also stay legible at interactive frame rates. Our Interactive Neural Computer is structured like a Neural Turing Machine and starts to address these problems with three modules trained end to end from pixels and actions. A surprise-gated slot memory, supervised by latent next-frame prediction (MEMORY-JEPA), stores what is off screen. An exploratory query bridge brings in the reasoning of a pretrained VLM, and a residual renderer predicts only the latent changes between frames. In long closed-loop rollouts, our model recalls occluded files in 64.2% of trials (chance is 33.3%), compared with at most 56.0% for recurrent and sliding-window baselines. It also raises terminal-text OCR accuracy from 1.3% to 50.1%. Finally, because the memory is explicit, it can be transplanted at test time to causally change what the model renders.

##### **From Grammar Deltas to Token Masks: Incremental Constrained Decoding under Runtime Grammar Evolution**

Because brain recordings are noisy, there exists an upper bound to the brain scores that any model can achieve. This bound, called the noise ceiling, is crucial for two reasons: it determines whether further improvements in model performance are possible, and it enables meaningful comparisons of brain scores across benchmarks. ##### **Generating Schema-Aligned Synthetic Dialogues and Measuring Their Diversity**
2611.32631v1 by Konrad Stolte, Toni Fetzer

Training or evaluating a model that turns a conversation into a structured record requires paired data, meaning a transcript together with the exact record it should be converted into. Naively prompting a language model for such dialogues yields small variations of a single conversation, so we add a sampled variation configuration, personas, and a topic plan that orders the fields of the record as explicit mechanisms for variation. We measure the variation that reaches the output through embedding space, where the embedding variance spreads over about two and a half times as many independent directions with our full configuration compared to without (participation ratio 23.3 against 9.2). ##### **REBEAL: Human References Are No Longer the Best Translation, and Metrics Do Not Notice**
2611.32623v1 by Ahrii Kim, Chanjun Park, Seong-heum Kim

Reference-based MT evaluation assumes the human reference is correct and better than the systems it scores. Both assumptions now fail, since the human reference ranks first in none of the 2024 WMT MQM campaigns and carries annotated errors in 19% to 87% of its segments. What a metric does with a flawed reference is a question about the metric, and it would have been worth asking before reference quality came into doubt. We introduce REBEAL (Reference Bias EvALuation), a benchmark that probes how MT metrics respond to seven types of controlled reference contamination, drawn from the error categories those campaigns record. Across eight widely used metrics and three language pairs, recent learned metrics (xCOMET, MetricX) prove more robust than their predecessors, but each family fails in its own way. Surface metrics show no length sensitivity, so contamination hides in longer segments, while learned metrics accept references in unrelated languages as nearly correct. The failure they share is named-entity contamination, which no metric detects and which flips up to 8 of 36 system pairs when ranking nine MT systems.

##### **When "Neutral" Means Irrelevant: A Construct-Validity Audit of NormAd-ETI**
2611.32614v1 by Aarav Singh, Animesh Pathak, Christian Hoang, Navyansh Singh, Avantika Singh

NormAd-ETI scores a model's judgment of everyday actions as acceptable, unacceptable, or neither as the cultural context shrinks from a specific rule to a country name. We audit what that three-way score measures. It combines two decisions: whether a supplied norm applies, and whether the action conforms to it. Separating them shows that when only a country is shown, applicability falls to near chance while conformity stays well above it, because the prompt withholds the rule a Neutral label depends on. Because one answer carries both decisions, the score follows the steadier conformity and hides the weaker applicability, and no single prompt recovers the two separately. Dedicated forced-choice probes separate them for frontier models but not for small open models, where the applicability judgment still tracks the hidden acceptability label. Adding a written rationale reduces that conflation without removing it. We recommend reporting the two results separately and measuring the conflation gap.

In LLM task execution, a constraint on the permitted disclosure granularity of personal attributes is sometimes imposed on the output. Detecting such violations requires capturing not only direct privacy leakage (San Francisco) but also semantic leakage (California). We further found that semantic leakage depends less on the model than on the permitted disclosure granularity and the task's need for the private attribute; reliable auditing must therefore target semantic leakage together with these two factors. 2611.32573v1 by Atefeh Hemmati Golsefidi, David Pisinger

One of the most common models for handling uncertainty is two-stage stochastic programming. Choosing the right number of scenarios is a trade-off between the uncertainty that can be represented and computational cost. A recent method, Deep Generative Scenario Search, generates synthetic scenarios and improves them by learning from the decisions they induce. The original approach used a black-box gradient estimate, since decisions are piecewise constant across scenarios. This paper provides the missing analytical foundation by deriving the gradient in closed form from recourse duality and an adjoint solve of the regularized reduced problem. The decision-focused direction, with a heuristic direction as a backup when duals are unavailable, defines the sampling subspace of a guided evolution strategy rather than being used as a direct update. For any guiding subspace, the expected update is a descent direction of the smoothed objective, so an inaccurate guide adds variance while the expected step still descends. For six problem families, both guided estimators outperform the baseline. The decision-focused version reduces the mean gap by three-quarters, and on the two families with the most integer variables the reduced sets beat the exact solver's two-hour incumbent in most runs, using a fifth of the time.

Specifically, we devise the margin retention score (MRS), which measures the first-order projected degradation of the initial answer's logit margin over competing options across conditions and assesses how changes in latent knowledge representations affect this margin. ##### **What If Chinese Were Latinized? A Counterfactual Study of Input Representation, Tokenization, and Language Modeling**
2611.32525v1 by Zijie Zheng, Ej Zhou

If Chinese had been Latinized, as the Latinxua Sin Wenz movement proposed, what would it have meant for Chinese NLP? Homophones and tone marking complicate the answer. The resulting Pinyin vocabularies not only exhibit expected homophone collisions, but also show approximately 10% higher fertility than characters. An alternative BPE configuration changes the relative fertility of the two representations. We further pretrain Llama-style models on Chinese characters and Pinyin. The Pinyin models perform worse on per-character perplexity, homophone disambiguation, and overall ZhoBLiMP minimal-pair accuracy. An exploratory phonological probe yields near-chance performance for both representations. The broader implication is that written input representations are not merely neutral encodings. Choices for representations affect language modeling and their downstream performances. Latinization never actually happened for Chinese, but other languages did, such as Hangul and Latin script replaced character in Korean and Vietnamese; the cost we measure is a guide to where modeling effort for those languages should concentrate.

Further experiments demonstrate improved pairwise execution consistency and gains on Hydra-MDP and DrivoR without additional training, supporting executable code as a complementary representation for driving world models.

##### **VISE: Verifier-Independent Skill Self-Evolution with Feedback from Tool Implementations**
2611.32480v1 by Yuxuan Liu, Chongyang Du, Yuhao Zhang, Jiahe Guo, Kawai Chung, Wenbin Hu, Huihao Jing, Hongyu Luo, Qing Zong, Haochen Shi, Haoran Li, Yangqiu Song

Agent skills provide reusable workflows that guide LLM agents in using tools. Refining these workflows requires feedback on missing or incorrect instructions, but task-specific verifiers are not always available. We propose VISE (Verifier-Independent Skill Evolution), a scripts-first approach that uses tool implementations to guide skill revision. It extracts tool capabilities, input requirements, usage conditions, and output formats from source code. Using the task request and execution trace, it compares this information with current workflow instructions to identify missing or conflicting steps. It then recommends specific edits to SKILL.md, linking each change to the relevant implementation and usage conditions. We evaluate VISE as an additional revision stage for four evolution methods on SkillsBench and SkillLearnBench. Compared with the corresponding No-verifier conditions, it improves overall average task success by 9.14-13.35 percentage points. The proportion of tasks solved in all three runs increases by 12.36-18.29 points. A case study shows how revised instructions connect tool outputs to later steps. These results support tool implementations as a practical feedback source for skill self-evolution.

摘要：

##### **Progressive Reliable Supervision for Black-Box Universal Domain Adaptation**
2611.32474v1 by Jing Li, Zhe Zhang, Huateng Sun, Yuhui Yang, Wanli Xue, Xu Cheng, Fan Shi, Jianhua Zhang, Qinghua Hu, Shengyong Chen

Black-Box Universal Domain Adaptation (B$^2$-UniDA) aims to adapt a target model to an unlabeled target domain under arbitrary label-space relationships, while neither source data nor source-model parameters are accessible during adaptation. Under such restrictive information access, reliable target supervision is unavailable, while predictions from the black-box source model can be unreliable due to source bias and domain shifts. To address this challenge, we propose Progressive Reliable Supervision (PReS), which progressively establishes and refines reliable target supervision by coordinating complementary knowledge from the black-box source model, foundation models, and the evolving target model. PReS first identifies reliable known samples by exploiting semantic consistency under diffusion-based perturbations and cross-model consensus between the black-box source model and CLIP. It then estimates the sample-wise reliability of the two predictors and adaptively integrates their complementary predictions to construct reliable pseudo labels. The resulting supervision is exploited asymmetrically to encourage confident and discriminative predictions for reliable known samples while maintaining uncertain yet locally consistent predictions for potential unknown samples. As adaptation proceeds, the emerging target-domain knowledge is further leveraged to refine both the pseudo labels and the known/unknown sample partition, forming a closed-loop process in which supervision construction and target adaptation mutually reinforce each other. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmark datasets under diverse label-space relationships demonstrate that PReS achieves state-of-the-art or highly competitive performance under the restrictive B$^2$-UniDA setting.

摘要：

##### **STAR: Counterfactual Dual-World Rollouts and Spatial Target Adaptive Reconstruction for Visual On-Policy Distillation**
2611.32472v1 by Yi Zou, Zhuang Yan, Yudong Zhang, Xu Wang, Yang Wang

Vision-language models often rely on global context and language priors while overlooking answer-determining local details. Privileged visual on-policy self-distillation supervises full-image student rollouts using a frozen teacher that views high-resolution evidence crops. Yet our cross-scale diagnostics show that models retain over half of their full-image accuracy when local evidence is completely masked, indicating that single-image correctness does not guarantee reliance on local evidence, while the need for visual correction also varies across tokens with their spatial attention states. We propose STAR, a visual on-policy self-distillation framework combining independent counterfactual dual-world rollouts with spatial-attention-guided target reconstruction. Local image editing constructs paired worlds with identical questions and backgrounds but distinct answer-determining facts, where a shared student samples trajectories independently to expose contextual shortcuts under image-consistent prefixes. Within each world, the teacher contrasts real and mean-color crops to determine the visual correction direction, while the divergence between student image attention and a teacher-centroid spatial reference adaptively controls token-wise target log-probability reconstruction. Without auxiliary attention losses or test-time tool overhead, STAR improves Qwen3.5 across scales by up to 8.04 percentage points in average accuracy over initialization across six fine-grained benchmarks, reaches OPSD's final accuracy $4.7\times$ faster, and generalizes to out-of-domain reasoning tasks.

摘要：

##### **BCP: A Lightweight Boundary-Controlled Framework for Online Sign Language Recognition and Translation**
2611.32471v1 by Xiao Ma, Lin Li, Shiqing Wu, Wang Gao, Chuanbo Liu, Jianquan Liu

Online sign language recognition (SLR) and translation (SLT) incrementally produce gloss sequences or natural language text from sign video as it arrives, a capability essential for real-time communication and live captioning. However, powerful pretrained offline models cannot be directly deployed in this setting, while existing online methods typically retrain the entire model on simulated streaming prefixes constructed from offline videos, incurring high costs. To address this challenge, we propose Boundary-Controlled Prefix Commit (BCP), a unified lightweight framework that converts pretrained offline methods into online counterparts for sign-to-gloss (S2G), sign-to-text (S2T), and sign-to-gloss-to-text (S2G2T). Specifically, BCP freezes the pretrained model and introduces a lightweight boundary adapter that decides when to commit, supervised by Connectionist Temporal Classification (CTC) alignments from a pretrained SLR model, without any human annotation. At inference, a Boundary-Controlled Wait-K Output Policy then decides what to commit via prefix voting, yielding adaptive burst commits while preserving the output progress of the base Wait-K schedule. Experiments on PHOENIX-2014T and CSL-Daily across four offline methods show that BCP, with less than 0.1% additional trainable parameters per offline method, achieves a strong quality-latency trade-off on S2T and remains competitive on S2G and S2G2T.

摘要：

##### **Gamification in the age of AI: surveying player perceptions of motivation, manipulation, and data protection**
2611.32464v1 by Kurt Horvath, Tom Tucek

Modern games and applications increasingly employ artificial intelligence (AI), user modeling, and gamification to influence user behavior, sustain engagement, and monetize attention. Although these mechanisms can enhance motivation and enjoyment, they also raise concerns about manipulation, personal data usage, and transparency. To examine how players perceive these dynamics, we conducted a survey with 28 adult participants. Respondents expressed strong concerns about the collection and exploitation of personal information in games and gamified applications, while also reporting low awareness and a limited sense of control over how their data is used. They evaluated progression features such as leveling systems and leaderboards as motivating. In contrast, they rated retention-focused mechanics such as daily login rewards and random reward reveals as highly manipulative. Participants reported that these systems can create fear of missing out or resemble gambling. Participants also expressed support for regulatory measures that limit exploitative design and protect vulnerable users. These findings reveal a clear gap between increasingly powerful engagement strategies and user trust, illustrating the need for more responsible and transparent gamification practices.

摘要：

##### **Describe, Don't Ask: Caption-Driven On-Policy Distillation for Fine-Grained Visual Perception**
2611.32463v1 by Haozhan Shen, Liangbo He, Jun Lan, Huijia Zhu, Weiqiang Wang, Tiancheng Zhao, Jianwei Yin

Vision-language models (VLMs) often recognize fine-grained details more accurately in a relevant crop than in the full image. Multimodal on-policy distillation (OPD) methods exploit this by transferring the advantage of a crop-view teacher to a full-image student. However, their supervision relies on region-specific questions curated through an elaborate synthesis pipeline, which brings three weaknesses: (1) every region needs a question--answer pair written exclusively for it, locking training into costly QA curation that is hard to scale to unlabeled images; (2) all rollouts of a region answer the same question, so the teacher supervises only the details it asks about, leaving other visual elements in the region unsupervised; and (3) every question presupposes that its target exists, so the student becomes prone to answering even when the target is absent. In this paper, we study how to turn unlabeled images into broad visual supervision through multi-aspect self-description and privileged-view distillation, and propose CaptionOPD, which replaces tailored questions with a fixed set of image-agnostic caption prompts, ranging from general descriptions to focused captions of parts, colors, counts, text, and layout. The rollouts of a region receive different prompts, so the teacher supervises various aspects of the region. Moreover, because caption prompts mask the model to describe what is visible instead of querying a specific target, they avoid presupposing what the region contains. Using the model itself as the crop-view teacher, CaptionOPD requires no questions, no answers, and no larger model annotations: only images and region boxes. Experiments show that, trained on the same images and regions, CaptionOPD outperforms prior OPD methods across multiple fine-grained and general perception benchmarks.

摘要：

##### **MAGPIE: Agentic Multi-Reference Image Generation via Executable Scene Construction**
2611.32462v1 by Yifan Dai, Daili Hua, Bohan Zeng, Yuran Wang, Jialing Liu, Bozhou Li, Chengzhuo Tong, Yang Shi, Kai Zeng, Liu Yang, Xinyu Liu, Shujie Li, Yiyan Ji, Bingyin Mei, Yushuo Guan, Yuanxing Zhang, Fangcheng Fu, Wentao Zhang

Multi-reference image generation requires a model to preserve the appearance of selected subjects while arranging them according to an instruction. Despite recent progress in reference-based generation and compositional control, reliably organizing selected subjects into the intended scene remains challenging. Although existing methods provide various forms of compositional guidance, the planned relationships are not always explicitly instantiated for inspection and refinement before image synthesis. To address this limitation, we introduce MAGPIE, which represents the intended composition as a simplified 3D scene defined by an executable Blender program. A coding agent links each scene instance to its reference, checks the geometry and rendered view against the instruction, and revises the program to correct discrepancies. The final proxy rendering serves as a visual plan for the image generator. Using MAGPIE-Instruct-3K, we train the generator to follow this plan while drawing each subject's appearance from its reference. MAGPIE improves both Qwen and FLUX on DreamOmni2 and SconeEval. With FLUX, it improves overall success on DreamOmni2 by 28.21 percentage points over the original backbone and achieves the highest overall score among the models evaluated on SconeEval.

摘要：

##### **Phoneme-identity depth profiles do not reliably predict LoRA placement for dysarthric ASR**
2611.32455v1 by Bernard Muller, LaVonne Roberts

Parameter-efficient adaptation of a speech foundation model to dysarthric speech requires a rarely justified choice: which encoder layers to adapt. A recently proposed strategy is to probe the encoder, find where phonetic information becomes most decodable, and put the adapters there. We test that inference across two ASR models (Qwen3-ASR, Whisper-small) and two dysarthric corpora (Mandarin CDSD, English UASpeech), holding recipe and seed discipline constant.
  The inference fails. Measured under one pipeline rather than inherited from published figures, all four phoneme-error curves minimise between 83% and 92% of relative depth, each model's minimising layer moving no further between corpora than between probe seeds. The best adaptation band is not similarly constant: none of three natural mappings from the curve to a band -- steepest improvement, lowest band-mean error, or knee location -- recovers the best tested configuration. We do not claim no statistic of the curve could work, only that these three do not.
  Placement still matters, but is model- and corpus-specific. On CDSD the models disagree -- Qwen3-ASR prefers its upper third (29.88% CER, three-seed mean), Whisper-small all-block LoRA (53.56%) -- while on UASpeech neither favours the upper band. At three seeds, upper-versus-all is resolved (-1.31 pp, CI [-2.16, -0.25]) but upper-versus-middle is not (-0.83 pp, CI [-2.06, +1.86]). The two models' preferences differ as a directly tested interaction (-4.71 pp, CI [-6.17, -2.89]); we designate this the primary claim, and record that the designation was made post hoc. On Qwen3-ASR x UASpeech, adapting near the input is reliably worse than not adapting at all (+9.90 pp CER). We recommend choosing the band by a short adaptation sweep on the model and corpus in hand, not by reading it off a probe.

#### Abstracts
##### **On the Creation of Object-Centric Event Logs from Patient Journeys**
2611.33074v1 by Saimir Bala, Neven Borak, Maximilian König, Anjo Seidel, Zeinab Soleimani, Lorenzo Famiglini, Lev Petrov, Jonas Witt, Mathias Weske

Interactions between patients and health systems as well as conditions that individuals experience during a disease are represented in patient journeys. Therefore, patient journeys are important assets to investigate how care is being provided, and how health systems can be designed to better suit the needs of patients. While there is initial work on patient journeys from a process mining perspective with traditional case notions, object-centric process mining techniques have yet to be covered. This is unfortunate, since due to the rich structure of objects and their relationships, object-centric process mining approaches are particularly promising in this context. This paper addresses this gap by investigating object-centric event log (OCEL) generation from patient journeys. Based on a list of challenges and requirements, the paper presents an approach for the creation of OCELs from patient journey data. The approach is evaluated on a synthetic patient cohort provided by our industry partner.

摘要：

##### **Can LLM Judges Catch Unsafe Clinical Actions? A Fault-Injection Study**
2611.33012v1 by Neda Ghafouri

As clinical AI agents begin to interact with electronic health records (EHRs), large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to evaluate their actions. But how reliably can these judges recognize clinically dangerous mistakes, especially when an agent appears to have completed its task successfully? We investigate this question using MedAgentBench by introducing controlled clinical errors into verified agent trajectories. The experiments include 416 faulty trajectories, eight error categories, and six open-weight LLM judges. By comparing each faulty trajectory with its correct counterpart, we measure whether judges genuinely recognize errors rather than simply flagging more actions as unsafe. The findings show that access to clinical evidence significantly improves judge reliability. When shown only the final answer, judges cannot distinguish errors that leave it unchanged, while flagging 56% of correct trajectories. With full trajectory information, the ability to distinguish unsafe actions from correct ones reaches a score of 0.75, and providing actual EHR responses improves discrimination by 0.39. Surprisingly, including the agent's reasoning does not improve overall performance and reduces detection of incorrectly reported clinical values by 0.18, which remain the hardest errors to detect. MedGemma performed worse than the general-purpose model and was the only judge to miss potentially dangerous medication overdoses. We also find that the benchmark's original grader accepts all 38 trajectories with an intentionally skipped laboratory check, while combining LLM judges with a rule-based checker informed by the full EHR record detects 97% of injected faults.

摘要：

##### **ASCENT: A Benchmark for Evaluating and Advancing Stepwise Diagnostic Reasoning in Large Language Models on Common Clinical Scenarios**
2611.32734v1 by Yera Choi, Yeong Hwa Kim, Jaedeok Lee, Taekang Kim, Sangdoo Yun, Seong-Eun Moon

Large language models (LLMs) excel at medical question answering yet are rarely evaluated on the stepwise diagnostic reasoning that defines real clinical workflows, where impressions are revised as information accumulates. We build Annotated Stepwise Clinical rEasoning for NaturalisTic Diagnosis (ASCENT), a clinician-annotated benchmark and training resource of 3,078 stepwise problems derived from MedQA-USMLE that decomposes each vignette into EMR-aligned steps (Findings, Impression, supporting Rationale), enabling evaluation of intermediate reasoning under incomplete information.
  Experiments and training with ASCENT revealed insights into how current LLMs handle stepwise diagnostic reasoning. Even strong reasoning models that perform well on MedQA-USMLE leave substantial headroom on ASCENT, and general-purpose frontier models trail further, exposing a persistent gap between fully informed and stepwise diagnosis. Fine-tuning Qwen2.5-7B and 32B on ASCENT yields measurable F1 gains over both pre-trained and HuatuoGPT-o1 CoT-trained baselines, with gains driven primarily by precision. Complementary robustness analyses (counterfactual perturbation, format-vs-content control, judge agreement, and rollout) further show that ASCENT-fine-tuned models rely on the diagnostic content of prior impressions rather than imitating their output format, while error propagation under rollout remains a key challenge for clinical deployment.

摘要：

##### **GeoSurge: Geometry-Grounded Multimodal Reasoning for Auditable Liver Resection Planning**
2611.32657v1 by Wenjie Deng, Zijian Tu, Xueyin Zhou, Huayi Wang, Zhuoyun Du, Mingyu Chen, Haochao Ying, Jian Wu

Surgical resection planning centers on generating executable resection surfaces from anatomical evidence and operative constraints. Medical multimodal foundation models enable anatomical understanding, and segmentation models predict resection regions, but neither translates these capabilities into explicitly constrained, executable resection surfaces. We present GeoSurge, an anatomy-grounded framework that combines mask-derived metrics with structure-aware point-cloud tokens to generate executable, editable resection surfaces. To make resection geometry learnable and editable, GeoSurge maps actions to parametric geometry through a surface-action grammar. Using this grammar, anatomy-oriented and tumor-centered templates generate candidates, which are filtered by hard constraints and semantic deduplication. Training supervision is then derived from candidate-expert geometric agreement and independently recomputed feasibility metrics. At inference, the reasoner uses patient evidence and surgical requirements to select or revise an action, which an independent geometry engine subsequently materializes and verifies. GeoSurge achieves the best overall scores on DeepTumour Liver and CRLM-QA, improving the constraint satisfaction rate from 73% to 100% on CRLM and from 56% to 99% on GeoSurge-Bench.

摘要：

##### **Can AI Design a Better nnU-Net? A Hypothesis-Driven Architecture Discovery Loop from Failures to Design Rules**
2611.32384v1 by Songtao Jiang, Yuan Wang, Fucheng Niu, Hualiang Wang, Jian Wu, Zuozhu Liu

Neural architecture search and LLM-driven evolution often reduce evaluation to a scalar fitness score, discarding failures that could explain why designs succeed or fail. We present a hypothesis-driven architecture discovery loop in which LLM agents diagnose structured error patterns, formulate falsifiable hypotheses, test counterfactual controls under a frozen nnU-Net pipeline, and distill validated mechanisms into executable trigger-to-action rules. Across eight search waves, the loop identifies two principles for 3D medical image segmentation. First, reallocating computation from high-resolution decoders to the mid-encoder preserves thin-anatomy boundaries while reducing cost. Second, support-confined region contrast addresses errors between nested anatomical compartments, with anatomy-dependent triggers selecting when the rule applies. These principles produce LeanNeXt (0.78 MACs and 0.92 training-step time relative to nnU-Net; +1.63 Dice points on four-fold KiTS23 cross-validation) and trigger-routed LeanNeXt+. The latter improves all 15 evaluated CT/MRI settings over nnU-Net and exceeds the 3.3 larger ResEnc-M on 12 of 15 benchmarks at 0.30 its parameter count. Relative to random sampling, one-shot LLM generation, and score-selected agent search, the loop shows how failure analysis can yield reusable design rules across modalities.

摘要：

##### **DiCT: Teaching X-ray Vision-Language Models to Reason via Imagining CT**
2611.32344v1 by Zhenghao Lu, Xiaotian Zhang, Songtao Jiang, Yiqun Lin, Siming Fu, Hualiang Wang, Zuozhu Liu

Diagnosing with X-rays often requires reasoning about the 3D locations of abnormalities and their spatial relationships to surrounding anatomical structures, yet such reasoning remains challenging due to the loss of spatial information in 2D projections. To address this challenge, we propose Dreaming in CT (DiCT), a three-stage progressive CT-to-X-ray reasoning transfer framework that leverages a CT vision-language model (VLM) to guide an X-ray VLM. Through this CT-guided transfer, the student acquires CT-informed 3D reasoning capabilities, enabling it to reason directly from 2D X-rays without explicit CT reconstruction at inference time. The framework begins with instruct the dreamer, which equips the X-ray student with initial CT-oriented reasoning capabilities and establishes a structured interface that distinguishes X-ray observations from CT imagination. Building on this structure, visualize the dream introduces CT-guided on-policy distillation (OPD), where the CT teacher selectively guides the X-ray student's reasoning trajectories to transfer 3D anatomical knowledge. Finally, manifest the dream adapts the acquired reasoning capabilities from digitally reconstructed radiographs (DRRs) to real clinical X-rays. Experiments across two VLM backbones on 3D-RAD and ReXVQA demonstrate DiCT's effectiveness across radiological reasoning tasks, achieving a 1.66 percentage point improvement over a CT reconstruction-based baseline on 3D-RAD Abnormality Identification with 12.77$\times$ lower inference computation. Our code is available at https://github.com/HongYep/DiCT.

摘要：

##### **Refining and Aligning Clinical Reasoning with Health Guidelines via Formal Constraints**
2611.32247v1 by Xiaoyang Fan, Yufan Cai, Zhe Hou, Jin Song Dong

Clinical reasoning from incomplete or conflicting patient evidence must account for low-probability but high-risk concerns while remaining grounded in health guidelines. We introduce VerClin, an explainable framework that extracts guideline conditions, exclusions, recommendations, and priorities into executable knowledge and represents patient conversations as scoped cases. A deterministic four-valued reasoner produces traceable guideline matches while preserving uncertainty and enforcing exclusions. Multiple specialty agents then screen matched guidelines for case applicability, and a consultation model synthesizes accepted guidance through specialty recommendations and a sentence-preserving formal-constraint channel. We present accepted guidance in ascending order of the sum of its priority and trigger-strength ranks, which can surface high-risk guidance with weaker triggers. VerClin outperforms direct generation and Top-5 RAG on HealthBench Hard and HealthBench Professional. On the LLMEval-Med subset, it outperforms direct generation and ties Top-5 RAG. Component analyses further examine semantic closure, example-guided refinement, specialty screening, and constraint-conditioned delivery.

摘要：

##### **Blaming the Machine, Billing the Victim? How Mock Jurors Reassign Fault from Generative AI Systems and Respond to Liability Cliffs in Tort**
2611.32188v1 by Tin Trung Nguyen, Jiannan Xu, Bao Kham Chau, Hal Daumé, Zubin Jelveh

Generative AI complicates tort liability attribution when people blame a generative AI system that cannot itself bear legally relevant fault. We investigate where this blame goes when fault must be allocated among legal persons, and whether American discontinuous apportionment doctrines influence mock jurors' factual findings of fault. Such doctrines include Contributory negligence, Joint and several liability, and their 50% or 51% variations, all of which have a fault-percentage threshold or a "cliff" where the victim-entitled compensation (or a defendant's liability) jumps to zero (or to all compensatory damages). Our pre-registered human subjects experiment (N=199 Prolific participants) shows that as many U.S. mock jurors (36%) blame the AI system itself, the AI blame often "leaks" to the victim or to the AI user (in 27% to 73% of our AI-blaming records, depending on how leakage is calculated). Although mock jurors rarely change their blame assignment from one side of the liability cliff to the other, these participants do not acknowledge the legal doctrine or its monetary consequence when explaining their changes. Our empirical results illustrate how a narrow doctrinal exception in tort, carving out "joint and several liability" for those deploying or using Generative AI without reasonable care, might mitigate the impact of blame leakage on victims' deserved compensation without introducing significant jury nullification risks.

摘要：

##### **HuluOPD: Unifying Heterogeneous Medical Specialists into One Lightweight Model with Unlabeled Prompts**
2611.32021v1 by Yuncheng Zhang, Songtao Jiang, Yitian Tang, Zijie Meng, Zihan Xu, Xuxiang Ren, Jian Wu, Hualiang Wang, Zuozhu Liu

Specialist medical vision-language models (VLMs) are strong within their specialties but hard to unify: their training data are often private, and independently developed specialists cannot be assumed to share an architecture. Deploying these large models separately is costly. We present HuluOPD, an on-policy distillation recipe that unifies heterogeneous specialists into one lightweight VLM using only unlabeled prompts, without requiring reference answers or the specialists' training data. The lightweight model answers each prompt, and the specialist of the prompt's modality supervises its response; a continuation-aligned cross-tokenizer loss makes this possible even when they differ in architecture and tokenizer. We apply HuluOPD to five specialists, for ultrasound, pathology, optical coherence tomography, fundus photography, and dental imaging. To distill and evaluate them, we build HuluOPDCorpus, 914k unlabeled prompts, and HuluOPDBench, 47k held-out test items, from over 90 public datasets, and score all models under one protocol on 132k test items, including third-party benchmarks. A single model with 10% of the five specialists' combined parameters retains 94% of their question-answering performance, and one with 2% retains 89%. To our knowledge, this is the first demonstration of unifying heterogeneous medical specialist VLMs without requiring their training data or a shared architecture.

摘要：

##### **Modeling Event-Conditioned Emotional Expression Response with LLM Agents in a Synthetic Social Simulation**
2611.31851v1 by Rebecca Marigliano, Peter Carragher, Kathleen M. Carley

Emotions are central drivers of human behavior, yet most behavioral models fail to capture their role in shaping perception, decision-making, and collective action. Application areas such as emergency response and public health are especially constrained by the lack of emotion-aware modeling. While large language models (LLMs) are increasingly integrated into agent-based models (ABMs), little work has examined how to endow LLM agents with emotional reasoning or validated their ability to reproduce empirically observed emotional patterns. This study addresses this gap by introducing a hierarchical prompting framework that conditions LLM agents with demographic, psychological, and contextual cues to generate event-conditioned emotional responses to real-world events. We validate the resulting event-conditioned emotional responses against large-scale social media reaction data derived from the Social Science One Facebook URL Shares Dataset, complemented by demographic and personality benchmarks. Our simulation results reproduce both polarized and consensus-driven emotional patterns with significant fidelity, supporting the application of LLM agents for emotion-aware modeling in behavioral simulations. We also find, however, that systematic gaps in emotional expression of LLM agents and human populations exist, such as the underexpression of anger relative to human baselines. As such, these findings highlight the need for continued calibration if LLM agents are to serve as proxies for human populations in behavioral modeling. By quantifying their divergence from human populations in terms of emotional expression, we hope that our methods can aid in this effort.

摘要：

##### **JECT: Addressing the Context Shortcut in Joint-Embedding Predictive Learning for Chest CT**
2611.31849v1 by Hanbin Ko, Hong-Jun Choi, Jihun Kim, Young-Gon Kim, Dongheon Lee, Chang Min Park

Joint-embedding predictive architectures learn by predicting features of masked regions from visible context. In chest computed tomography, the findings that decide a diagnosis, such as a small nodule, are often not predictable from the surrounding anatomy. A hidden finding is then best predicted toward normal anatomy, and the prediction loss stops rewarding the encoder for representing it. We call this the context shortcut. Conditioning the predictor on the radiology report repairs the prediction, but in our setup the encoder gains little over image-only pretraining: the report supplies what the encoder never had to learn. JECT therefore gives the report two roles: a condition for the predictor and, through masked section-text prediction, a target for the encoder, which must predict the report's anatomical sections from the unmasked scan. A hierarchical three-dimensional encoder that predicts at every stage keeps this clinical content at every scale. In ablations, the text target improves classification and localization, while conditioning adds little to the encoder. Trained from scratch on 173k volumes, the recipe reaches the level of current computed tomography foundation models in classification, retrieval and nodule malignancy, and of task-specific models in lung-cancer risk prediction after fine-tuning. Conditioning still matters: its predictor can be reused, with no extra training, to show where a report sentence acts, changing most at the finding the sentence describes. Code and pretrained weights are available.

摘要：

##### **Rapid Exploratory Maps and their potential role in supporting evidence-to-policy dialogues and processes**
2611.31693v1 by Gareth J. Hollands, Ian Shemilt, James Thomas

Producing useful evidence syntheses requires clearly formulated research questions linked to an understanding of their optimal scope. When policymakers (or other end users) commission or steer the production of systematic reviews or other evidence syntheses to fulfil a demand for evidence, these parameters are rarely specified sufficiently for production to proceed. Instead, these details are typically developed via a collaborative dialogic process involving evidence producers and users to ensure the resulting synthesis will meet users' needs. This process is informed by scoping and (more-or-less formally) mapping the existing evidence base, which can take many weeks or months, slowed by applying mostly manual processes to identify and characterise the evidence. Recent developments in artificial intelligence (AI) technologies have opened up new opportunities for more rapid, iterative, and responsive mapping of relevant evidence to support such early-stage evidence-to-policy dialogues and processes. In this article, we outline and characterise this emerging approach, termed Rapid Exploratory Maps (REMs). We also present two illustrative case studies drawn from first-hand experiences within a programme of evidence reviews production to inform government policy in England. While this approach could be transformative for the mapping process, its broader potential is as yet largely unassessed, and it confers important challenges and likely risks, as well as benefits. Determining whether REMs could usefully support more efficient ways of working with end users when developing and designing evidence syntheses will require continued development and careful evaluation.

摘要：

##### **Right Destination, Wrong Journey: Synthetic Counseling Dialogues Miss Human Interactional Dynamics**
2611.31686v1 by Ziwei Gong, Yuchen Huang, Nicholas Deas, Melanie Subbiah, Julia Hirschberg

LLM-generated counseling dialogues are increasingly used for training and evaluating mental-health systems, yet existing evaluations focus largely on response quality and final outcomes rather than how support unfolds over time. We conduct a trajectory-centered evaluation of process fidelity across ten human, reconstructed, and synthetic counseling datasets. Our results show that synthetic dialogues often reach plausible endpoints but differ from human support in recovery dynamics and turn-by-turn responsiveness. While generation design can shift global properties such as recovery timing and trajectory structure, contingent patient--counselor responsiveness remains consistently lower in fidelity across models and generation paradigms. Our experiments on controlled synthetic datasets show that targeted generation interventions can narrow the gap with real data on several trajectory-level measures, but do not fully recover interactional fidelity, with turn-level responsiveness remaining limited. We release three controlled synthetic datasets comprising 2,050 dialogues, together with annotations and the evaluation framework.

##### **CROSS-Calib: Selective Channel Calibration for Ultrasound Reconstruction**
2611.31684v1 by Tongxiao Hu, Junyu Zhou

Ultrasound imaging provides views of internal tissue in real time for medical diagnosis. However, ultrasound signals may be affected by interference, and correcting errors and removing transient interference while preserving valid echoes remains challenging. We propose CROSS-Calib, a channel recovery method that uses predictions from other channels as references for amplitude and phase calibration, while a gate using context selectively combines calibrated measurements with predicted echoes to retain usable samples and repair unreliable ones. On the in vivo test set, CROSS-Calib outperforms all baseline models under gain errors and transient interference, while phantom tests show that the model restores cyst contrast with echo widths of wire targets close to the clean reference. CROSS-Calib can perform channel calibration and selective repair before beamforming to improve echo usability and image quality.

##### **Genome-to-Image Translation: Reconstructing Escherichia coli Morphology from Genomic Sequences**
2611.31132v1 by Son T. Huynh, Thuy T. Le, Thu H. A. Huynh, Binh T. Nguyen

Antimicrobial resistance in Escherichia coli is established in routine practice by culture-based susceptibility testing, which is slow relative to the clinical need for an early treatment decision. Resistance status also leaves a measurable trace in cell morphology, and whole-genome sequencing of clinical isolates is increasingly available, which raises the question of whether colony appearance can be predicted directly from an isolate's genome. We formulate this as a conditional image-generation problem. The assembled contigs of each isolate are encoded into a single 768-dimensional vector by a frozen genomic language model through a segment-contig-isolate aggregation hierarchy, and a conditional Wasserstein GAN with gradient penalty synthesizes colony images from this embedding together with the applied antibiotic and the resistance label. We train on 167,356 single-cell images from 22 clinical blood isolates imaged under azithromycin, ciprofloxacin, tetracycline, and a no-drug control, compare seven pretrained convolutional backbones inside the generator, and select ConvNeXt-Tiny on distributional image-quality metrics. The synthetic images are then assessed extrinsically: added to a reduced real training set, they improve per-antibiotic resistance classification, with a significant gain in resistant-class recall.

##### **Leakage-Safe Auditing of Attribution-Driven Data Curation in Medical Imaging**
2611.31096v1 by Malay Phadke, Akshay Gokhale

When can an attribution-derived anomaly justify changing a medical-imaging training set? We formulate attribution-driven curation as three separately testable claims: anomaly detection, harmfulness estimation, and intervention utility. We test the first and third using a patient-disjoint, identifier-preserving audit of a 3D convolutional classifier for computed tomography angiography aneurysm candidates. We identify 232 exactly zero-valued arrays among 1,201 positive patches, including 170 training positives, and invalidate a historical 321-patch attention-failure subgroup whose identifier-free records were joined to files by position. On the held-out test set, GradCAM++ spatial concentration is central for every candidate class, reversing the historical pattern. GradCAM++ and Integrated Gradients show negative rank-order agreement for positives, limiting method-independent anatomical interpretation. Across five paired training seeds, removing the 170 corrupted training positives yields AUC 0.9617 +/- 0.0044 versus 0.9652 +/- 0.0039 for full-data training (difference -0.0035; 95% CI -0.0082 to 0.0007). Excluding 34 raw-zero test positives widens rather than closes the gap, countering a blank-memorization explanation. Budget-matched random-removal controls suggest the result is not simply an effect of reducing training size. Input corruption does not necessarily imply that deletion improves generalization. Our framework makes leakage control, sample identity, and intervention testing explicit in attribution-driven medical-imaging data curation.

##### **A Comparison of Uncertainty in AI and Dynamical Climate Ensembles**
2611.31064v1 by Jacob B. Landsberg, Devin M. McAfee, M. A. Fernandez, Elizabeth A. Barnes

Climate model ensembles and their uncertainty estimates are essential for evaluating risk and designing climate policy. Yet, in the assessment of newly developed AI climate models, evaluating uncertainty is often secondary to diagnosing mean-state climate biases and trends. In this work, we conduct the first systematic comparison of uncertainty representation in ensembles of state-of-the-art AI and dynamical atmospheric climate models. Examining 2-meter temperature, 500 hPa geopotential height, and precipitable water, we find that the AI ensemble exhibits reduced internal variability and model uncertainty across variables relative to the dynamical ensemble. We show that this dampened variability causes the AI ensemble to produce more limited climate extremes and yields underdispersion relative to ERA5. Under a uniform warming scenario, the AI ensemble shows increased model uncertainty. Dynamical models grapple with their own challenges, exhibiting stronger miscalibration (overdispersion) relative to ERA5 and larger climate biases than the AI models. As AI and dynamical climate models are mutually integrated into operational climate prediction, careful consideration of their differences in uncertainty representations is crucial for grounded assessment of climate risk.

##### **Automatic Occupational Coding through Retrieval-Augmented Generation**
2611.31051v1 by Mathijs A. Langezaal, Egon L. van den Broek, Lukas P. A. Arts, Marcel Goldberg, Grégoire Rey, Melissa C. Friesen, Sarah J. Locke, Nathaniel Rothman, Qing Lan, Roel C. H. Vermeulen, Susan Peters

Annually 1.9 million deaths and 89.7 million disability-adjusted life years are caused by exposure to agents during work (e.g., welding fumes, silica dust, and noise pollution). To improve worker health, high-quality exposure assessments are crucial. Therefore, free-text job descriptions from large-scale occupational cohorts are manually standardized through occupational classifications and linked to hazardous exposures using Job Exposure Matrices (JEM). However, manual coding is slow, unreliable (i.e., 44-89% inter-coder reliability), and costly, due to i) short, ambiguous free-text job descriptions, ii) classifications containing hundreds to thousands of categories, and iii) the absence of gold standards. With 46%-79% accuracy, state-of-the-art automatic occupational coding tools achieve classification performance on human level. However, their performance drops to 17-26% on out-of-distribution datasets. Furthermore, their supervised architectures limit them to occupational classifications present in the training set. Consequently, JEM using other classifications are incompatible, leaving vital exposure-response associations unexplored. Our Retrieval-Augmented Generation framework, OPERAG, enables classification into any occupational classification without expert-labelled databases. It retrieves coding index information and combines this with Large Language Model-based classification. Validated on AsiaLymph's 36,062 real-world job descriptions, OPERAG achieved 42.6% occupational classification accuracy on the 4th coding level, substantially outperforming existing tools and approaching human coders' 51-56% inter-coder reliability. Using two JEM named ALOHA+ and SHOCK, 89.9-98.6% accuracy was achieved on exposure assessment. OPERAG approaches human-level, universal occupational classification, enabling efficient and effective large-scale exposure assessment to improve worker health.

##### **Triage Accuracy of Jev and Open-Weight System One Decision Models and Their Use for Early Emergency Recognition in Symptom-Assessment Applications**
2611.31012v1 by Marvin Kopka, Austen El-Osta, Christoph Kollwitz, Felix Balzer, Florian Bontrup

Laypeople increasingly use large language models (LLMs) for triage advice, but LLMs answer in variable free text, which makes it hard to evaluate them for use in medical devices. Decision models, which assign a probability to each predefined triage category, may be easier to evaluate. To test their triage accuracy, we evaluated 16 decision models (14 open-weight, 2 proprietary) on 233 synthetic and 10,000 real-world cases, yielding 163,728 total assessments. The best models triaged 72.1% of synthetic cases correctly and agreed with an existing algorithm in 56.1% of real-world cases. They were more promising for recognising emergencies early: given only demographics, chief complaints, risk factors and red-flag answers, the best model reached an AUROC of 0.87. At a 5% error margin, it could decide for or against emergencies for about half of users. Decision models should therefore not replace triage engines but could shorten symptom assessments at setting-specific error margins.

##### **The AMPEL Platform: An Open-Source Project for Developing Hospital-Wide Clinical Decision Support Systems under EU-Regulation**
2611.30990v1 by Alexander Twrdik, Daniel Steinbach, Martin Federbusch, Clemens Malkawi, Maria Schmidt, Toralf Kirsten

As incentives to digitize more and more aspects of medical healthcare are gaining traction, additional medical data is becoming available. A major goal is to use this data to gain further insights, support medical staff, and ultimately improve patient care. This demands sophisticated solutions that can handle current and future vast amounts of data, all without disrupting well-established workflows of care takers. Additionally, the solution must fulfill the documentation and risk-management obligations of the Medical Device Regulation (MDR), which governs whether a device may be used in patient care in Europe. In this context, we introduce the AMPEL platform, an open-source solution for creating clinical decision support systems (CDSS) that fulfil the requirements of the MDR. Centered around a message broker (Kafka), it adopts an event-driven, distributed architecture, allowing for scaling and efficient processing of medical data as it gets generated. Relying on platform-specific events to implicitly describe the platform's interfaces makes it both extensible and agnostic to the hospital information system in use. With these events being defined in the Protocol Buffers format, medically evaluated algorithms can be independently implemented in a variety of programming languages and easily linked to the platform, extending its analytic capabilities as desired. The platform features a basic UI and alert functionality that can be used to gain medical staff's attention and present analysis results in a traceable and explainable fashion. We further show in a use case how the platform was used to build LAMPE, a CDSS developed and operated at University of Leipzig Medical Center. It demonstrates how the platform solution can be used in a complex and already established environment, while acknowledging current limitations and outlining planned developments to overcome these.
