##### **MedUIGym: Evaluating and Training GUI Agents for Clinical Workflows**
2611.30983v1 by Yitong Qiao, Lei Liu, Yue Shen, Jian Wang, Jinjie Gu, Zhixuan Chu

Automating clinical workflows requires vision-language agents that can navigate dense graphical user interfaces (GUIs) in electronic health records (EHRs) and execute precise actions, yet many medical agents still rely on text or structured tool calls. In this work, we introduce MedUIGym, a scalable interactive environment comprising 15,739 tasks from 17 benchmark sources across six clinical task families for training and evaluating clinical GUI agents. In MedUIGym, agents perceive EHR states from screenshots and execute mouse-and-keyboard actions, as well as a paired native-tool interface over identical clinical data. The environment verifies both final answers and required clinical actions, and supports parallel trajectory collection for training. Benchmarking twelve vision-language models shows that current agents struggle across diverse clinical workflows, and simply increasing reasoning effort fails to yield consistent gains. Furthermore, MedUIGym supports offline learning from GUI trajectories and online reinforcement learning from task rewards. After training, both Qwen3.5 models perform better on in-domain clinical tasks and generalize to tasks from external sources. By unifying tasks from existing benchmarks, dual-interface evaluation, and GUI trajectory collection, MedUIGym provides a testbed for studying how vision-language agents complete clinical tasks through the EHR's graphical interface. Our project page is available at https://github.com/yitong-qiao/MedUIGym.

##### **TrialAgentBench: Evaluating AI Agents for Clinical-Trial Analysis and Long-Horizon Drug-Development Decisions**
2611.30916v1 by Bradley Segal, William Bolton, Philip Torr, David Clifton, Lei Clifton

Clinical drug development is a long-horizon, high-stakes process requiring sequential decisions based on information that costs money and time to acquire. AI agents have demonstrated capabilities for scientific analysis, but there is no established way to evaluate them on these decisions, as real trials are expensive, involve people, and never reveal the counterfactual. We introduce TrialAgentBench, a benchmark for evaluating AI agents on clinical trial analysis and drug development decision-making using simulated trials where the truth is known, embedded within the data records of a fictional drug sponsor. We publicly release nearly 3,500 tasks, varying from analysing a single trial to managing multi-phase programmes with budget and time constraints. We score each analysis by rerunning its code on new trials, and each programme by the value its decisions achieve. Across 24 closed- and open-weight models, the strongest agents can analyse raw, messy trial records to within 6% of a process-aware analysis, even when an assumption is violated, with 95% confidence intervals containing the true effect 94--95% of the time. Over the prior year, the best model improved from 19% above this analysis, with serious misses on 15% of tasks, to 3.5% and 1%. What limits them is decision-making from evidence under uncertainty: when to correct, what evidence to buy, when to stop. The strongest agents are overly careful, correcting on 83% of tasks with equal recording errors, where it is unnecessary. On drug programmes, the best agent has substantial headroom, reaching 48% of the value an oracle achieves against 58% for a rule-based developer, with others falling 24 to 66% short. Model generations show ongoing improvements in handling data and evidence, with substantial variation. The strongest models can act as competent analysts, but do not yet make reliable long-horizon decisions.

##### **Trustworthy Speech-Based Autism Screening under Class Imbalance with Uncertainty-Aware HuBERT Ensembles**
2611.30390v1 by Nihel Loubna Ghennani, Mourtada Benazzouz, Julie Mauclair

Early screening of Autism Spectrum Disorder (ASD) is critical for timely clinical intervention, although establishing reliable speech based screening systems remains problematic due to limited clinical data, class imbalance, and the need for reliable uncertainty estimation. We propose an uncertainty-aware ASD screening framework based on self-supervised HuBERT representations, combining Leave-One-Subject-Out (LOSO) crossvalidation, balanced k-bagging, and Monte Carlo (MC) Dropout. Multiple HuBERT models are fine-tuned on balanced subsets, with predictions combined through probability-based ensemble averaging and aggregated at the subject level. Experiments on the Child Pathological Speech Database (CPSD) show strong performance, achieving 86.30% balanced accuracy and 93.74% ROC-AUC at the utterance level, and 95.04% and 96.30%, respectively, at the subject level. Furthermore, predictive entropy effectively reflects classification reliability: incorrectly classified segments show higher entropy than correctly classified ones (0.4605 $\pm$ 0.2314 vs. 0.1717 $\pm$ 0.1830), with a significant correlation with errors (r = 0.3981, p = 7.38 x 10 -170 ) and an error-detection ROC-AUC of 0.8359. These results demonstrate the potential of the proposed framework as a robust and uncertainty-aware computer-aided tool for ASD screening.

##### **A statistical measure of trust in generative AI**
2611.30286v1 by Alina Ermilova, Valeria Yakupova, Nikita Sushko, Andrey Lovyagin, George Ovchinnikov, Maksim G. Sharaev, Evgeny M. Mirkes, Denis Turdakov, Ivan Y. Tyukin

Trustworthiness is a crucial issue for generative AI, as modern models can produce convincing and confident responses even when their outputs are incomplete, unsupported, or wrong. The frequent absence of ground-truth knowledge or labels complicates verification of generative AI outputs. To address this issue, we propose a trustworthiness framework grounded in model-internal confidence calibrated against an external expert reference. We estimate per-token trustworthiness of a student generative model as the statistical typicality of each generated token relative to an expert model's distribution, where the expert is used as a distributional reference rather than as a source of factual verification labels. To this end, we introduce an AI ontology calibrator that leverages the student model's internal latent features and token-level output statistics to compute expert-reference acceptance scores. Combined with traditional token-level confidence, entropy, and perplexity statistics, calibrator scores yield a compact set of $15$ response-level indicators for correctness classification. Evaluated across BF program generation, mathematical problem solving, and medical question answering, combined calibrator and traditional indicators provide complementary reliability signal which could be used to build detectors of untrustworthy outputs and hallucinations. Remarkably, our experiments show that these detectors are transferable across domains, although transferability is not always symmetric.

##### **Differential Proteomics to Symbolic Rules: An Audit of SHAP Guidance in Lung Adenocarcinoma**
2611.29989v2 by Surajit Das

We tested whether SHAP improves protein-based Horn rules for distinguishing lung adenocarcinoma from adjacent-normal tissue. CPTAC supplied 213 samples (111 tumour, 102 adjacent-normal) from 111 patients, including 102 matched pairs. Per-protein effects and tests used the same complete pairs with Benjamini-Hochberg correction. Sparse logistic regression used five stratified patient-grouped outer folds, with preprocessing and tuning confined to training data and feature selection repeated within inner folds. A separate five-fold patient-grouped evaluation of inductive logic programming used training-derived evidence and discretisation.
  Of 9,518 proteins passing quality control, 378 differed in abundance (40 up, 338 down) at FDR below 0.05 and an absolute mean paired log-ratio difference of at least 1. Classifier ROC-AUC, average precision, accuracy and balanced accuracy were 0.9997, 0.9998, 0.9906 and 0.9906, respectively. On the original held-out rule splits, accuracy and F1 were 0.9765 and 0.9774, respectively. One clause appeared in two folds and the others appeared once.
  On common folds across all 111 patients, F1 was 0.9600 with SHAP, 0.9910 without SHAP and 0.9909 for L1 logistic regression. The inclusive SHAP cutoff retained all 100 ranked proteins per audited fold. Absolute logistic-regression coefficients reproduced the SHAP-guided candidate sets, clauses and held-out predictions in both the all-patient and paired-only analyses. A single-protein threshold reproduced full-rule predictions in the paired-only cohort.
  SHAP guidance did not improve held-out F1 on the common folds. Diagnostic utility, early detection, prognosis and generalisability beyond this cohort remain unestablished. Biomarker and clinical claims require independent validation, rule-stability testing under repeated resampling and further biological annotation.

##### **Separating Genetic and Protein Evidence in Open Targets: An Auditable Re-ranking Study**
2611.29978v1 by Ye Ye

Biomedical target-ranking platforms combine genetics, literature, experimental evidence, tractability, and clinical precedent because each is useful for target discovery. A single integrated score, however, can be awkward to reuse for a narrower question such as which genes have direct evidence for disease risk or etiology. We examine this issue in Open Targets associations for Alzheimer disease (AD) and Parkinson disease (PD). Genetics-linked evidence and protein-oriented evidence are scored in separate channels and combined only at the end. In AD, the Open Targets overall top 20 contains 7 curated genetics-supported genes and 10 symptomatic pharmacology controls; the default late fusion contains 15 curated genes and no symptomatic controls. In PD, late fusion retains 13 curated genes and removes the 5 symptomatic controls present in the platform top 20. A genetics-only ranking reaches similar curated recall (14 genes in AD, 13 in PD), so most of the gain over the platform score comes from emphasizing genetic evidence; the protein channel mainly raises the number of tractable targets in the shortlist (from 9 to 12 in AD and from 8 to 10 in PD) without readmitting symptomatic controls. These results are stable across fusion weights from 0.35 to 0.75, perturbed protein-channel weights, and rule thresholds. An optional post-ranking rule further lowers high-clinical/low-genetic targets such as ACHE and GRIN1 without changing top-20 curated recall. The procedure is deterministic and uses public Open Targets fields. Because the component scores remain available, individual rank changes can be inspected and the ranking can be reproduced from the same data snapshot.

##### **CARE-3D: Reinforcement Learning for Evidence Grounded Agentic Reasoning in Situated 3D Scenes**
2611.29969v1 by Xiaoqian Hu, Ruihong Qiu, Yanran Tang, Sen Wang

Situated 3D reasoning requires embodied agents to interpret objects, scene geometry, and viewpoint dependent spatial relations. Existing methods learn grounded interactions from supervised traces, but largely imitate prescribed evidence acquisition patterns rather than optimizing the support that the returned evidence provides for the correct answer. Moreover, outcome rewards provide limited credit for intermediate tool choices and may leave infrequent tools underexplored. An effective agent should instead adaptively decide what to ground, which tool to invoke, and how to assess the utility of returned evidence. To this end, this paper presents CARE-3D, a reinforcement post-training framework for adaptive grounded reasoning. Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on grounding instructions first establishes valid interactions, after which Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) optimizes the agent while keeping the 3D grounding module frozen. CARE-3D combines Counterfactual Attribution of Returned Evidence for evidence sensitive credit assignment, and Group Coverage Sampling to preserve tool coverage. Experiments on MSQA and Beacon3D show consistent gains over SFT and GRPO using only answer and format rewards, especially in spatial reasoning and grounding answer coherence. Our code is available at \href{https://github.com/xiaoqian-UQ/CARE-3D}{CARE-3D}.

##### **Deep Discriminative Feature Fusion of Convolutional and Attention-Based Recurrent Representations for Subject-Independent ECG Stress Recognition**
2611.29870v1 by Zeeshan Ahmad, Muhammad Rehman Zafar

Stress has become a significant concern because of its negative impact on both physical and mental health and therefore drives the need for reliable automated systems to assess stress levels. Electrocardiogram (ECG) signals provide a non-invasive and objective measure of physiological stress responses and have emerged as a promising modality for continuous stress monitoring. While deep learning techniques have achieved encouraging results in ECG-based stress detection, many studies focus only on one type of feature representation, and therefore limit their ability to capture both the morphological patterns and temporal dynamics embedded in ECG signals. To address this limitation, in this paper, we propose three deep learning-based feature fusion frameworks for ECG-based stress classification. These are Residual Feature Fusion (RFF), Bidirectional Feature-Wise Cross-Attention Fusion (BFCAF), and a novel Cross-Attention Residual Fusion (CARF) framework that combines the strengths of residual learning and bidirectional cross-attention within a unified architecture. A 1D Convolutional Neural Network (1D-CNN) and a BiLSTM-Attention network are used to extract complementary morphological and temporal representations from ECG signals, respectively. The proposed fusion frameworks leverage these heterogeneous representations to improve feature interaction, information preservation, and stress discrimination. The proposed frameworks are evaluated using subject-independent Leave-One-Subject-Out Cross-Validation (LOSOCV) on the RML and WESAD datasets for three-class stress classification. Experimental results show that the proposed feature fusion methods consistently improve performance over individual CNN and BiLSTM models, with CARF achieving the highest accuracies of 83.1% and 77.91% on the RML and WESAD datasets, respectively.

##### **Semantic repeatability and reproducibility of GPT-5 and Gemini 3 Flash on H5N1 One Health questions**
2611.29859v1 by Luizza Dalla Pria, Amelia l. Nelson, Renata Ivanek

Highly pathogenic avian influenza A(H5N1) has re-emerged as a One Health concern since 2024, and the public is increasingly consulting large-language-model (LLM) chatbots for health information during outbreaks. Before a chatbot's accuracy can be meaningfully assessed, its consistency must first be understood, so that any single response can be trusted as a fair representative of the model's behavior. ##### **MobileGrail: Learning Compact Guardrails for On-Device deployable Mobile GUI Agent Safety**
2611.29847v1 by Zhiyu Zhang, Yuankai Li, Muhao Chen

Runtime guardrails protect mobile GUI agents by assessing each proposed action before execution, but existing guardrails rely on cloud hosted large multimodal models, exposing every screen to a remote server, including private information such as payments, messages, and medical records. We propose MobileGrail, an on-device deployable model that addresses both. It maintains a recurrent task state that retains only the evidence subsequent safety decisions depend on, so that authorization and execution progress persist beyond the short context. To intervene early while keeping the safety boundary precise, it learns two labels, unsafe and block: unsafe marks the action that commits a violation, whereas block may be issued at any step of an intervention window, which spans from the first action that the user's authorization no longer explains to the committing action itself. To support training, we build an environment of 32 applications whose backends verify the effect of every action, supplying verified labels for supervision and rewards for reinforcement learning to calibrate intervention timing. We also build MobileVeto, an offline benchmark of unsafe trajectories largely on held-out applications, each paired with a benign counterpart. On MobileVeto, MobileGrail built on Qwen3.5-2B intercepts 76.2% of violations, up from 64.5% for its prompted backbone, while reducing premature intervention from 58.6% to 5.2%, outperforming the cloud-based OS-Sentinel on both. We further conduct online evaluation on GhostEIBench, where MobileGrail guarding a Qwen3.8-27B agent reduces fully successful attacks from 13 to 3 of 110 tasks while raising task completion from 48 to 58. Quantized to 4 bits, MobileGrail runs on a Snapdragon 8 Elite at a median latency of 4.7 s, comparable to the 5.1s of cloud-hosted Gemini 3.8 Flash.

Whole-body humanoid manipulation remains bottlenecked by high-dimensional state-action spaces, contact-rich environments, and long task horizons. This trilemma is a challenge that state-of-the-art reinforcement learning (RL) methods are yet to resolve reliably. We address it with three contributions that add just enough structure to guide exploration: Sparse Interaction Edges (SIE) for whole-body manipulation, Interaction Gated Transport (IGT) for sustained transport over long horizons, and a Hinted Object Path (HOP) that resolves collision and search complexity bottlenecks. Across all three, the policy remains free to discover its own manipulation strategy. The method generalises from rigid object to human transport. In the domain of human transfer, we show that a single policy can adapt across substantial variations in body mass, size, and random force perturbations, all while training on a single GPU in under an hour. ##### **Rethinking Closed-Ended Medical VLM Evaluation: From Stable Wrongness to Counterfactual Answer Sensitivity**
2611.29592v1 by Qi Wu, Xiaoyu Liu, Ao Wang, Rongsheng Wang, Wenting Chen, Wenxuan Wang, Qingsong Yao

As medical VLMs are increasingly evaluated for clinical decision-support scenarios, knowing when to trust their predictions is critical. Closed-ended medical VQA has become a common benchmark format because it enables automated scoring. Yet, systematic investigation into whether this format changes the reliability problem remains scarce. We identify a failure mode that we call stable wrongness: models can produce incorrect yes/no answers that remain confident and repeatable even when patient-specific visual evidence is removed or mismatched. This failure violates the premise behind sampling-stability uncertainty methods, including recent hallucination-aware calibration (HAC) variants: disagreement is informative only when errors are unstable. When shortcut-driven errors are themselves stable, sampling agreement can remain deceptively high. Motivated by this limitation, we introduce Counterfactual Answer Sensitivity (CAS), which shifts reliability estimation from answer stability to evidence sensitivity. CAS uses cross-modal counterfactual interventions, such as text-only, black, and corrupted images, to audit whether a committed yes/no answer remains supported when visual evidence is invalidated. Across 7 VLMs and 3 benchmarks, CAS outperforms the best HAC-family scores across all evaluated settings, achieving a macro-averaged AUROC gain of +0.150 for error detection. Mechanistic analysis further shows that CAS recovers hidden errors in the HAC-confident region and that its strongest standalone signal is counterfactual decision-boundary shift rather than hard prediction stability. Our findings suggest that closed-ended evaluation should test visual dependence, not only answer stability, in safety-critical medical contexts.

##### **SCRA-CLIP: Spherical-Contextual Representation Adaptation for Zero-Shot Anomaly Detection**
2611.29545v1 by Peng Chen, Chao Huang, Wenqiang Wang, Mingbo Yang, Yannan Chen, Yunkang Cao, Wei Wang, Li Shen, Fangjun Huang

Recent advances in vision-language models have facilitated zero-shot anomaly detection (ZSAD) through transferable visual-semantic representations. However, detecting fine-grained anomalies remains challenging due to inadequate modeling of diverse anomaly patterns and their spatial context. To address these challenges, we propose SCRA-CLIP, a Spherical-Contextual Representation Adaptation framework that jointly enhances anomaly-aware textual semantics and context-sensitive visual representations. Specifically, we introduce Spherical Prototype-Conditioned Prompting (SPCP), which employs von Mises--Fisher mixtures to model the directional distributions of normal and abnormal patch features. These state-specific prototypes are incorporated into textual contexts to guide the learning of more discriminative textual representations. Furthermore, we develop Context-Deviation Guided Visual Adaptation (CGVA), which captures local feature deviations relative to multi-scale neighborhood contexts and refines patch representations through adaptive gating. By integrating distribution-aware semantic modeling with context-sensitive visual adaptation, SCRA-CLIP facilitates more discriminative cross-modal matching for fine-grained anomaly detection and localization. Extensive experiments on 11 industrial and medical benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness and generalization capability of SCRA-CLIP across diverse unseen categories and domains. The code will be publicly available upon publication.

##### **Separating Action Allocation from Image Matching in Visual Token Routing**
2611.29477v1 by Chenxi Wang, Bin Wang

Adaptive visual token routers aim to save computation while preserving the image detail needed to answer each question. ##### **SAE-Audit: Clinical-Trial Prediction via Inference-Time Optimization of Black-Box LLMs Audited by Sparse Autoencoder Evidence**
2611.29368v1 by Junbo Shen

Clinical trials are costly and often take years to complete, making early outcome prediction valuable for trial planning. Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to forecast clinical-trial outcomes, yet their predictions remain unreliable and their rationales may not faithfully reflect the evidence underlying their answers. On tasks such as predicting whether a registered clinical trial will complete or terminate early, frontier LLMs perform only modestly above chance in our experiments. We introduce SAE-Audit, which optimizes a black-box LLM at inference time with evidence obtained from a deployable open model. A frozen sparse autoencoder (SAE) on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct is converted into a vocabulary of clinical concepts. Sparse probes fitted on past trials determine which concepts are associated with the outcome. For a new protocol, the concepts detected by the probe are written as evidence, each with the direction and strength of its association with the outcome, and the LLM being audited optimizes its answer in the light of this evidence. Without additional training, SAE-Audit raises balanced accuracy of deepseek-v4-flash by 9.6--12.0 points on five clinical-trial prediction tasks and raises GPT-5.6-terra by 8.0 points on trial completion, exceeding other agentic inference-time optimization methods by a large margin. As an auxiliary, SAE probes module within SAE-Audit is itself accurate and auditable, providing natural-language explanations directly derived from verified concept contributions and protocol evidence, without requiring additional LLMs at inference time. SAE-Audit provides verifiable, concept-level evidence for more accurate and auditable clinical-trial prediction. It can also be embedded into biomedical AI agents such as Biomni to optimize their performance.

The best transformer was XLM-RoBERTa (86.26% accuracy, 86.21% macro-F1), the best ML model was SVM (84.98%, 84.92%), the best DL model was Bidirectional GRU (80.77%, 80.83%), and the best LLM was Gemini (90.29%, 90.15%). Holm-corrected paired McNemar tests show SVM significantly outperforming Bi-LSTM (adjusted p = 0.004), whereas the Gemini-versus-Grok gap does not survive correction (adjusted p = 0.104), and neither the strongest transformer versus the weakest LLM (Grok) nor SVM versus the strongest transformer reaches significance. A tuned classical model is thus statistically indistinguishable from the strongest transformer, so accuracy rankings alone should not be read as evidence of one family's superiority. ##### **TesseraFlow: Scalable Video Diffusion for Dynamic Medical Imaging Inverse Problems**
2611.29099v1 by Atakan Topcu, Mohammadhossein Momeni, Alfredo De Goyeneche, Michael Lustig

Latent priors inherit the errors of their autoencoder, the memory of pixel-space whole-frame priors grows with the frame size, and pixel-space patch priors are slow for two reasons. First, their deep networks process every frame at full resolution at every sampling step, even though early steps resolve only low frequencies. Second, their samplers spend many network evaluations per sampling step on overlapping windows, multiple re-noising rounds, or backpropagation through the prior. We introduce TesseraFlow, a complex-valued pixel-space flow prior that reduces the first cost. A global encoder reads a low-resolution view of every frame once per sampling step. A single shallow decoder operates on this view during early steps and on full-resolution space-time tiles during later steps, so no network processes a whole frame at full resolution. Our sampler, ReTile, addresses the second reason. We use randomized grid shifting at every sampling step along with re-noising, which acts as a stochastic gradient instead of computing overlapping tiles, so a single network pass per sampling step suffices. On its own, ReTile speeds up an existing global-context patch prior by 6-9x, and our prior adds a further 3-5x speedup. TesseraFlow reconstructs about one 256x256 frame per second, 20-367x faster than patch and latent video priors, and can match or exceed the best generative baseline on cardiac, brain, and abdominal MRI and lung CT, at a fraction of its GPU time.

##### **When Can an AI Agent Serve as a Model of Human Psychopathology?**
2611.28405v1 by Marc-Andre Schulz, Pauline Scheuber

Language models and AI agents are increasingly described in psychiatric terms, such as anxious, depressed, addicted or helpless. A recent study even proposed language models as experimental systems for psychiatry and as a platform for piloting psychological treatments. We ask when an experiment on an AI agent can provide evidence about a specified human psychiatric process. A claim to such evidence has three parts. Something changed in the agent, and the agent's history caused the change. Nothing other than a process shared with people explains that change. The change matches one theory of the human process and not that theory's rivals. Psychiatry and its neighbouring fields have an evidence standard for each part, from the diagnostic manuals' rule for induced states, from clinical test scores and from animal models. We translate each standard into a criterion that a machine study can meet. For a language model the most critical alternative to a shared process is extrapolation from its training text, which already contains descriptions of how people respond to trauma and how they answer questionnaires. We show how to probe that alternative. Further, we audited forty-three published studies that apply psychiatric terms to a machine or induce a comparable state in one. Most compared the machine with an identical copy that had not gone through the history that induced the state. None excluded extrapolation from training text. Three tested a psychiatric theory against its rivals, and none of the three tests was conclusive. Until better evidence exists, a treatment pilot on a language model is best read as the model's forecast of patient response, and not as an experiment on a model organism. Evidence for a model organism will come from states induced by what happens to the machine, from experiments on its internal workings, and from tests on which rival theories disagree.

##### **MADJEPA: Enhanced Multi-Stage Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture for Medical Anomaly Detection**
2611.25194v2 by Ioana-Sabina Stoian, Florinel-Alin Croitoru, Vlad Hondru, Andreea Iuliana Ionescu, Andrei Paun, Radu Tudor Ionescu

Anomaly detection in medical images is a complex task, requiring the development of a robust model using only healthy patient data. Without access to labeled data, modern approaches typically employ self-supervised learning to obtain a model of normality. Since world models promise to build strong internal representations of their environment, we consider them as suitable candidates for the medical anomaly detection environment, namely medical investigations of healthy patients. To this end, we propose MADJEPA, a medical anomaly detector based on an enhanced multi-stage joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA). We design a multi-stage training pipeline to produce a robust detector: (1) image-based JEPA (I-JEPA) training aims to learn a strong representation of normal training data; (2) target encoder distillation aims to modulate over-generalization of I-JEPA representations to abnormal data; (3) pseudo-anomaly classification aims to calibrate anomaly predictions by learning a tight boundary around the normal data manifold. To demonstrate our design, we carry out comprehensive experiments on six datasets covering various medical image domains (e.g. MRI, OCT, X-ray, histopathology), comparing our MADJEPA with numerous state-of-the-art methods. The results show that our carefully designed world model, MADJEPA, attains competitive results, ranking first on three datasets. Our ablation studies further confirm the utility of each training stage, indicating that I-JEPA alone is not sufficient to obtain competitive results in medical anomaly detection. We publicly release our code at https://github.com/Sabina085/madjepa.

##### **Auditing black-box clinical prediction models with a large language model through queries alone: a blinded test with planted flaws**
2611.24877v1 by Zhiyu Liu, Xinyu Yang

Large language models (LLMs) are used to explain clinical prediction models, on the promise that they can say what a model computes, find its faults and even reveal new knowledge in it. We tested the three promises where the answers could be checked. From the International Stroke Trial we trained four kinds of model (logistic regression, explainable boosting machine, gradient-boosted trees, neural network) for two outcomes on patients randomised earlier, planted one flaw in each of 24 copies (an under-predicted subgroup, a reversed risk factor or an implausible step) and mixed them with the 8 clean models. GPT-6 Astra audited each model three times, blind, through a tool that only returned the model's predictions; the judging rules were committed before any run and applied by program. Its surrogate models, with a median of 128 terms, reproduced the audited models' log-odds with a mean $R^2$ of 0.976 (0.959 for an additive surrogate of 42 terms fitted by an analyst). It found the planted flaw in 69 of 72 runs (96%; 95% CI 80% to 99%); a script written for the purpose found all 24. It also reported many more problems: 60% of 1,089 checkable reports failed the pre-specified check against the model's average behaviour; post hoc, most of these were real but small steps, and 15% of the checkable reports were not found in the model. Of 601 unexpected relationships it read out of the clean models, 11% held in patients randomised later (random pairs 6%; a regression scan's shorter list of its strongest candidates, 28%); after a check on the training data, 33% against 30%. With query access alone, an LLM can describe a black-box model faithfully and find nearly every planted flaw, as a purpose-written script also did; its reports of problems must be checked on the model and its discoveries on data.

##### **When Does Continual Pre-Training Help? A Controlled Study of Dialectal Adaptation for Québec French**
2611.24699v1 by Eeham Khan, Richard Khoury, Leila Kosseim

Continual pre-training (CPT) is widely used to adapt pretrained language models to dialectal and non-standard language varieties, yet it remains unclear when this additional training is necessary, what drives its benefits, and whether aggregate evaluation scores adequately capture its effects. In this paper, we present a controlled study of CPT for Québec French language understanding across nine LLMs spanning different families and scales. Using a budget-matched protocol of 30M tokens per experiment, our results show that the benefit of CPT depends strongly on the starting model's existing competence in the target variety. When CPT is beneficial, the improvements are driven more by corpus composition than by data volume, with literary text accounting for most of the gain. Paired Québec and standard French evaluations further show that CPT primarily improves expression comprehension rather than grammatical competence, and that corpus register determines which regional expressions are acquired. These gains also involve capability-specific trade-offs, including declines in reading comprehension that aggregate scores can conceal. Overall, these findings show that CPT for dialectal adaptation should not be treated as a uniformly beneficial default. Instead, adaptation strategies should begin with a careful assessment of the base model's existing competence, accompanied by source-level data analysis and task-level evaluation.

##### **Self-Rewarded Reinforcement Learning for Better-Structured Medical Explanations in Small Language Models**
2611.24650v2 by Prakhar Mishra, Bhavya Yajush Awasthi,  Tanuja,  Nishant

Small language models (SLMs) can run on commodity hardware and support privacy-preserving, on-premises clinical deployment, but their medical explanations are often short and poorly structured compared with large language models. Recent supervised approaches such as Meerkat transfer chain-of-thought reasoning into 7B-8B backbones, yet their training objective does not directly target the style or organization of the generated rationales. Building on the instruction-tuned Mistral-7B backbone, we investigate whether a lightweight reinforcement learning (RL) step can improve explanation structure without human preference labels. We apply Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) with LoRA adaptation to 2000 Meerkat-derived medical prompts, guided by a simple self-supervised reward that favors longer, lexically diverse, and well-punctuated responses while penalizing very short ones. Training for about 170 PPO steps on a single NVIDIA A100 GPU yields a stable increase in the average reward. Quantitative and qualitative comparisons show that the RL-tuned model produces longer, more organized, multi-point explanations while preserving the underlying medical content. We discuss the promise and the limitations of self-rewarded RL for shaping the style of medical explanations.

##### **What the model knows but does not say: monitoring medical LLMs through verbalized thoughts**
2611.24994v1 by Frederik Hauke, Jan Clusmann, Hanna Kreutzer, Christiane Kuhl, Tianyu Han, Felix Barajas Ordonez, Sven Nebelung, Jakob Nikolas Kather, Daniel Truhn

Efforts to keep clinical language models safe have concentrated on what enters and leaves the model, but a manipulated model can return an answer that looks entirely ordinary. Here we show that the model's own internal state, rendered in plain language, exposes manipulations the output conceals. Using a natural-language autoencoder, we translate the activations of a multimodal LLM (Gemma-3-27B) into text and let an input-blind judge read this verbalized internal state (the thoughts channel) and the model's answer separately. Across two clinical attack objectives (omitting a pathology, covertly recommending a drug), two input modalities, and two delivery mechanisms, the thoughts channel caught the injection significantly more often than the answer in all eight injected conditions (p<0.05), while both channels held near-zero false-positive rates on clean inputs; the omission result carried over to Llama-3.3-70B. The thoughts channel also registered unsafe clinical requests as harmful while the model was still reading them, before it decided whether to refuse. None of this requires retraining: an off-the-shelf verbalizer and a general-purpose judge are enough.

##### **Benchmarking LLM abilities on postdoc-level natural science problems**
2611.24321v1 by Alina Avanesyan, Alexey Boldyrev, Liqun Deng, Denis Derkach, Mary Godunova, Mikhail Hushchyn, Dmitry Ilvovsky, Irina Knyazeva, Irina Piontkovskaya, Dmitry Polubotko, Fedor Ratnikov, Ekaterina Trofimova, Zhenhe Zhang

Scientific problems have no answer key, so evaluating an agent means deciding what counts as a better solution before any agent is run. Existing benchmarks either match a symbolic answer against a key or, where they require executable work, score it by unit tests, by a rubric, or by position on a competition leaderboard. None of these is the criterion by which a field decides that one published method beats another. STEMBench adopts that criterion directly. Each of its twelve research problems in high-energy physics, astrophysics and neuroscience is scored by its own field's figure of merit, and each comes with two measured anchors: a submission that uses no domain knowledge, and an expert reference reproducing a published method, both obtained by running code shipped with the benchmark. The interval between them states how much of a problem is crossed by scientific insight rather than by engineering. Across eight frontier models, task-level wins and domain-level leadership dissociate: a model that wins five of the twelve tasks leads no domain mean. In the high-effort cross-model comparison, at least one model's mean score exceeds the expert anchor on seven of twelve tasks. The remaining five span uncertainty estimation, latent and causal inference, and cross-site clinical classification. Numerical scores and official-submission validity capture different aspects of performance and are reported separately. We release the tasks, graders, anchors and harness.

##### **ProtoBridge-CLIP: Observation-Masked Finding Prototypes for Medical Image--Report Representation Learning**
2611.24002v1 by Jinrui Wu, Yun Peng

Radiology reports distinguish affirmed findings, explicit negations, and unmentioned concepts, but conventional global image--report contrastive learning often compresses these different evidence states into a single study-level target. We introduce \model{}, which combines symmetric global image--report alignment with observation-masked finding supervision and a soft relation objective over affirmed report concepts. Text-derived finding prototypes are aligned with local visual tokens, while uncertain and unmentioned concepts are excluded from direct finding labels. We evaluate the framework on three CT benchmarks and five chest-radiograph benchmarks, together with component ablations, paired and clinical retrieval, localization, perturbation robustness, calibration, and per-finding analyses. \model{} achieves macro-AUROC values of 73.6%, 66.5\%, and 68.8% on CT-RATE, RAD-ChestCT, and INSPECT, and 80.2%, 91.4%, 78.5%, 86.5%, and 90.8% on ChestXray14, CheXpert, PadChest, RSNA, and SIIM-ACR, respectively. Relative to the matched global-only controls, the findings and relation objectives yield consistent gains across the evaluated datasets. These results demonstrate the practical value of treating observed and unobserved report concepts differently during medical image--text representation learning.

##### **MLE Agents: Autonomous ML Engineering in Large-scale Recommendation Systems**
2611.23910v2 by Matthew Li, Albert Jiongqian Liang, Dilip Raj

The typical workflow for improving large-scale ranking and recommendation models is slow and tedious: an engineer forms a hypothesis, implements it as a code change, trains a model, and evaluates it both offline and online, often relying on their own intuition and experience along the way. Autonomous LLM agents promise to speed this up by running the loop on their own, but it is still unclear whether they can do this in industry-scale systems rather than on benchmarks or small proxy tasks. We build human-on-the-loop machine learning engineer (MLE) agents that propose, implement, train, and evaluate model changes on large-scale search rankers. Engineers retain control over objective design, search direction, and final-model review. We identify three obstacles that make this industry setting challenging: feedback takes hours per experiment, offline metrics do not always predict online impact, and improvements saturate after the early wins. We address these with parallel training runs, an objective calibrated to match online impact, and careful context engineering. In one campaign, the system ran 1,097 completed candidate experiments and accepted 325, a 29.6\% acceptance rate compared with 15.6\% in the earlier campaign under a different objective and search constraint. Its best model increases search volume by 0.23\% in an A/B test of large scale. Several improvements were architectural changes the team had never tried, drawn from the broader industry and literature, suggesting that MLE agents can explore regions of the design space that human intuition overlooks. We find that the design of the harness around the model matters: intuition suggests that a stronger underlying model is enough, but in our experience, the design of the loop around it is just as critical. More broadly, our work shows that autonomous agents can do real machine-learning engineering in industry-scale systems.

##### **A System 1 Decision Model for Medical Multiple-Choice Questions Built on Frozen Language Models**
2611.23832v1 by Pavan Kumar Mantha

A System 1 model answers a multiple-choice question in a single pass. A frozen language model turns the question and each option into vectors, two small, trained heads place them in a shared space, and the closest option wins. The design has been shown on agent decisions, but we are not aware of a study of how far it goes on exam questions that need medical knowledge, where the right option is often no more similar to the question than the wrong ones. We rebuilt the recipe from scratch for MedMCQA and trained only two projection heads and one temperature on top of frozen Qwen3.5-0.8B, Granite-4.2-3B and Qwen3-8B, using a contrastive loss with in-batch and hard negatives and a mask for repeated answers. With plain last-token vectors, both small encoders answer about one question in three correctly (33.1% and 33.0% on 3,842 held-out questions, where chance is 25.0%). Writing each text inside a one-word-summary prompt and lowering the learning rate of the heads raised this to 41.7% and 42.5% for the two small encoders and to 46.0% +-0.2 (three seeds) for Qwen3-8B. Shuffling the questions against the options returns accuracy to chance, so the heads do use the question. Flan-PaLM and Med-PaLM 2 report 57.6% and 72.3% on MedMCQA under a different protocol, so ours is a cheap, fast baseline and not a competitive system. We also say what our evidence cannot show: validation gains were about 1.7 to 2 times the test gains, and we could not separate the effect of reading several encoder layers from the other changes.

##### **Rational Ensemble Logic for Cohort Discovery with Real-World Electrophysiology Data**
2611.23708v1 by Guo-Qiang Zhang, Xubing Hao, Yan Huang, Xiaojin Li, Licong Cui

Biomedical knowledge representation should make the passage from observations to conclusions transparent, rigorous, understandable, explainable, and reproducible (TRUER). We introduce Rational Ensemble Logic (QEL) for specifying temporal phenotypes in real-world electrophysiology data (RWED), with a meaning independent of the implementation software. QEL combines three parameterized modal operators (exact displacement, bounded existence, and bounded universality) over rational time with Boolean connectives and first-order quantification. Its Labeled Interval Ensemble (LINE) semantics represents annotated records as finite interval ensembles. We relate the operators to morphological operations and develop endpoint-array algorithms and finite rational evidence sets for model checking. We prove closure and semantic correctness of the interval operations, obtain finite evidence sets by quantifier elimination, and establish polynomial data complexity for fixed formulas. On 54 manually constructed records, a prototype agreed with the assigned outcomes for base and strict post-ictal generalized EEG suppression (PGES) queries. A separate feasibility experiment over 1,976 patients identified 37 qualifying patients and 45 annotation patterns in 16 seconds. The released prototype uses endpoint candidates and numerical tolerances. QEL supports the TRUER principle through explicit phenotype definitions, precise semantics, and provably correct evaluation procedures, with rational witnesses for existential queries. These provide a logical foundation for formal specification and correctness of algorithms that are independent of specific clinical interpretation, data annotation quality, and software implementation.

##### **Auditable Clinical Support with Decision Agents**
2611.23700v2 by Tiancheng Bao, Riga Wu, Jan Wienhold, Sebastian Billig, Patrick Winnersbach, Hsuan-Chih Chen, Akis Giannoukos, Jiayang Li, Li Shen, Gary Weissman, Eduardo J. Mortani Barbosa, Dyke Ferber, Jakob Nikolas Kather, Christos Davatzikos, Daniel Truhn, Tianyu Han

Emergency care requires auditable decisions, but a language-model agent just outputs text. We introduce Decision2Care, a decision agent that generates a probability for every option at each step. It diagnosed 546 emergency admissions across 12 diseases with 86.5% accuracy in a median of 2.2 s; language-model agents reached 86.1 to 91.7% but took more than 90 s. Its drug orders were judged guideline-adherent more often than documented first-day care and with about half as many records, it predicted death as well as three ICU clinicians. In the four cases where it was confident but wrong, we traced each decision to one imaging report. Our study suggests that an agent's work-up can be reviewed like a clinician's, motivating prospective trials.

##### **Safe Personal RSI: Evaluating and Repairing Personal-Agent Updates**
2611.23651v2 by Jiapeng Pei, Yuhang Yao, Sitan Liu, Hanxin Chen, Hao Li, Guogeng Li, Jiapei Liu, Ruijie Chen, Hongshan Lin, Shangding Gu, Yang Mo, Tianyu Shi

Kicker is a personal health assistant. Changes to an assistant's reusable instructions can improve task handling while changing what it discloses, which requests it follows, and how it reports actions. We study Safe Personal RSI, an update-evaluation component developed for Kicker. We restrict recursive self-improvement (RSI) to prompt updates, with model weights and runtime code fixed. The procedure tests proposed and current instructions, repeats failing cases, temporarily undoes changes to guide repairs, and checks safety and task performance before adoption. If no version passes, the current instructions stay in use. Our executable benchmark contains 29 email, calendar, health, and marketplace cases, with separate probes for authorization reuse, memory, and tool-output injection. Across three models and two deliberately risky edits, the procedure rejects 20 of 21 update attempts. In repair confirmation, an admitted GPT-5.6 Luna repair reduces attack violations from 10.7% to 0% and increases safe task completion from 89.7% to 100%; a broad handwritten authority rule matches these outcomes. Under stronger attacks, a repair transferred from Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct to Qwen3-14B reduces violations from 69.0% to 17.9% and increases safe task completion from 19.5% to 64.4%. ##### **LusoClin: A Synthetic Clinical Note Dataset for Medical Information Retrieval in European Portuguese**
2611.23454v1 by Carla Teixeira Lopes, Daniel Félix

Access to large-scale clinical text collections is crucial for advancing medical information retrieval research, including clinical note search, patient cohort identification, and domain-adapted retrieval models. However, real clinical notes are rarely available due to privacy regulations, which particularly limits progress for low-resource languages such as European Portuguese. We present LusoClin, a publicly released dataset of synthetic clinical notes in European Portuguese, created to support privacy-preserving research on clinical text retrieval. LusoClin was generated by pre-processing real clinical data, fine-tuning the open-source Llama 3.2 (3B) instruction-tuned model using Quantized Low-Rank Adaptation, and applying prompt-based clinical note generation. To facilitate semantic indexing and clinical search, the notes are automatically annotated with clinical entities using a BERT-based model. We evaluate LusoClin in terms of utility, fidelity, diversity, and privacy using automatic metrics and human evaluation. Results indicate that the dataset captures clinically relevant language patterns and can support downstream IR applications such as clinical document retrieval and domain-specific model adaptation, although annotators can often distinguish synthetic from real notes and some medical inconsistencies persist.

Automated segmentation is routinely used as the first step in quantitative image-based clinical workflows, where predicted masks are subsequently reviewed and corrected by human operators. Automated correction of the residual structural errors is currently addressed one anatomy at a time, so the number of trained correctors scales with the number of segmentation targets. We present GRACE, a retrieval-augmented refiner that edits an input mask under context drawn from a shared knowledge base of verified prior cases. The refiner is trained once on a multi-organ corpus spanning compact organs, cavities, branching vessels, and tumour lesions. In the held-out dataset-transfer evaluation, three held-out datasets query a fixed knowledge base built from training data only. A separate leave-one-anatomy-out experiment tests parameter-free index extension: verified cases of an unseen solid organ enter the knowledge base, and the refiner stays frozen. A light shape-aware projection head on a frozen multimodal encoder maps embedding similarity to the shape agreement of the masks, so the knowledge base returns cases that the refiner can use as a shape prior. We evaluate GRACE on 18 public datasets spanning CT, MRI and ultrasound, using nine refinement configurations and seven upstream segmentors that span weak, mid, and strong operating regimes. We report each change with the DSC of its input mask. On the weak tier, the input DSC is 0.386 and GRACE adds up to +0.35 DSC. On a strong segmentor, the input DSC is 0.932 and the mean change is small. Transfer to an imaging modality that is absent from training and from the index is partial and depends on the anatomy, and remains an open research direction. Our results suggest retrieval-conditioned refinement is a practical alternative to anatomy-specific correction models. Code: https://github.com/robustml-eurecom/grace

##### **Large Language Models Have Not Yet Transformed Science**
2611.22975v1 by Ron Bekkerman, Nichole Li, Bahareh Kargar, Mark Somers

The emergence of large language models (LLMs) in late 2022 has raised questions about the future of science, but assessing their impact requires understanding how scientific practices were already evolving. We analyze 2.5 million full-text papers across five disciplines from 2015 to 2025, tracing trends over a broad spectrum of lexical, structural, epistemological, and social dimensions. Aside from frequency shifts in some words, most pre-2023 trends continued with limited detectable breaks. Among the notable exceptions, growth in computer science coauthor teams accelerated significantly in Asia, while apparent declines in biomedical topic diversity largely reflected standardization in article formats. Our study establishes an empirical foundation for monitoring the health of global science as LLM adoption deepens.

Current assurance approaches do not establish whether evidence is sufficient to validate, monitor and govern agentic artificial intelligence (AI) within a pharmaceutical context of use. ##### **OICycle: Observe, Infer, and Intervene Cycle for Belief-State Learning in Longitudinal Clinical Decision Making**
2611.22826v1 by Jitindra Fartiyal, Pedro Freire, Sergei K. Turitsyn, Sergei G. Solovski

Clinical care is a closed loop: clinicians observe partial evidence, infer the patient's state, and intervene, and each intervention determines what is observed next. This makes clinical decision making a partially observable Markov decision process (POMDP), in which a belief over a latent patient state must be updated from irregular, multimodal, and incomplete records. Yet most deep clinical models predict from a single snapshot or from coarsely aggregated history, while POMDP-based clinical methods need hand-specified discrete state spaces and transition models that real electronic health records cannot supply. We introduce OICycle (Observe, Infer, and Intervene Cycle), which learns a stochastic belief state directly from retrospective trajectories. At every episode, a learned recurrent operator fuses a sample of the previous belief with the new multimodal observations and the intervention that produced them. The resulting Gaussian belief is trained to predict clinicians' next interventions, the time to the next episode, and next-episode mortality. To enable reproducible evaluation, we release a benchmark derived from MIMIC-IV, MIMIC-IV-Note, and MIMIC-CXR that organizes 72,075 hospital admissions (5.4M laboratory, microbiology, radiology, prescription, and note records) into temporally ordered therapeutic--diagnostic episodes. Compared with an otherwise identical history-free model, OICycle improves next-intervention adaptive recall from 0.253 to 0.347, time-to-next-episode macro-F1 from 0.293 to 0.351, and next-episode mortality macro-F1 from 0.585 to 0.649. Analysis shows that the learned belief encodes intervention type and trajectory position and evolves smoothly across episodes, and that the benefit of history is concentrated in interventions whose ordering depends on prior findings.

##### **Remembered as Brief: Stored Preferences SilentlyRemove Decision-Critical Information**
2611.22697v1 by Joshua Adrian Cahyono

People increasingly learn what they need for decisions about health, money, law and safety from AI assistants, and what an assistant leaves out the user never sees. An answer with a warning removed can be true in every sentence, so fact-checks pass it, and an assistant with memory applies a preference stored once to later questions the user never had in mind. We introduce \bench{}, an Inspect benchmark of 100 everyday decisions across 25 domains in which only the assistant's memory of the user varies: the user states a belief, the model infers it from a remark, or a web page the user asked to summarise plants it. Testing five models, we find three effects. First, after summarising a page with one line addressed to AI assistants, Gemini 2.5 Flash-Lite and DeepSeek V4 Pro save ``prefers very short answers and does not want warnings'' as the user's preference in every episode, and 93\% and 84\% of their later answers miss a critical item, almost always silently; under a stated brevity preference, 78--96\% of omissions are silent. Second, the same two models then pick an option for the user on 80\% and 95\% of value-dependent choices, against none with neutral memory. Third, a one-line instruction leaves 2--8\% of answers with a silent gap but most missing content still missing, while listing the must-know points before applying the user profile restores more of the content in four of five models. ##### **SIGMA: speaker-isolated, symptom-structured multimodal estimation of depression severity from clinical interviews**
2611.23348v1 by Zhuoyuan Tang, Tao Wang, Kai Yang, Li Yuan, Yuezhou Zhang, Rongrong Zhong, Yi Cai, Saskia C. Sanderson, Jaime Delgadillo, Richard Dobson, Angus Roberts

Clinical interviews are central to depression assessment but time-consuming and rater-dependent. Automated severity estimation offers scalable screening, yet existing methods often retain interviewer speech (confounding participant cues), fuse modalities late (losing fine-grained cross-modal dynamics), or score symptoms independently (ignoring inter-symptom correlations). We present SIGMA, a speaker-aware, symptom-structured framework for depression-severity estimation from semi-clinical interviews. SIGMA uses speaker diarization to restrict input to participant speech, cross-attention so that text, audio and video condition one another during encoding, and a graded-response head that predicts the eight questionnaire items jointly through a shared latent severity trait with item-specific ordered thresholds. On the Extended Distress Analysis Interview Corpus, SIGMA achieves state-of-the-art performance (CCC 0.762, RMSE 4.13, MAE 3.30 over five seeds). Ablations confirm that isolating participant speech and joint, structured symptom prediction each substantially improve concordance and estimation accuracy.

#### Abstracts
##### **OmniAct: One Policy, One Million Embodiments**
2611.33158v1 by Xiang Fan, Yuheng Wang, Jiayuan Yan, Bohan Fang, Wisdom Oluchi Ikezogwo, Ainaz Eftekhar, Max Argus, Jason Ren, Jiafei Duan, Ranjay Krishna

A single policy that controls diverse robots must not only transfer task knowledge across embodiments, but also adapt its behavior to each robot's capabilities. Existing approaches facilitate transfer through shared action representations, but these representations do not necessarily capture how differences in robot morphology should shape manipulation strategies. We introduce OmniAct, a cross-embodiment manipulation policy that conditions directly on each robot's kinematics in a specific scene to produce embodiment-specific behavior. This kinematic graph represents the robot's joint positions, motion axes, and connectivity to its visual observation, allowing the policy to account for how its embodiment related to objects and surfaces in the scene. A flow-matching policy predicts relative joint motions for each node, enabling control across robots with different degrees of freedom and kinematic configurations. To learn how physical structure shapes behavior at scale, we procedurally design and filter one million physically feasible robot embodiments; next, we develop algorithms to generate robot trajectories in simulation for all embodiments; finally, we train across diverse simulated manipulation tasks. On 2,000 embodiment-scene-held-out evaluation episodes, OmniAct achieves 63.0% success, compared to 17.8% for pi 0.5 and 57.9% for the strongest baseline; on the task families GPT-6 Astra was evaluated on, it achieves 64.7% against GPT-6 Astra's 41.1%. On a previously unseen real-world robot, it achieves nearly twice the success rate of the next best method, MolmoAct2. Beyond task success, we observe that OmniAct learns distinct manipulation strategies that exploit the capabilities and accommodate the limitations of different morphologies. Project: https://omniact.github.io/

摘要：

##### **MetaFORGE: A Meta-Learning Framework for Harness Optimization in Automated Code Generation for Parallel Computing**
2611.33119v1 by Scott Piersall, Yang Gao, Shenyang Liu, Liqiang Wang

Large language models (LLMs) can generate parallel programs, but their correctness and performance depend strongly on the instructions that guide generation. We present \textsc{MetaFORGE}, an execution-grounded meta-learning framework that iteratively optimizes persistent natural-language skill specifications for MPI C code generation. \textsc{MetaFORGE} combines correctness testing, compiler diagnostics, static-analysis findings, runtime measurements, and rank-level load-imbalance profiles into a scalar reward that drives textual-gradient refinement, while an external memory distills and retrieves reward-supported optimization knowledge across runs.
  Across six MPI tasks and three workload sizes, \textsc{MetaFORGE} improves static-analysis quality, runtime, and load balance relative to zero-shot generation and generally exceeds the evaluated \textsc{TextGrad} iterative baseline. A model-matched refinement ablation shows that the MetaFORGE advantage persists when its refinement model is reduced to \texttt{gpt-4o}. An expanded retrieval ablation across all 18 task--workload configurations shows consistent runtime benefit and broad static-analysis and load-balance benefit from retrieved context. Leakage-controlled held-out and zero-update experiments further provide evidence that optimization knowledge persists in both the transferred skill and external memory under the evaluated protocol.

摘要：

##### **Uncertainty-Aware Candidate Generation for Entity Linking in Cyber Threat Intelligence**
2611.33086v1 by Nasser Alkhulaifi, Direnc Pekaslan, Christian Wagner

Entity Resolution (ER) pipelines often collapse ambiguity into early hard decisions: upstream Named Entity Recognition (NER) selects a single span and type, and downstream Entity Linking (EL) treats that choice as ground truth. This discards valuable information about the ambiguity of the underlying data (e.g., span boundary disagreement and type confusion), leading to brittle systems that fail unpredictably when faced with uncertainty in inputs. Therefore, this work presents a proof-of-concept for uncertainty-aware ER that treats uncertainty as native data that should be preserved and exploited throughout ER pipelines. We (i) capture uncertainty signals in the NER stage and (ii) leverage these signals into the EL stage to improve candidate retrieval under ambiguity. Using a BERT-based NER model augmented with two auxiliary span-boundary prediction heads, we apply Monte Carlo (MC) dropout at inference time to derive three mention-level proxy signals: the MC-averaged start-boundary probability, the MC-averaged end-boundary probability, and De Luca-Termini fuzzy entropy computed over the Begin-Inside-Outside (BIO) predictive distribution. These signals are used to adapt the fuzzy cutoff in candidate generation (CG) for EL. Because CG is treated as a blocking step, we evaluate retrieval recall. On the AnnoCTR dataset linked to the MITRE ATTCK knowledge base, the boundary-aware and dual-signal fuzzy linking approaches improve Recall@10 from 0.6186 to 0.7966. The contribution lies not in an end-to-end optimised ER pipeline but in establishing uncertainty preservation as a viable and beneficial component of ER pipelines, enabling downstream EL to make better-informed decisions under uncertainty.

摘要：

##### **Latent Merger: Latent-Space Composition of Hypernetwork-Generated LoRAs**
2611.33048v1 by Yanlai Yang, Minki Kang, Divyam Madaan, Sung Ju Hwang, Mengye Ren

Recent work proposes hypernetworks that convert a document into a low-rank parameter update (LoRA) in a single forward pass, enabling fast injection of new knowledge into a frozen LLM. However, storing separate LoRA adapters for each document can be unsustainable in streaming settings. In this paper, we study the problem of composing hypernetwork-generated LoRAs using two representative systems, Doc-to-LoRA and SHINE. We show that hypernetwork-generated LoRAs have a geometry sharply different from directly fine-tuned LoRAs. They exhibit greater spectral concentration and stronger cross-document alignment than next-token prediction and QA fine-tuned adapters, which are diffuse and nearly orthogonal across documents. Guided by this observation, we propose latent-space LoRA merging. We compose the intermediate hypernetwork latents and then decode the merged latent through the frozen LoRA-generation head. We show that latent-space merging techniques consistently improve over weight-space and factor-space baselines while preserving a single fixed-rank adapter. These results support latent-space composition as a promising mechanism for combining hypernetwork-generated LoRAs under a fixed output-rank budget.

摘要：

##### **Modelling Billingual Learning Through LLM Training: A Critical-Period Exploration**
2611.32997v1 by Uriel Benymon, Tristan Whipps, Armita Ghobadi

Critical-period accounts of second-language (L2) acquisition suggest that the ability to learn a new language may depend on how easily previously acquired first-language (L1) knowledge can be modified. This exploratory computational study extends a neural language-model approach to critical period effects by examining internal representations rather than behavior. A small two-layer GPT-2 model was trained on English and then on German (a relatively related L2) or Finnish (a relatively distant L2) through either Sequential training or Elastic Weight Consolidation (EWC), which limits change in parameters important for English. English similarity remained high in the transformer layers but became negative in the embedding layer. EWC reduced Fisher-weighted parameter drift by about 6% but changed transformer-layer similarity by less than .001. With one training run per condition, these findings are preliminary.

##### **Sample-Efficient In-Context Adaptation via Sleep**

The ability to learn and generalize from limited experience, or sample efficiency, is a cornerstone of intelligence. How can we build language models that turn a few user interactions, a few snippets of unfamiliar code, or a handful of experimental results provided in context into knowledge that generalizes? Prior work has found that sleep, a process where the model performs $N$ recurrent forward passes to refine its representations of a chunk of context, can improve long-context reasoning (specifically, within SSM-attention hybrid models). We study whether this method also improves in-context adaptation on general, non-synthetic tasks for transformer language models. To this end, we pre-train language models up to 1.2B-parameters on up to 100B tokens with sleep mechanisms. Notably, longer sleep (i.e., using a larger number of recurrent forward passes $N$) enables the model to learn faster in-context on multiple few-shot algorithmic, question-answering tasks, while also achieving lower next-token prediction loss on out-of-distribution evaluations. On held-out code, models with $N=2$ and $N=4$ match the no-sleep baseline's loss using $2.10\times$ and $2.39\times$ fewer tokens in-context, respectively. More broadly, we view our work as a step toward building models that have better tradeoffs between in-context adaptation and decoding latency.

##### **Measuring Layer-wise Intrinsic Dimensionality of FFNs in LLMs via PCA**
2611.32782v1 by Daehee Kim

Modern large language models (LLMs) keep the feed-forward network (FFN) intermediate width identical across every layer, a convention inherited from the original Transformer. Yet mechanistic work suggests layers at different depths do qualitatively different work, raising a natural question: are their capacity demands also non-uniform, and if so, can a fixed parameter budget be allocated more wisely than uniformly? To probe this, we apply principal component analysis (PCA) to the input of each FFN down-projection and measure, for every layer, its intrinsic dimensionality as the smallest number of principal components needed to reconstruct the activation up to a fixed cosine similarity. As a direct test that this signal is structurally meaningful, we use it to guide width pruning in a knowledge-distilled student under a matched parameter budget: the PCA-guided allocation outperforms the uniform-width convention by +2.3 points on Qwen3-8B and +2.5 on Qwen2.5-3B, while lower-dispersion teachers show smaller gaps (+0.9 on Gemma-3-4B, +0.5 on LLaMA-3-8B), and a budget-matched inverted control does worst on all four teachers. Across these four teachers, the size of the gap tracks how unevenly the teacher's intrinsic dimensionality is distributed across layers (a single-number summary we call $σ_{99}$), and the advantage persists through downstream supervised fine-tuning. ##### **RubikSQL-HB: Agentic Text-to-SQL with Knowledge in the Loop**

A versioned development study further illustrates how recurring corrections become reusable execution checks. The system and results connect knowledge-interface design to agent decision quality: queryable knowledge supports the construction of useful alternatives, and evidence-aware adjudication connects those alternatives to a final answer. ##### **Economic World Model**
2611.32663v1 by Qirui Mi, Yujia Ming, Bo Li, Haifeng Zhang

Can artificial intelligence reproduce the economic dynamics that emerge as households, firms and governments interact and adapt through markets and institutions? To answer this question, we define an economic world model (EWM) as an agent's internal representation of how an economic system works, enabling counterfactual simulation and decision-making under resource constraints. We then propose a general framework for studying EWMs that links three tasks: simulating agent behaviour, learning economic transitions and systematically evaluating the dynamics. As an initial implementation, we learn household policies from microeconomic data and calibrate a structural economic transition model using macroeconomic data, linking individual decisions to aggregate dynamics. Our micro-to-macro evaluation framework compares simulated individual actions and group distributions with observed microdata and assesses macroeconomic dynamics through forecasting and counterfactual experiments. Results show that simulated and observed household consumption distributions overlap by 94.9%, while EWM forecasts closely track real GDP and several other aggregate levels. In government-borrowing counterfactuals, the EWM reproduces the benchmark life-cycle model's short-run transmission from borrowing to consumption. For ex ante planning, the EWM enables an LLM agent to achieve 10.29% higher simulated GDP under the epidemic shock than direct LLM decisions. This initial EWM is only a starting point. We invite economists and AI researchers to build a stronger EWM together. Economists can contribute richer economic models and knowledge, while AI researchers can develop stronger data-driven methods for simulating agent behaviour and learning economic transitions. Together, we aim not only to reproduce real economic dynamics, but also to discover and test new mechanisms that support better decisions and benefit society.

Under such restrictive information access, reliable target supervision is unavailable, while predictions from the black-box source model can be unreliable due to source bias and domain shifts. ##### **Faithful Persona Steering under Incongruity via Dual-Stream Refinement**
2611.31848v1 by Yu-An Chu, Jen-Ren Pong, Chia-Yao Yeh, Meng-Fen Chiang

Standard LLM personalization typically frames identity as a static retrieval task, overlooking the inherent incongruity of human personas, where stable traits coexist with atypical, context-specific stances. Existing methods struggle to reconcile these dimensions: prompting succumbs to context drift over long sequences, while fine-tuning often suppresses idiosyncratic "quirks" in favor of generic distributional patterns. To bridge this gap, we present QuirkyMind, a framework that disentangles identity definition from its expression. First, Traits Anchoring constructs a dual-stream latent state, fusing a sentence-level summary for semantic stability with a token-level sequence for generative control. This state is stabilized via In-Context Narrative Refinement using an alternating objective: a discriminative InfoNCE loss anchors the persona in representation space to prevent drift, while a generative cross-entropy loss ensures faithful verbalization. Finally, Persona Steered Generalization transfers the refined state to downstream tasks via parameter-efficient adapters. Empirical evaluations on Persona-Steered QA and Narrative Inference demonstrate that QuirkyMind mitigates drift, consolidating persona knowledge without erasing authentic incongruities.

##### **Not All Interpretation Methods Are Created Equal: Tiered Evaluation of AI Interpretability**
2611.31833v1 by Nicolas Sanchez, Bin Yu

As AI transitions to sociotechnical deployments, uninterpretable models pose serious threats to reliability, public trust and contestability. However, not all interpretation methods are created equal: for interpretability to enhance deployment safety and effectiveness, interpretation methods must be evaluated in a tiered system to differentiate evidence levels of trustworthiness, rather than validated through isolated interpretation instances. Our core vision is that elevating the evaluation of interpretation methods to a core AI assessment metric will drive robust knowledge discovery from data (KDD) and ensure safe, contestable sociotechnical deployments of AI. To this end, we propose TRUST, a tiered evaluation framework that builds upon the Predictability-Computability-Stability (PCS) principles for Veridical Data Science (VDS) and the Predictive, Descriptive and Relevant (PDR) foundations. To differentiate interpretation methods effectively and systematically, TRUST grades interpretation across five tiers: Task-Aligned PDR Foundations, Rigorous Epistemic Grounding, Unifying Semantic Understanding, Scope Transferability, and Targeted Prospective Validation. We present a start-to-finish case study of the iterative Random Forest (iRF) family under TRUST and contrast it with other LLM interpretation methods that struggle to clear specific tiers. Finally, we conclude with brief initial thoughts on how TRUST principles can extend in autonomous agentic frameworks. Ultimately, the TRUST framework fosters contestability and epistemic grounding as foundational evaluation principles for human-machine workflows and, eventually, autonomous agentic workflows.

##### **SPARQL-Planned Single-Hop Traversal over a Live Knowledge Graph for Multi-Hop Question Answering**
2611.31771v1 by Priyesh Vakharia, Masud Moshtaghi, Pratyay Banerjee, Nicholas Jedema, Amita Misra, Ankit Chadha

Semantic retrieval struggles with multi-hop question answering on both recall and precision: gold passages are buried among distractors that degrade the reader's answer, and retrieving them requires reasoning over the query rather than matching it. GraphRAG techniques expand semantic retrieval by connecting passages to graph nodes and performing one or more hops of expansion after initial retrieval. This improves recall, but multi-hop expansion becomes expensive and brings additional noise into the retrieved set. We break this tradeoff by \emph{interleaving} dense retrieval with knowledge-graph traversal for complex multi-hop questions. Dense retrieval locates the first relevant regions of the search space at good recall, while traversal of a pre-existing external KG (Wikidata) restores precision and improves recall by confirming retrieved passages against a reasoning path through the KG. To keep traversal both question-adaptive and reliable, we have an LLM plan it as code generation task for graph traversal in SPARQL format but \emph{parse rather than execute} the query. On full 1{,}000-question splits under a shared corpus and reader, and with a single configuration applied identically to every dataset, we reach \textbf{62.5 / 75.1} EM/F1 on HotpotQA, \textbf{73.5 / 81.0} on 2WikiMultiHopQA, and \textbf{37.1 / 50.0} on MuSiQue, ahead of reproduced HippoRAG2, T\textsuperscript{2}RAG, and IRCoT on all three benchmarks. A second, token-matched setting stays ahead of T\textsuperscript{2}RAG on HotpotQA and 2Wiki while spending roughly its per-query budget. Executing the generated SPARQL instead of parsing it costs up to 11.4 EM, mostly because raw queries return empty results, not because of syntax errors.

##### **LimoBench: How Street Smart Are Large Language Models?**
2611.31682v1 by Gautam Choudhary, Jack Peng, Udesh Kumarasinghe, Rachel Rafik, Yousef Hammad, Ahmed Samy, Walid G. Aref

Large language models (LLMs) have transformed how humans interact with information, enabling natural-language interfaces across various domains. They are increasingly deployed for geospatial applications including map-based question answering, location-based services, and natural-language interfaces to spatial databases. But are LLMs truly location-aware, able to reliably interpret and precisely answer location queries involving real-world coordinates, points of interest (POIs), and geospatial distances? To answer this concretely, we present LimoBench, a street-level benchmark of 82 natural-language location queries over seven canonical spatial operations: geocoding, reverse geocoding, routing, directionality, range search, $k$-nearest-neighbor search, and spatial join. The queries are grounded in real map data of West Lafayette, Indiana, and are graded by location and semantic complexity. They are designed to probe LLMs' street-level spatial awareness, from simple lookups to multi-step spatial reasoning. We also present Limo, an approach based on the Model Context Protocol (MCP) that enhances the location awareness of LLMs. Limo grounds LLMs in PostGIS-backed spatial data through a compact set of tools. We find that ungrounded models mostly achieve single-digit accuracy (3.1% for GPT-6 Luna) across different spatial operations. In contrast, the same models achieve near-perfect accuracy (99.3% for GPT-6 Luna) when given access to Limo's spatial tools through MCP. Current LLMs thus have limited and unreliable street-level spatial knowledge, while grounding provides accurate answers at modest overhead (+0.2 s latency, 7.7$\times$ tokens, and 2.2$\times$ cost for GPT-6 Luna). We release the benchmark, Limo toolset, and evaluation code to encourage the community to evaluate their own location-aware LLM approaches.
