##### **Wavefront: Low-Rank Drafting with Parallel Causal Refinement for Speculative Decoding**
2611.31638v1 by Huy Huu Nguyen, Xin Huang

Speculative decoding accelerates LLM inference by letting a cheap drafter propose tokens that the target model verifies in parallel. Parallel block drafters such as DFlash and P-EAGLE propose a whole block in one forward pass, but they lose the dependence between tokens in the block; DSpark restores part of it with a lightweight correction head that runs sequentially. Measuring DSpark, we find that this head overrides the base prediction on most slots, so the expensive base readout through the target's full lm_head mostly provides a seed. We therefore propose Wavefront, which follows a simple principle: shrink the base and strengthen the correction head. Wavefront replaces the full lm_head with a learned low-rank base, conditions the correction head on the whole drafted prefix within the same block, and resolves it with a few parallel Jacobi passes. A fixed pass budget makes the draft step free of data-dependent control flow, so we capture it as a single CUDA graph in vLLM with fused kernels. On Qwen3-8B, Wavefront improves throughput over xPress, DSpark and DFlash by 2.6%, 10.7% and 23.6% on average across math, code, chat and agentic benchmarks, and by 9.6%, 14.3% and 28.9% on SPEED-Bench long-context prompts. On Qwen3.8-27B it leads DFlash2, DSpark and xPress by 7.6%, 10.6% and 5.0%, and by 7.4%, 5.8% and 6.0% on the same long-context prompts. The gains persist as serving load grows to concurrency 32.

##### **Tablassert: Declarative Extraction of Knowledge Graphs from Biomedical Tables**
2611.31614v1 by Skye L. Goetz, Jared C. Roach, Gwênlyn Glusman

Tables published in biomedical research papers, particularly supplemental tables, capture knowledge assertions that may not be in the main text of the manuscript for several reasons, including low statistical significance, space limitations, and narrative scope. These tables include data (e.g., association statistics, assay readouts, curated gene lists, and omics) encoding relationships between biological entities. Tablassert converts such tabular data (Excel, CSV, TSV, Parquet) into knowledge graphs ready for the NCATS Biomedical Data Translator (Fecho et al. 2025). A user declares in YAML how each table's data map to subject-predicate-object-qualifier statements; Tablassert resolves free text (e.g., ''lung cancer'') to standard identifiers, attaches provenance and statistics, and emits graph files conforming to Translator standards (D. Unni, Bruskiewich, and Moxon 2026; D. R. Unni et al. 2022).

##### **Safe Autoformalization: Sufficiency and Necessity with Entailment Graphs**
2611.31571v1 by Umut A. Acar, Eli Goldweber

Autoformalization translates a natural-language statement into a formula that captures its intent, which depends on the statement's context: the background that disambiguates it. LLMs make autoformalization practical, but their probabilistic output carries no formal guarantees. We propose techniques for safe autoformalization that certify one-sided logical adequacy: a formula sufficient for the intent is safe to enforce, and a formula necessary for it is safe to rely on. We use LLMs to generate candidate formulas and order them by logical strength in an Entailment Graph (EG), an ordering a solver decides. Each edge carries a residual and a co-residual, the two adjoints of the entailment order, all fixed by the candidate pool alone. In a context, two searches return the sufficient frontier, the weakest candidates that entail the statement, and the necessary frontier, the strongest candidates the statement entails; each step is one local check against an edge's residual or co-residual. The frontiers bracket the statement and yield the ambiguity residue, which holds exactly of the situations the candidates certify neither as satisfying nor as violating it; the statement is ambiguous precisely when the residue is satisfiable. For natural-language statements and contexts, an LLM entailment oracle decides the checks; it is sound if it accepts only checks that hold, and complete if it accepts every check that holds. We prove that a sound oracle makes the search safe regardless of pool quality, and that a complete one makes the frontiers tight. On security-critical AWS properties, EGs are safe in all 20 cases of an expert-adjudicated study, where majority vote over equivalence clusters and Universal Self-Consistency are unsafe in 25% and 10%. An ablation with the same oracle but no graph is unsafe in 25% and rejects every candidate in another 30%. We machine-checked our proofs in Lean.

##### **Denoising Diffusion with Progressive Posterior Sampling**
2611.31558v1 by Zeyu Luo, Shuai Huang

Diffusion models provide effective generative priors for solving noisy linear inverse problems. However, existing posterior sampling methods may require singular value decomposition (SVD) of the measurement operator or prior knowledge of the measurement noise variance, while guidance based on local gradients provides only a limited view of posterior geometry. We propose progressive posterior sampling (PPS) with manifold projection, which automatically estimates the measurement noise variance and avoids SVD. During reverse diffusion, PPS constructs a Gaussian approximation to the posterior distribution in closed form and guides a sample drawn from the local prior toward the approximate posterior mean. We then project the guided sample onto a spherical level set that approximates the local noisy image manifold, promoting consistency with the local geometry of the diffusion prior. Experiments on FFHQ and ImageNet across four restoration tasks and three measurement noise levels demonstrate competitive reconstruction accuracy and perceptual quality by PPS.

##### **SpatialCraft: Evolving Procedural Intelligence from Experience and Skills for Spatial Reasoning Agents**
2611.31475v1 by Xiwei Liu, Yulong Li, Xuhui Li, Zhixiang Lu, Haolin Yang, Jianxu Chen, Yifan Lu, Di Fan, Yutong Xie

Spatial reasoning agents increasingly rely on external tools, such as 3D reconstruction, segmentation, and geometric computation, to answer questions that a multimodal large language model (MLLM) cannot resolve from pixels alone. Tool access, however, shifts the bottleneck to orchestration: the agent must decide which tools to call, in what order, and how to interpret their outputs, yet a stateless agent repeats the same mistakes across tasks. We ask whether a frozen tool-using agent can improve its orchestration from its own interaction history, without parameter updates. We present SpatialCraft, a self-evolving framework that maintains two forms of external knowledge: Experiences, condition-action tactics distilled by contrasting rollouts of the same task, and Skills, multi-step procedures with explicit activation and termination conditions. Three designs couple the two streams. Experiences can be anchored to a Skill, appearing only while it is active rather than once per task. A Contrastive Utility Score estimates each Experience's effect by randomly withholding it across rollouts of the same task, separating its contribution from task difficulty, and Experiences with consistently positive utility are consolidated into the Skills they support. Across five spatial benchmarks and four backbones, SpatialCraft achieves the best accuracy in all 20 settings, showing that a frozen agent can learn to orchestrate spatial tools from its own experience.

##### **Leaks by Installments: Cross-Session Answer Disclosure in LLM Tutors and a Ledger-Based Defense**
2611.31429v1 by Venkata Muttineni, Karthik Narayan, Christopher David

Tutors built on large language models (LLMs) are told not to give away answers, but a learner does not have to ask for the answer all at once. Existing safeguards judge each reply within the current conversation, so a learner can open a new session, reword the problem, or request a worked solution to the same problem with different numbers. We call this cross-session disclosure and propose a defense: a persistent, per-learner disclosure ledger. For every problem a learner brings, the system builds a typed solution graph. Before a reply is released, a gate matches the reply's own content, not the learner's wording, against the ledger by result values and by type-labeled solution structure, then computes which steps the learner could now derive. The reply is withheld if that set reaches the final answer or exceeds a budget. In a simulation of 3,000 problems, the ledger reduced leakage through renumbered practice variants from 100% to 10.6% while honest learners completed 94-99.5% of problems. With two real LLM tutors (Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct and Phi-3.5-mini-instruct) on 60 problems, leakage through renumbered variants fell from 0.58 to 0.08 and from 1.00 to 0.18, with 98% honest completion. The ledger did not reduce split requests beyond a per-session gate in the live runs. All acceptance criteria were fixed before each run.

##### **Learning What vs. Learning How: Decomposing Task and Interface Competence in Agentic Training**
2611.31427v1 by Chaeeun Lee, Hossein A. Rahmani, Aryo Pradipta Gema, Kexin Xu, Emine Yilmaz, Pasquale Minervini, T. Ian Simpson, Chen Jin, Amrutha Saseendran

LLM agents operate through harnesses that define system prompts, action formats, feedback loops, and resource constraints. A model may possess the knowledge needed to solve a task yet fail to express that knowledge through a particular harness. This raises a basic question: when a policy fails under a given harness, is it missing task knowledge or the competence to operate the interface? We distinguish two components of agentic competence: task competence, the knowledge and reasoning needed to solve a task, and interface competence, the ability to act on that knowledge through a given harness. Across four reasoning and agentic benchmarks, we show that harness-conditioned training yields vastly different outcomes depending on how these interact. Multi-harness distillation aggregates task knowledge from trajectories collected through different harnesses into a shared policy, but this knowledge is realised only when training preserves or improves competence on the inference-time interface. Otherwise, single-harness training can induce interference in interface competence, sometimes catastrophically collapsing performance on a held-out harness despite improvements in underlying task knowledge. To disentangle task and interface supervision, we introduce harness rehearsal: distillation on traces that carry minimal task knowledge while exercising the target interface. Cross-harness task supervision or rehearsal alone yields little gain, but their combination yields gains far larger than either: on BFCL V3 multi-turn with Qwen3-4B, they achieve 8.5% and 32.5% respectively, versus 58.8% together. Finally, on-policy reinforcement learning (RL) is more robust than distillation to single-harness training: it does not substantially degrade performance on other harnesses, and interface competence acquired through multi-harness distillation persists through subsequent RL.

##### **ToolSmith: An Automatic Tool-Generation Framework for Multimodal Search Agents**
2611.31198v1 by Rui Sun, Junhua Huang, Luca Zancato, Md Mofijul Islam, Kai-Wei Chang

Recent multimodal search agents use reasoning, vision, and live web search across many turns to answer questions beyond parametric knowledge by orchestrating a small library of hand-crafted search and visual tools. However, the library is laborious to create and maintain, and frozen when new query types, evidence formats, and failure modes are encountered in the wild. Human practitioners, by contrast, cope with insufficient tools by fashioning new ones from familiar parts and reusing them across tasks. Inspired by this, we propose ToolSmith, an automatic tool-generation framework that grows and reuses modules on top of a lean base tool library. We evaluate ToolSmith on three in-domain multimodal search benchmarks and ten out-of-domain benchmarks under a VLM-as-a-Judge protocol. ToolSmith consistently improves over the base library across deployed agents of varying scales, with larger backbones reaping greater gains, demonstrating the scalability of the approach. Our code and data are available at https://threesr.github.io/toolsmith/.

##### **SRCWDNet: Semantic-Relational Cross-Window Dual-Graph Network for Multi-Label Remote Sensing Scene Classification**
2611.31135v1 by Fareeha Amir

Multi-label remote sensing scene classification remains a challenging task due to complex spatial-spectral patterns, extreme class imbalance, and inter-label dependencies that are often insufficiently modeled. To address these challenges, we introduce a Semantic-Relational Cross-Window Dual-Graph Network (SRCWDNet), which integrates three key modules: (1) a Cross-Window Attention module that captures local and global visual context through learnable global tokens; (2) a Dual Label-Graph module that adaptively combines statistical co-occurrence graphs and learned semantic-embedding graphs to facilitate label-relational reasoning; and (3) a Neural Classifier Chain that sequentially predicts labels to exploit inter-label dependencies. Evaluated using five-fold cross-validation and trained with an Asymmetric-Graph-Consistency loss, SRCWDNet achieves accuracies of 98.93% and 96.53%, with corresponding F1-scores of 0.9365 and 0.8723 on the MLRSNet and China-MAS50k datasets, respectively, outperforming CNN, transformer, and lightweight baselines. These findings demonstrate that jointly modeling visual attention and adaptive label correlations can substantially improve multi-label remote sensing scene classification, particularly under long-tailed class distributions.

2611.31346v1 by Yuhan Bian, Zhaokai Liang, Jia Wang, Haoyu Liu, Yu Chen, Jianfeng Qu, Zhixu Li

Recent advances in MLLMs have improved social understanding, enabling models to recognize emotions and distinguish truthful from deceptive behaviors. ##### **Self-Upgrading Embodied Agents**
2611.31117v1 by Jiajun Fan, Neil He, Qinglong Zheng, Changyi Yang, Tong Wei, Andy Mao, Zetian Li, Chaoran Cheng, Hui Ren, Ruihan Guo, Xianrui Zhong, Xizheng Yu, Jingyuan Li, Xi Zhu, Kaiqu Liang, Chang Xu, Zun Qian, Zidi Xiong, Yuezhu Zhao, Xu Huang, Ge Liu

An embodied agent built on a language model goes to work with the weights it was given and keeps them for life. To get better, it needs more training, or reflection, memory, search or prompts that cost every step and are lost when the task changes. Editing weights by reasoning, weight-space programming, could end this, but has stayed with models that only reason or relied on a far larger commercial model. We introduce the self-upgrading embodied agent, to our knowledge the first that learns from its own experience by upgrading its own weights on the job, between its tasks, with no training, no gradient, no reward model, no second model, no test-time scaling and no harness or prompt engineering. By day a 27B agent works in a household environment. At night it reads its record, finds many of its steps wasted on replies that reach the token limit before any action, traces the habit to one direction in its hidden states, and rewrites one tensor of its weights. An agent that rewrites itself must first never break itself, so it is its own examiner: it tests every change against its unchanged self on practice tasks and keeps only what holds. When an edit holds, the next day it is a better agent, at the same cost per step: over thousands of held-out episodes, 48 fresh seeds a task, its one kept edit makes it complete significantly more tasks, in fewer steps, with fewer wasted and fewer tokens. This self-improvement is multi-round, stable and continual: through the nights that follow, the upgrade holds, without collapse. Of four sizes of its family, only the 27B agent does this. From here on, an embodied agent need not keep training and inference apart: as it works, it gathers its own experience and upgrades itself, with no separate pre-training or post-training and no human intervention. We believe this opens lifelong continual learning for robots: stable, continual, recursive self-improvement.

##### **RUIBench: What Makes a Good Robot-Use Interface?**
2611.31115v1 by Yanzhe Chen, Zhijun Cao, Zechen Bai, Wenzheng Zeng, Kevin Yuchen Ma, Yiqi Lin, Guoqiang Liang, Xiaokang Liu, Ke Sun, Kevin Qinghong Lin, Mike Zheng Shou

##### **CocoonBench: Can Multilingual Search Agents Escape Language Information Cocoons?**
2611.30727v1 by Hongchuan Zeng, Bowen Liu, Changjiang Gao, Senyu Han, Zhipei Xu, Peisong Wang, Xinbing Liang, Yuhan Li, Nuo Chen, Blaze Chen

Multilingual search agents can access the global Web, yet may remain trapped in a language environment with insufficient evidence; we refer to this failure as a language information cocoon. However, existing search benchmarks largely operate within a single language, especially English, overlooking entity connections across multiple languages. We introduce CocoonBench, a benchmark that evaluates whether multilingual search agents can escape such language information cocoons. CocoonBench mines answer-centered relational paths from a frozen Wikidata-derived graph and selects clue sets that are individually necessary and jointly identify a unique answer. Using language-specific entity names, it records each task's minimum language cover, removes direct identifiers, and renders each task as matched prompts in three languages together with its construction certificate and provenance. Across 10 state-of-the-art models, mean accuracy on CocoonBench ranges from 33.8% (DeepSeek-V4-Flash) to 69.7% (Claude-Opus-4.8), leaving even the strongest system below 70%. The same construction certificates support path-aligned trajectory analysis, enabling diagnosis of model failures in language routing, evidence-path recovery, and answer use. The result shows that escaping a language cocoon requires more than switching languages: successful cross-lingual search depends on both covering the correct languages and connecting the evidence found across them.

摘要：

##### **HOSE: Hierarchical Orthogonal Subspace Erasure for Concept Unlearning in Diffusion Models**
2611.30592v1 by Xiaoqi Wei, Li Li, Kejiang Chen, Hanzhou Wu, Xinpeng Zhang

Text-to-image diffusion models can generate harmful or sensitive content, such as pornographic or violent imagery, raising serious security concerns. Model editing has recently emerged as an efficient approach to mitigate this issue. It modifies pretrained models with minimal parameter updates to alter specific concepts while preserving overall model capabilities. However, due to the entangled representations of different concepts in diffusion models, direct modifications inevitably disrupt non-target concepts. Meanwhile, U-Net's hierarchical heterogeneity hinders unified strategies from balancing erasure and quality. To address these issues, we propose Hierarchical Orthogonal Subspace Erasure (HOSE). This method projects parameter updates into the subspace orthogonal to general knowledge, thereby eliminating interference with non-target concepts. Considering the hierarchical heterogeneity of the U-Net architecture, we further propose a hierarchical adaptive mechanism, constructing differentiated subspace projections for different layers to precisely adapt to the model's hierarchical structure, thereby resolving the inherent trade-off between erasure and image quality in unified constraints. Experimental results demonstrate the proposed method reduces harmful content generation by 80% compared with the unedited model. While it inevitably incurs some image quality degradation, it still outperforms other editing-based baselines in terms of quality preservation, and achieves a significantly better trade-off between harmful content suppression and quality loss than these baselines. Notably, our approach demonstrates higher adversarial robustness than existing SOTA method, indicating that the target concepts have been more thoroughly erased. The source code is publicly available at https://github.com/sevenqiiu/HOSE.

##### **SuSi-RAG: A Hybrid Graph RAG for Equipment Maintenance**
2611.30588v1 by Hansi Zhang, Wilma Johanna Schmidt, Wanlin Yang, Xiaozhi Shen, Adrian Paschke, Evgeny Kharlamov

In the manufacturing domain, equipment maintenance tasks depend on expert knowledge. Their documentation is often stored in heterogeneous formats and sources. The enhancement of LLMs with Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is promising for bridging the scattered knowledge, but traditional vector-based RAG shows weaknesses with two characteristics which are common in real maintenance queries: complex causal chains and cross-domain synthesis. To address this, we propose SuSi-RAG, a hybrid Graph RAG solution, that matches a user query and each maintenance record using subgraph similarity. This work shows an in-use system: SuSi-RAG has run on an active Bosch production line for more than eight months, handling 10,664 question-answer interactions and demonstrates upper bound answer accuracy of 96.25% (confirmed error rate 3.75%). In a proof-of-concept, SuSi-RAG improves top-1 retrieval accuracy from 61.5% to 96.2% and expert-rated answer quality from 44.9% to 84.1% against a vector-based baseline. We present an integrated operational feedback loop to update SuSi-RAG.

##### **Body and Soul: Decoupling Robotic Brain and Policy with RoboHarness-Zero**
2611.30550v1 by Yihao Shen, Yuxuan Chen, Xuantong Liu, Jun Gao, Weijian Luo, Tianyang Hu

Vision-language-action (VLA) policies can execute individual skills but fail to complete tasks that require combining several of them. In our audit of Xiaomi-Robotics-1, an estimated 43\% of failures involve choosing the wrong target or losing track of task progress. Wrapping a frozen VLA in a vision-language model (VLM) harness is a natural remedy. Existing harnesses can gain substantially from task- and policy-specific preparation, raising the question of how much of that gain transfers to a new task or policy. An ideal harness should (i) need minimal preparation for a new task, (ii) need no redesign for a new policy, and (iii) preserve standalone capabilities the policy already has. To the best of our knowledge, our \ours{} is the first harness built this way. Its premise is that choosing targets and tracking progress are easy for a VLM such as GPT-6 without task preparation, while the frozen VLA already handles continuous, contact-rich motion. The frozen VLA therefore executes continuous control while the VLM plans zero-shot and monitors execution; when progress stalls or targets drift, the VLM first rephrases the instruction and, only as a fallback, executes short generated code over generic motion primitives before returning fine control to the VLA. The supervision rules stay fixed across policies, requiring only a thin interface adapter and an automatically profiled skill configuration per policy. On 200 RoboCasa365 scenes, \ours{} with GPT-6 Astra raises the success rate of frozen Xiaomi-Robotics-1 from \textbf{\color{blue}57.5\%} to \textbf{\color{blue}70.5\%} and preserves \textbf{\color{blue}90.4\%} of its standalone successes (104/115) while rescuing 37 of 85 failures. On a shared 50-scene subset, the same rules add 8 to 10 percentage points to each of four other frozen policies, while existing preparation-based harness systems often fail to transfer to a new policy.

##### **When Does Continual Pre-Training Help? An Empirical Study of Decoupled Knowledge and Generation Trajectories in Clinical LLMs**
2611.30507v1 by Niclas Doll, Jasper Schulze Buschhoff, Shalaka Satheesh, Héctor Allende-Cid, Katrin Klug

Continual pre-training (\textit{CPT}) is a standard route for domain adaptation, yet existing studies typically track pre-training loss or multiple-choice question answering (\textit{MCQA}) accuracy alone, offering only a narrow view of how adaptation unfolds. We jointly track factual knowledge via \textit{MCQA} and open-ended behavior via pairwise preference rankings across $18$ checkpoints for each of eight instruction-tuned \textit{Qwen} models ($0.5B$ to $8B$), adapted to German medicine with \textit{CPT} and \textit{SLERP} merging. The two axes converge at consistently different rates, with preference rankings reaching $90\%$ of their total gain after a median of $1.7B$ tokens versus $3.5B$ for \textit{MCQA}. A judge-free token-distribution measure reaches this threshold at $1.9B$ tokens. Per-aspect scores indicate that these early preference gains largely reflect surface alignment, meaning models can sound domain-appropriate before their factual knowledge catches up. Larger adapted models close more of the gap to unadapted next-tier generalists, whereas preference gain per FLOP falls by about an order of magnitude across our size range. We therefore argue that judge-based preference scores, which can plateau while \textit{MCQA} knowledge is still improving, should not be used alone to infer adaptation convergence or domain readiness.

##### **HWRE-Bench: Can LLM Agents Understand Hardware They Did Not Design?**
2611.30422v1 by Peilong Zhou, Cangyuan Li, Kaiyan Chang, Ying Wang

Hardware benchmarks for LLM agents all run the design flow forward, from a specification to RTL, a layout or a bug fix. We ask the reverse question: given an implemented artifact, such as a layout, a gate-level netlist or a protected simulation model, can an agent work out what it does well enough to act on that knowledge? We introduce HWRE-Bench, a benchmark for agentic hardware reverse engineering built on one observation. Each step of the forward flow discards information (names, hierarchy, word-level structure, generator parameters, the programming model), and a reverse-engineering task asks the agent to recover what was lost and to prove it with an executable deliverable that the artifact alone cannot supply. Since layout extraction is now mechanical and a correct netlist is already a cycle-exact model, every task carries a mandatory copy-the-netlist control that must score zero. HWRE-Bench spans five tracks (Extract, Abstract, Audit, Interoperate, Modify), four information-loss levels and white- versus black-box access, all on original designs. We report a pilot of 148 rollouts from two agents on four seed tasks under 16 conditions, with stage-annotated trajectories. Agents built their own layout extractors and audited a sanitized layout as reliably as a netlist, at about 27% more turns. Keyless, equivalence-preserving obfuscation changed neither pass rates nor defect-repair scores. Whether agents read the structure at all depended on task framing rather than on necessity, so pass rate alone does not show understanding. Provider safety filters stopped 16% of one agent's runs on the audit tasks. These results argue for deliverables beyond equivalence and for reporting run validity explicitly.

摘要：

##### **When Does Reasoning Help Entity Disambiguation? Small LLMs In-Domain and Under Shift**
2611.30334v1 by Samy Haffoudhi, Fabian M Suchanek, Nils Holzenberger

Entity disambiguation (mapping ambiguous mentions in text to entities in a knowledge base) is a foundational step in tasks such as knowledge graph construction, question answering, and information extraction. Recent work has applied LLMs to this problem, achieving stateof-the-art performance. Notably, reasoning LLMs have been shown to perform entity disambiguation in a true zero-shot fashion, generalizing across knowledge bases and domains. However, it is unclear whether this capability survives, or remains competitive, with smaller LLMs, where entity disambiguation has typically relied on reranking rather than reasoning. We ask when reasoning is worth its cost at small scale. We compare pointwise, listwise, and reasoning-based candidate-selection components within a classical coarse-to-fine entity disambiguation pipeline, across the classical, few-shot and true zero-shot settings. The reasoning paradigm is trained with distillation and reinforcement learning recipes. The benefit of reasoning grows with domain shift: negligible in-domain, decisive in the true zero-shot setting. We show that two distinct factors are at work: the reranker's uncertainty over the candidates locates where the paradigms disagree, while shift governs which is right when they do.

##### **MMMem: Self-Improving Agents with Task-Aware Multimodal Experience Consolidation**
2611.30287v1 by Yuxuan Chen, Changwei Lv, Yunfei Li, Zhongjing Du, Kehui Tan, Zijun Liao, Ruochong Zheng, Hao Chen, Can Deng, Zheni Zeng, Chang Liu, Jie Chen

AI agents accumulate conversations, images, and documents through long-term interactions with users. Answering later questions often requires connecting evidence across these records, yet the resulting understanding is rarely retained, allowing mistakes to recur on related questions. Existing multimodal memory methods largely focus on storing and retrieving records, with limited mechanisms for learning from completed tasks and consolidating task-specific experience. We therefore propose MMMem, a task-aware multimodal memory framework that extracts and consolidates experience from completed tasks through four complementary strategies. First, source-linked multi-view memory organizes original text, images, and rendered pages alongside generated scene images that combine clues across records for retrieval. Second, source-generated tasks support knowledge alignment at two levels: warm-up consolidates source knowledge into task-relevant experiences in external memory, while self-training adapts the model's parametric knowledge to task demands. Third, the same vision-language model distills completed tasks into experiences that record the task context, conclusion, and support links. Finally, task-aware retrieval recalls source views and relevant experiences for answering and verification, while source-supported reuse guides long-term retention. Across three memory benchmarks and two vision-language readers, MMMem outperforms eight baselines. With Qwen3.5-9B, MMMem achieves relative gains of 36.9%, 17.6%, and 15.2% over the best-performing baselines on LoCoMo, Mem-Gallery, and LooGLE v2, respectively. Ablations show consistent gains from training and memory warm-up, while case studies illustrate how individual components work in practice.

##### **A statistical measure of trust in generative AI**
2611.30286v1 by Alina Ermilova, Valeria Yakupova, Nikita Sushko, Andrey Lovyagin, George Ovchinnikov, Maksim G. Sharaev, Evgeny M. Mirkes, Denis Turdakov, Ivan Y. Tyukin

Trustworthiness is a crucial issue for generative AI, as modern models can produce convincing and confident responses even when their outputs are incomplete, unsupported, or wrong. The frequent absence of ground-truth knowledge or labels complicates verification of generative AI outputs. To address this issue, we propose a trustworthiness framework grounded in model-internal confidence calibrated against an external expert reference. We estimate per-token trustworthiness of a student generative model as the statistical typicality of each generated token relative to an expert model's distribution, where the expert is used as a distributional reference rather than as a source of factual verification labels. To this end, we introduce an AI ontology calibrator that leverages the student model's internal latent features and token-level output statistics to compute expert-reference acceptance scores. Combined with traditional token-level confidence, entropy, and perplexity statistics, calibrator scores yield a compact set of $15$ response-level indicators for correctness classification. Evaluated across BF program generation, mathematical problem solving, and medical question answering, combined calibrator and traditional indicators provide complementary reliability signal which could be used to build detectors of untrustworthy outputs and hallucinations. Remarkably, our experiments show that these detectors are transferable across domains, although transferability is not always symmetric.

##### **Learning Heterogeneous Reasoning in Large Language Models: From Mode Compatibility to Local Bottlenecks**
2611.30281v1 by Sunbowen Li, Dandan Song, Shengkun Ma, Xuanning Cao

Post-training large language models routinely pools reasoning trajectories from diverse teachers or algorithms, yet it remains unclear whether standard supervised fine-tuning (SFT) can learn such heterogeneous strategies without explicit mode conditioning. We study the learnability and local bottlenecks of heterogeneous reasoning through a formal mixture formulation and controlled experiments. We first formalize the representational compatibility of the optimal autoregressive model with latent reasoning mixtures: the target next-token distribution weights modes by their prefix posteriors and concentrates on a single mode once alternative modes assign negligible likelihood to the prefix. In a verifiable symbolic graph-search environment with paired breadth-first and depth-first search trajectories, no consistent disadvantage is observed for unlabeled mixed SFT relative to homogeneous or labeled baselines under matched token budgets while preserving both modes. Scaling across 0.6B--14B models and data budgets largely eliminates global mode confusion, shifting the primary bottleneck to local state-tracking transitions. Because a local mistake affects the final outcome only when subsequent reasoning is sensitive to that step, we find that only a small fraction of local errors are outcome-determining \emph{Critical Reasoning Nodes (CRNs)}, and that single-step repairs at these nodes rescue failing trajectories across symbolic search and natural language benchmarks whenever the model has sufficient downstream competence to complete the remaining derivation. Our environment is open-sourced at https://github.com/Bowen1911/Symbolic_graph_env.

##### **PixelOne: From Options to Pixels in a Single Forward for GUI Grounding**
2611.30235v2 by Kanghui Tian, Shuai Dong, Yuhan Zhu, Congcong Wang, Ji Zhao, Tianxiang Jiang, Zhixiong Zhang, Shengyuan Ding, Yi Wang, Jiaqi Wang, Yu Qiao

GUI grounding requires precise clicks at low latency. Recent models such as GUI-Decisions and GUI-Swift predict coordinates in one forward pass using fixed global bins with local refinement. However, screenshot downsampling removes detail needed to locate small controls, while fixed bins cover larger pixel ranges at higher resolutions. We introduce PixelOne, which transfers spatial knowledge from a search teacher to a student that predicts clicks directly from screenshots at native resolution. The teacher uses VideoJEV's text-option scorer to select rows and columns and progressively zoom into candidate regions. The student inherits the teacher's parameters and matches its distributions over explored regions through spatial distillation alongside box supervision. At inference, the same decision head selects a visual token, and a lightweight pixel head predicts the click within it, all in one forward pass. A click-aligned loss encourages pixels inside the target box to score above those outside. Across four benchmarks, PixelOne-Pro-35B-A3B achieves 83.8% mean accuracy, the highest among the evaluated single-forward grounders, with a mean latency of 0.43 s per target.

##### **CLIP-FMoE: Scalable CLIP via Fused Mixture-of-Experts with Enforced Specialization**
2611.30233v1 by Luong Tran, Lan-Cuong Nguyen, Huynh Dang Nguyen, Dat Nguyen Cong, Dung D. Le, Van Nguyen

Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures have emerged as a promising approach for scaling deep learning models while maintaining computational efficiency. However, existing MoE adaptations for Contrastive Language-Image Pre-training (CLIP) models suffer from significant computational overhead during sequential training and degradation of zero-shot capabilities. To address these limitations, we propose CLIP-FMoE, a novel approach that integrates MoE architecture into CLIP fine-tuning. Our method uses Isolated Constrained Contrastive Learning, a pipeline that trains specialized experts on cluster-based data partitions to accelerate expert specialization. Additionally, we introduce a Fusion Gate mechanism to mitigate catastrophic forgetting of pre-trained knowledge. Extensive experiments across multiple benchmarks demonstrate that our approach achieves consistent improvements on downstream tasks while preserving zero-shot capabilities. Furthermore, our method demonstrates robust performance across varying context lengths, making it particularly suitable for diverse real-world applications.

##### **Retrieval-Augmented Generation in the Agentic Era: Tradition, Present, and Prospect**
2611.30181v1 by Chenming Tang, Haolin Li, Yiran Li, Zixian Yang, Lizhu Zhai, Rui Sun, Qizhou Huang, Yunfang Wu

Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) couples frozen language models with external, updatable knowledge, so that generation is grounded in retrieved evidence rather than in parametric memory alone. This work maps RAG in the agentic era by joining them on a single lifecycle axis, from offline indexing through pre-retrieval, retrieval, post-retrieval, and orchestration over these stages. It treats indexing and orchestration as first-class components and agentic workflows as a paradigm of their own, and it covers the agentic work that has appeared since those reviews. Beyond these components, it covers evaluation, applications, and the questions that remain open for future exploration. From tradition to present and beyond, this work systematically reviews RAG from a perspective of the agentic era.

##### **Trustworthy agentic lunar robotics: safety-constrained contingent planning with a foundation-model planner**
2611.30179v1 by Bingqi Huang, Bingchuan Wei, Yingkai Cai, Zhaokui Wang

Sustained lunar-base operations require robots that restore equipment and deliver cargo under conditions unforeseen before deployment. Flown space autonomy executes pre-validated procedures and enters a safe hold outside their scope, whereas foundation models synthesize plans on site that cannot be verified before execution. We formulate these operations as safety-constrained contingent planning with irreversible actions, erroneous records, three-valued physical evidence, and costly, deadline-constrained sensing. We propose asymmetric knowledge-level replanning (AKR), which treats the planner as an untrusted black box: reversible actions run on the planner's assumptions, and irreversible actions run only after a minimum-cost sensing cover verifies every precondition. Under sound measurements, no irreversible action executes on a false precondition for any planner or record, the sensing cost before an irreversible action is bounded below for every sound knowledge-based policy, and execution with a complete planner ends in a verified goal or a certified hold. AKR executed no hazardous action in 21,000 Monte Carlo trials with sound observations or in 58 full-stack runs; the FF-Replan acting rule executed one in 7% of those trials. The median AKR run took 1.10 times the fastest run on the same mission, against 1.50 under the K-replanner acting rule. AKR completed 20 of 21 contingency and sequential-fault runs with an unchanged task model; the original PLEXIL and EUROPA$\rightarrow$PLEXIL procedures completed none.

##### **Reading the Tea Leaves, Again: Can Large Language Models Predict Language Model Training Runs?**
2611.30141v1 by Xinran Zhao, Xiang Li, Tong Chen, Jinchuan Tian, Zichun Yu, Tongshuang Wu

LLM agents now propose research ideas and run the experiments that test them, but they train every run to its end, whereas a researcher reads a run's configuration and the beginning of its curve and stops the runs that show no promise. Reading a run this way takes more than describing or implementing a training method: it takes knowing what a design choice does to a run. We build Tea Leaves to measure this knowledge on 682 real training runs across pretraining, fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning (RL): a forecaster sees the first 20% of a run with its configuration, description, and code, and predicts the rest. Across 11 LLMs and a range of forecasting baselines, the strongest LLMs match or exceed the best curve fitter on smooth loss curves (skill 0.66 against 0.62) and lead on noisy RL reward curves (0.43 against 0.13). What bounds every forecaster is what the log carries: on sparsely logged community runs all methods lose most of their skill, and none reliably flags a failing run in advance. Beyond the curve, information helps and effort does not: related runs in the prompt cut the error on loss curves by 39%, and knowledge of training dynamics lets the strongest LLMs order all 36 pairs of runs that differ by a described code change, which the early curves do not separate, whereas tools add little. This knowledge can be learned from logged runs: a small open model trained on public runs matches the best LLM on loss curves. Applied to the progress curves of research agents, the same reading ranks tasks by their remaining headroom, although for a single agent run it does not improve on the last value. We release the benchmark, the trained readers, and their training data to test and train LLMs' understanding of training and to help research agents decide which runs deserve their compute.

##### **OWE-Bench: A long-horizon benchmark for measuring real-world engineering**
2611.30017v1 by Jacob Cole, Martin Dimakis, Victor Barres, Bertie Vidgen, Edward Hu, Jason Wei

We present OWE-Bench (OpenWorldEngineer-Bench), a benchmark of 26 long-horizon engineering tasks written by domain experts in software engineering, physical engineering, machine learning, and scientific computing. Tasks range from optimizing kernels and designing an aircraft, to identifying the faults responsible for an earthquake. Each task requires significant domain knowledge and engineering work, with models averaging hundreds of millions of tokens per task. Solutions are scored on practical measures such as speed, cost and accuracy, both on provided cases the agent could test against and on hidden cases that an engineer would expect from the task description. The best-performing frontier model so far, Claude Fable 5.2, scores 54.6% and averages 211 million tokens per task. We highlight two findings. First, when models are given more time, they use more test-time compute and achieve higher scores. Models also scale differently with test-time compute: GPT-6 Astra achieves higher scores at shorter time limits and at equal token use, while Claude Fable 5.2 uses more tokens at longer limits to achieve the highest overall score. Second, each model scores lower on hidden cases than on the provided cases, by an average of 18 points, which we call the deployment gap. Models also fail to predict the deployment gap, overestimating their performance on hidden test cases by an average of 21 points. We hope that OWE-Bench grounds progress toward agents whose engineering solutions perform as well in deployment as they do in testing.

##### **SHAKTI: Evidence-Grounded Conversational AI for Nutrition and Health**
2611.29988v1 by Chaitanya Pallerla, Dongyi Wang

Large language models (LLMs) answer many diet and health questions but can state unsupported claims and cite sources that do not support them. On 1,000 stratified questions, we compared single-shot, multi-hop, corrective and deep-research retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) with Claude Sonnet. SHAKTI adds conversational query rewriting, USDA FoodData Central lookups, source-link checks and online verification of quotes and numbers. MedCPT reranking of hybrid retrieval gave the best MRR (0.21) and MAP@100 (0.14). Large-scale retrieval with agentic evidence selection and mechanical verification yields auditable, low-hallucination nutrition answers.

##### **LLM-Assisted Software Migration: from Module-Level Transformation to Repository-Level Integration**
2611.29942v1 by Yanan Zhang, Shengqi Liu, Tao Zhang, Yidi Liu, Alan Tang, Hailong Chen, Yantao Jia

Software migration adapts an existing implementation to a new language, version, API, framework, or platform while preserving its behavior. Large language models (LLMs) succeed on individual functions and modules, but this success does not readily carry over to entire repositories, particularly deployable applications. This survey reviews LLM-assisted migration from module-level transformation to repository-level integration, culminating in the delivery of complete applications. We analyze 96 sources---including 46 direct migration papers---across four perspectives: (1) supported software engineering activities, (2) model roles as editors or orchestrators, (3) orchestration workflows (agentless, single-agent, or multi-agent), and (4) enabling techniques including knowledge engineering, project context, environment feedback, and model adaptation. We then compare evaluation practices and benchmarks. Although 30 of the 46 studies target repositories, most acceptance evidence relies merely on build success, supplied tests, or static and model-based proxies. Moreover, compilation, visual similarity, test outcomes, and runtime behavior capture fundamentally distinct properties and cannot be conflated into a single success rate. We also identify critical gaps in requirements engineering support and in controlled comparisons of agent architectures. Finally, we discuss six open problems: application integration, platform differences, non-functional properties, generalization, attribution of gains, as well as cost and maintenance.

##### **Autonomous Multireference Quantum Chemistry with Artificial Intelligence Agents**
2611.29902v1 by Younghwan Kim, Ming-Hsiu Hsieh, Ankit Mathanker, Arpan Choudhury, Joshua Zhou, Qiaohong Wang, Matthew R. Hermes, Laura Gagliardi

Accurately describing chemical reactions that involve strongly correlated electrons remains a major challenge in computational chemistry. Multireference electronic structure methods can address this challenge, but their application requires expert decisions that are di!cult to automate, particularly when the electronic structure changes as chemical bonds form and break. Here we introduce CASPER, an autonomously agentic workflow that performs multireference calculations along chemical reaction pathways. CASPER combines chemical knowledge, electronic structure calculations, and iterative assessment to identify chemically meaningful active spaces and maintain their consistency as bonds form and break. We demonstrate its capabilities on five transition-metal reactions, for which CASPER generates consistent multireference potential energy profiles with minimal human intervention. Importantly, in two reactions, CASPER identifies critical electronic-structure problems that an independent Claude session fails to recognize without the structured workflow. These include an electronic-state crossing and the exclusion of an orbital involved in bond breaking. Our results show the importance of combining language models with chemical knowledge and independent assessment of computational results. Beyond automating calculations, such agents have the potential to help researchers better understand the underlying physics and chemistry and open new opportunities for investigating complex chemical reactivity and generating high-quality quantum chemical data at scale.

Agentic pipelines that combine graph detectors, deterministic rules, retrieval-augmented generation and an LLM judge are being proposed to monitor illicit cryptocurrency flows and to write regulation-citing justifications for analysts. They are evaluated on end-to-end accuracy or on the quality of those justifications, and neither shows which component made a decision. We audit such a pipeline on the Elliptic++ Bitcoin dataset with decision-source attribution, a trace-level protocol with three layers: per-case actual causation, interventional Shapley credit, and mediation of upstream risk scores. A deterministic wallet-score override carries about 94% of the credit for flags, and a verification gate cancels most of the judge's own flags, which fall on licit transactions. Testing the judge alone over 76 evidence bundles, four backbones and two prompts, we find that it follows an injected risk score on the anonymous transaction features it is given, although stronger models decide correctly when the facts are explicit; that a context too short for the system prompt leaves it with no verdict at all; and that it cites regulatory clauses that do not apply, which a substring-based citation check accepts. Hiding the score does not fix the judge, because evidence gathered with the score visible still encodes it. ##### **Into My Arms: Structured reinforcement learning for autonomous transport of objects and humans**
2611.29771v1 by Elle Miller, Reem Almheiri, Takuma Tsukakoshi, Ben Sanati, Tamon Miyake, Yushi Wang, Namiko Saito, Shigeki Sugano, Tetsuya Ogata, Rika Antonova, Oisin Mac Aodha, Sethu Vijayakumar

Whole-body humanoid manipulation remains bottlenecked by high-dimensional state-action spaces, contact-rich environments, and long task horizons. This trilemma is a challenge that state-of-the-art reinforcement learning (RL) methods are yet to resolve reliably. We address it with three contributions that add just enough structure to guide exploration: Sparse Interaction Edges (SIE) for whole-body manipulation, Interaction Gated Transport (IGT) for sustained transport over long horizons, and a Hinted Object Path (HOP) that resolves collision and search complexity bottlenecks. Across all three, the policy remains free to discover its own manipulation strategy. The method generalises from rigid object to human transport. We trace the difference to the unit of sender identity: Elliptic2 senders are entity clusters that recur across many labeled subgraphs, whereas Elliptic++ senders are individual addresses, and fewer than half of test transactions have a sending address seen in training. Musical style, a global attribute that functions throughout a whole piece, is a lever widely used by users in modern large music generation models. Yet how style is represented inside these models, and which components drive it, remains largely unexplored. In language models, circuit analysis traces a representation to a sparse sub-network of a few key components, namely attention heads and MLP neurons, and has been used successfully to explain and control global attributes such as emotion. In this work, we use circuit analysis to study the mechanisms behind musical style. Based on YuE2, we build a dataset that pairs the same lyrics with five styles and design a three-step framework. First, stage localization shows that the style forms mainly in the score-planning stage. Second, static observation across the positions of this stage further reveals that style is not a fixed vector but a dynamic representation across tokens. Third, we therefore merge the activation differences across positions into one direction per style, and trace the neurons and attention heads that drive this direction into a sparse circuit. Without any style text, steering this circuit successfully transfers one piece into the target style, matching and even exceeding the style prompt. ##### **Gaussian Belief Propagation and Periodic Jacobi Matrices on Trees: Spectral Curve, Antibound Poles, and Fixed Points**
2611.29381v1 by Yusuke Watanabe

Each energy strictly between it and the spectral bottom has at least two fixed points with positive definite local beliefs. Counterfactual replacements of a probe-defined entity component at a single prompt position redirect downstream bridge entities and the final answer. Conversely, replacements from queries that reach the same entity through different preceding relations, preserving the remaining relation suffix, retain the original answer in over 99.5\% of tested cases. These results distinguish a training-dependent schedule for factual recall from the causal role of retrieved-entity representations in subsequent composition.

摘要：

##### **Multimodal LLMs Know the Game but Assume the Real World**
2611.28745v1 by Jianxin Gao, Bohan Chen, Runze Li, Qi Zhang

Multimodal large language models can state the rules of a video game, but do they apply them when they only see the game? We introduce RuleCraft, 1,240 image questions over 155 Minecraft rules with outcomes verified in the game engine, each asked in text, on the image with the game named, and on the bare image. Six widely used models know the rules and recognize the game, yet answer the bare image by real-world physics: naming the game raises their accuracy on rules where the game departs from reality by 8.2-19.8 points on a main set and by 33.8-52.7 on a hard stress-test set. Their errors pick the real-world outcome, the gap is largest where the game leaves a scene unchanged, and reasoning traces state the game's rule, then answer by reality. On the main set, naming the game mostly swaps the real-world default for the game's. The pattern holds with reasoning, recurs in two more games, and persists on questions the models themselves take to be about the game; one neutral sentence asking for the rules of the world shown recovers much of the gap. Evaluations that name the game measure knowledge of its rules, not world selection.

摘要：

##### **PopMelodyGen: A Copyright-Aware Platform for Controllable Pop-Melody Generation**
2611.28686v1 by Di Lu, Xinran Zhang, Qingwen Zhou, Yan Gao, Yuan Zhang, Xiaoqing Wang, Yuming Sun, Xiaobing Li, Maosong Sun

Large-scale redistributable symbolic pop-melody data remain scarce due to copyright restrictions, creating a bottleneck for both practical deployment and reproducible research in music generation. We present PopMelodyGen, a copyright-aware platform for controllable pop-melody generation that combines rule-based synthesis with neural generation. Our approach first uses an expert synthesizer to produce large-scale labeled synthetic melodies from explicit musical knowledge and templates, providing scalable pretraining data with auditable provenance. A neural model is then pretrained on this corpus and adapted with a small licensed real-pop corpus to improve melodic naturalness while preserving controllability. The platform supports interactive melody creation with accompaniment playback and export for creators, as well as batch construction of annotated symbolic datasets for researchers. PopMelodyGen demonstrates a practical training and deployment paradigm for creative AI when large-scale reusable real-world data are limited.

摘要：

##### **Bridging Teacher-Student Capability Gaps with Recurrent Computation**
2611.28603v1 by Zeyi Huang, Jiuxiang Gu, Eli Shechtman, Yong Jae Lee, Zongze Wu

Knowledge distillation transfers capabilities from large language models to smaller students, yet stronger teachers do not always yield better students. Recent studies report diminishing or even negative returns from teacher scaling. We hypothesize that these limitations arise in part from insufficient student capacity to absorb the teacher's knowledge. We therefore investigate loop and recurrent computation as a way to expand the student's effective capacity by modifying its computation graph while keeping the backbone parameter count fixed. Under teacher-forced KD during pretraining, weight-shared looping improves performance but retains teacher-size saturation, whereas cross-token recurrence avoids the observed reversal with larger teachers. In mathematical post-training under both teacher-forced KD and on-policy distillation (OPD), recurrent students scale better with increasing teacher size than looped and vanilla LLMs do. Crucially, we show that a pretrained LLM can be readily fine-tuned into a looped or recurrent architecture jointly with OPD, without repeating pretraining. Our recurrent Qwen model improves with larger teachers and consistently outperforms looped and vanilla LLMs. This suggests a new post-training paradigm that integrates architectural conversion into distillation, avoiding a separate conversion stage while substantially improving student performance and maintaining near-vanilla inference latency.

摘要：

##### **CLASP: Deciding Structure and Claims Before Decoding for Whole-Slide Pathology Report Generation**
2611.28576v1 by Ziaullah Khan, Rashadul Islam Sumon, Md Ariful Islam Mozumder, Duc-Son Pham, Moon-Il Joo,  Hee-Cheol

Whole-slide pathology report generation has advanced through stronger slide encoders, retrieved knowledge, and, recently, slide-level multimodal LLMs. However, these generators leave every report-level choice to the decoder: how long the report is, how it is organised, and which findings it states and where. On the standard PathText-BRCA benchmark, we find that these choices dominate n-gram overlap, as reports from the query's hospital selected without learning approach the state of the art, while decoders under-generate and fall back to the most frequent findings. We present CLASP, a decide-then-decode generator that makes these choices explicitly, once per slide. Before decoding, an Exemplar Structure Prior (ESP) takes the sentence plan and length from reports of image-similar cases, a Calibrated Claim Decision (CCD) selects the findings from calibrated slide posteriors, and a Claim-to-Sentence Placement (CSP) assigns each finding to a planned sentence. A Plan-Conditioned Decoder (PCD), refined by structure-anchored sequence training, then writes the report under these decisions. With 35M parameters, CLASP outperforms retrained report generators and slide-level LLMs of up to 7B parameters, matches the strongest published results, and generalises to lung cancer from unseen hospitals. Its decisions change what the reports state, not only their length, transfer to an LLM decoder, and can withhold what H&E cannot show: withholding receptor status leaves 23% fewer contradictions with clinical records than the same network without decisions.

摘要：

##### **MACE: Memory-Augmented Crop-Disease Evaluation of Frozen Vision-Language Models**
2611.28573v1 by Tirtho Roy, Shreyan Ganguly, Muhammad Arbab Arshad, Nikhil Bhattacharya, Soumik Sarkar

In plant phenotyping, crop disease diagnosis has traditionally relied on expert assessment of visible symptoms and on models tailored to specific crops or diseases, and both are difficult to scale across field conditions. Recent methods provide frozen vision-language models with source-grounded knowledge bases that summarize the typical symptoms of each disease but describe little of how symptoms vary across individual cases. We introduce MACE (Memory-Augmented Crop-Disease Evaluation), a training-free method that supplements the knowledge base with a memory of symptom descriptions paired with known disease labels. MACE matches a description of the query image against this memory and retrieves only text descriptions and disease labels, so the query is the only image that the diagnosis model sees. Across ten crops, MACE with Claude Sonnet 5.5 improved accuracy over the knowledge-base baseline from 42.8% to 62.2% in distribution and from 50.7% to 57.0% on held-out image collections. Retrieved disease labels accounted for most of the gain, and adding the corresponding text descriptions improved in-distribution accuracy by about six percentage points.

摘要：

##### **General-purpose language models are neurosymbolic reasoners able to annotate proteins from sequence**
2611.28529v1 by Christopher J. Mungall, Marcin P. Joachimiak

Determining the function of genes across the tree of life is one of the major challenges in biology. Generative AI approaches to this problem generally fall into two categories: language models trained on biological sequences that are able to predict structure and function from signals in those sequences; and generalist language models trained on text, that are able to reason about biological function symbolically. A default operating assumption is that protein language models do not perform well on symbolic tasks, and that generalist language models do not perform well on sequence tasks. However, a recent report showed that frontier generalist models perform surprisingly well on next-residue prediction tasks. We set out to explore whether these capabilities extended to protein annotation.
  We assigned different frontier generalist models a variety of protein annotation tasks, including predicting the presence and coordinates of Pfam domains, loss of functional catalytic residues, and predicting structure and activity. On all annotation tasks, the frontier models performed well above chance; we did not benchmark them against specialised tools. The models also make characteristic hallucination errors, and they state them as confidently as their correct calls.
  Though untested against specialised methods, the nascent ability of frontier models to perform protein annotation from sequence alone is intriguing. The ability to reason over sequence, structure, and symbols in a single model allows for a neurosymbolic approach where a combination of sequence, experimental data and knowledge from the literature can be holistically combined to improve on de novo functional annotation of the protein universe.
  Prompts, test sets, model outputs and code: https://github.com/ai4curation/fable-sequence-to-function. Summary of results: https://ai4curation.io/fable-sequence-to-function/

摘要：

##### **SupportChain: Trace-Calibrated Aggregation of Quote-Grounded Reasoning Chains for Multi-Hop RAG with Small Readers**
2611.28519v1 by Zhexi Gu

Strong results in multi-hop retrieval-augmented question answering come from graph indexes read by models of 70B parameters or more, and building such an index calls a large language model over the whole corpus. We present SupportChain, which splits a question into hops, binds each hop's answer to a sentence quoted from a retrieved passage, estimates how reliable each execution is from its own trace, and merges several executions by the support of their answers, the pooled reliability of the executions that agree, stopping once support is high enough. With a 9B reader under the HippoRAG~2 protocol, SupportChain reaches 63.0 F1 on MuSiQue, above the best earlier result of 59.2, which used a 20B reader. With the same reader and embedder, it is significantly better than HippoRAG~2 on all five datasets, by up to 17.2 F1, and it builds no index. Merging by support is significantly better than majority voting, and than letting the reader choose among the candidates, on three of the five datasets and level with both on the other two. The reliability model needs only 100 answered training questions.

摘要：

##### **Automated Safety Analysis for Agentic Workflows**
2611.28469v1 by Yanju Chen, Ying Li, Bosi Zhang, Hanzhi Liu, Hongbo Wen, Yufei Ding, Yuan Tian, Yu Feng

LLM applications are increasingly built as agentic workflows: programs that combine model calls, external tools, and control logic to act autonomously on a user's behalf. Because these workflows execute real-world actions, logical flaws in how a workflow is wired can cause severe harm in deployed systems. Unlike prompt injections or malformed API calls, these workflow bugs stem from defects in the workflow's design, yet no existing tool can statically validate workflow logic across frameworks prior to deployment. We present Apsis, a static safety analyzer that detects workflow bugs before deployment. To operate across frameworks, Apsis lifts framework-specific code into a common intermediate representation (IR). This IR models a workflow as a semantic graph of steps, data dependencies, and shared state, while annotating each component with behavioral properties such as whether an input is mandatory or how a shared channel merges concurrent writes. Building on this representation, we introduce a bug description language that formalizes recurring classes of agentic failures as declarative patterns over the IR. By matching these patterns against the IR, Apsis detects structural and behavioral flaws and pinpoints the exact step responsible for each bug. To evaluate Apsis, we construct, to our knowledge, the first corpus of workflow bugs, distilled from real-world reports across 13 widely used frameworks. On a reproducible benchmark suite drawn from this corpus, Apsis detects workflow bugs with 91% precision and 84% recall, and uncovers 36 previously unknown bugs in real-world workflows.

摘要：

##### **Muted Distillation: Sealing Reasoning from Teacher Unlocks Token-Efficient Agents**
2611.28445v1 by Yuwei Zhang, Ian Miao, Wentao Zhang, Chen Qu, Bill Bi, Jingbo Shang, Donna Schut

On-policy distillation (OPD) is commonly adopted as one of the components in post-training of large language models (LLMs), but in multi-turn agentic tasks we find that the student model increases their reasoning lengths after training. Our empirical analysis shows that the reasoning token lengths is affected by how much the student can distract the teacher's scores. We introduce Muted Distillation (MD), a simple variant of OPD that hides the student's reasoning from the teacher, so the teacher judges what the student does without the interference of student's reasonings. MD takes a few lines of code and adds no extra data or hyperparameters. On the knowledge-work benchmarks OfficeQA Pro and SpreadsheetBench, it reduces output tokens per episode by 57\% and 53\% compared with standard OPD, making the student more concise than it was before training. At the same time, it matches OPD's accuracy on OfficeQA Pro and improves pass@1 by 4.8 points on SpreadsheetBench.

摘要：

##### **SMAD: A Semantics-Guided Multi-Agent Framework for Unsupervised Time-Series Anomaly Detection**
2611.28348v1 by Qinghua Liu, Junpeng Wang, Mingzhi Hu, Menghai Pan, Shubham Jain, Yuzhong Chen, Yan Zheng, Yiwei Cai, John Paparrizos

Time-series anomaly detection (TSAD) lacks a one-size-fits-all detector for heterogeneous data and anomaly types. Existing automation is limited by implicit objectives, potentially unreliable historical knowledge, or high computational costs. More fundamentally, it selects how to detect anomalies without explicitly defining what to detect. We propose SMAD, an LLM-based multi-agent framework that translates anomaly intent into executable, testable detection pipelines. Rather than selecting detectors against an implicit target, SMAD establishes a natural-language semantic contract grounded in measurable time-series properties, specifying target event patterns, expected score responses, and validation questions. This contract governs both detector construction and evaluation: LLM agents propose pipelines, while contract-matched synthetic interventions provide label-free evidence from the target series to assess semantic coverage and guide iterative self-refinement. A semantics-aware ensemble integrates validated candidates and attributes each alert to contributing detectors and supporting evidence. On univariate and multivariate TSAD benchmarks, SMAD improves detection performance over the strongest baseline by 11.5% and 9.6%, respectively, while outperforming general-purpose coding agents at 2-5x lower language-model token cost.

摘要：

##### **GERF: A Token-Efficient Multimodal Retrieval Framework for Schema-Guided Scientific Information Extraction**
2611.28295v1 by Yiyu Qian, Xing Wu

Scientific information extraction (IE) is a fundamental component of AI for Science, transforming experimental knowledge embedded in scientific literature into structured and traceable data for evidence synthesis, scientific database construction, and data-driven discovery. However, critical experimental details are often distributed across text, tables, figures, captions, and surrounding discussion, making them difficult to recover using conventional text-only extraction. Existing LLM-based and multimodal RAG pipelines typically rely on full-document context or coarse page-level retrieval, introducing substantial irrelevant evidence and high textual and visual token costs. We propose GERF, a Graph-based Evidence Retrieval Framework for token-efficient, schema-guided multimodal scientific IE. GERF represents each article as a heterogeneous evidence graph that connects schema fields, text chunks, figures, and figure-derived content through semantic and structural relations. Using schema fields as retrieval anchors, GERF combines direct semantic retrieval, Random Walk with Restart, and null-aware evidence expansion to construct compact, field-specific multimodal evidence packages. Visual interpretation is applied only to graph-selected figures, avoiding indiscriminate processing of the entire document. We evaluate GERF from two complementary perspectives: evidence retrieval quality and pipeline-level input-token consumption. On the expert-annotated SOFC-Exp corpus, GERF retrieves gold-supported evidence for 94.55% of the evaluated field instances, providing broad field-level evidence coverage for downstream extraction. In the token comparison, GERF reduces average input-token usage by 73.40% relative to full-context processing and uses fewer tokens than the evaluated page-level pipelines.
