#### Abstracts
##### **Can LLM Judges Catch Unsafe Clinical Actions? A Fault-Injection Study**
2611.33012v1 by Neda Ghafouri

As clinical AI agents begin to interact with electronic health records (EHRs), large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to evaluate their actions. But how reliably can these judges recognize clinically dangerous mistakes, especially when an agent appears to have completed its task successfully? We investigate this question using MedAgentBench by introducing controlled clinical errors into verified agent trajectories. The experiments include 416 faulty trajectories, eight error categories, and six open-weight LLM judges. By comparing each faulty trajectory with its correct counterpart, we measure whether judges genuinely recognize errors rather than simply flagging more actions as unsafe. The findings show that access to clinical evidence significantly improves judge reliability. When shown only the final answer, judges cannot distinguish errors that leave it unchanged, while flagging 56% of correct trajectories. With full trajectory information, the ability to distinguish unsafe actions from correct ones reaches a score of 0.75, and providing actual EHR responses improves discrimination by 0.39. Surprisingly, including the agent's reasoning does not improve overall performance and reduces detection of incorrectly reported clinical values by 0.18, which remain the hardest errors to detect. MedGemma performed worse than the general-purpose model and was the only judge to miss potentially dangerous medication overdoses. We also find that the benchmark's original grader accepts all 38 trajectories with an intentionally skipped laboratory check, while combining LLM judges with a rule-based checker informed by the full EHR record detects 97% of injected faults.

摘要：

##### **Embodied AI Fails the Mirror Test: How a Simple Mirror Breaks Robot Policies**
2611.33010v1 by Xuanchen Liu, Hejun Zhang, Boxuan Dong, Ying Wu, Heshuai Li, Wen Gao, Yonghong Tian, Weihao Yu

Can a simple mirror expose the limitations of today's robotic manipulation policies? Inspired by the mirror self-recognition test in animal cognition, we investigate whether robot policies can reliably manipulate physical objects in scenes containing their reflections. We introduce RoboDojo-Mirror, a benchmark built on RoboDojo that preserves task objectives, success criteria, and policy checkpoints while comparing a mirror-free baseline with single-mirror, dual-mirror, and mirrored-tabletop conditions. Across five policies spanning Vision-Language-Action models, World Action Models, and agentic policies, every mirror condition reduces aggregate success rates: averaged over the three conditions, the drop ranges from 10.8 to 56.7 percentage points across policies, and it reaches 62.5 points on the mirrored tabletop. Because a mirror is also a physical obstacle, we compare it against geometrically matched non-reflective panels; 50--88\% of mirror-specific failures occur without recorded robot--panel contact, so direct collisions alone do not explain the degradation. Trajectory and contact analyses instead reveal recurring behaviors consistent with phantom targeting of reflected objects and spatial mislocalization of real ones. Targeted diagnostics further characterize grasping near reflections, policy-dependent pushing progress, and physical-target interaction under main-camera occlusion. Real-robot experiments with $π_{0.5}$ and FastWAM also show lower overall success rates under the upright-mirror and mirrored-tabletop conditions. These findings reveal a shared vulnerability to reflective environments across diverse robot policies and motivate reflection-aware evaluation of embodied intelligence. Code is available at https://github.com/iPPLab/robodojo-mirror.

摘要：

##### **On Potentiality and Actuality Of Foundation Models**
2611.32991v1 by Fabian Gröger, Shuo Wen, Maria Brbić

Foundation models are built through large-scale pretraining, often followed by post-training, and are then adapted to specific tasks and behaviors. But, does the adaptation add truly new capabilities, or the base model already holds it? We decompose the update induced by different adaptations into a component shared across all inputs and a residual that varies with the input. We find that the shared component suffices for most of the adaptation gain and can be learned directly: a shift adapter that learns a constant vector per layer, identical for every input, reproduces the behavior of LoRA and full fine-tuning. We call this the potentiality principle of foundation models: the base model already contains the capacity needed for task-specific expertise. To adapt to a new task, the model just needs to be actualized, i.e., pushed in a certain direction via a constant, input-independent shift. We show the implications of this principle on parameter-efficient adaptation, steering, and convergence across models, and how it explains the neural thickets phenomenon.

摘要：

##### **ASCENT: A Benchmark for Evaluating and Advancing Stepwise Diagnostic Reasoning in Large Language Models on Common Clinical Scenarios**
2611.32734v1 by Yera Choi, Yeong Hwa Kim, Jaedeok Lee, Taekang Kim, Sangdoo Yun, Seong-Eun Moon

Large language models (LLMs) excel at medical question answering yet are rarely evaluated on the stepwise diagnostic reasoning that defines real clinical workflows, where impressions are revised as information accumulates. We build Annotated Stepwise Clinical rEasoning for NaturalisTic Diagnosis (ASCENT), a clinician-annotated benchmark and training resource of 3,078 stepwise problems derived from MedQA-USMLE that decomposes each vignette into EMR-aligned steps (Findings, Impression, supporting Rationale), enabling evaluation of intermediate reasoning under incomplete information.
  Experiments and training with ASCENT revealed insights into how current LLMs handle stepwise diagnostic reasoning. Even strong reasoning models that perform well on MedQA-USMLE leave substantial headroom on ASCENT, and general-purpose frontier models trail further, exposing a persistent gap between fully informed and stepwise diagnosis. Fine-tuning Qwen2.5-7B and 32B on ASCENT yields measurable F1 gains over both pre-trained and HuatuoGPT-o1 CoT-trained baselines, with gains driven primarily by precision. Complementary robustness analyses (counterfactual perturbation, format-vs-content control, judge agreement, and rollout) further show that ASCENT-fine-tuned models rely on the diagnostic content of prior impressions rather than imitating their output format, while error propagation under rollout remains a key challenge for clinical deployment.

摘要：

##### **Can AI Design a Better nnU-Net? A Hypothesis-Driven Architecture Discovery Loop from Failures to Design Rules**
2611.32384v1 by Songtao Jiang, Yuan Wang, Fucheng Niu, Hualiang Wang, Jian Wu, Zuozhu Liu

Neural architecture search and LLM-driven evolution often reduce evaluation to a scalar fitness score, discarding failures that could explain why designs succeed or fail. We present a hypothesis-driven architecture discovery loop in which LLM agents diagnose structured error patterns, formulate falsifiable hypotheses, test counterfactual controls under a frozen nnU-Net pipeline, and distill validated mechanisms into executable trigger-to-action rules. Across eight search waves, the loop identifies two principles for 3D medical image segmentation. First, reallocating computation from high-resolution decoders to the mid-encoder preserves thin-anatomy boundaries while reducing cost. Second, support-confined region contrast addresses errors between nested anatomical compartments, with anatomy-dependent triggers selecting when the rule applies. These principles produce LeanNeXt (0.78 MACs and 0.92 training-step time relative to nnU-Net; +1.63 Dice points on four-fold KiTS23 cross-validation) and trigger-routed LeanNeXt+. The latter improves all 15 evaluated CT/MRI settings over nnU-Net and exceeds the 3.3 larger ResEnc-M on 12 of 15 benchmarks at 0.30 its parameter count. Relative to random sampling, one-shot LLM generation, and score-selected agent search, the loop shows how failure analysis can yield reusable design rules across modalities.

摘要：

##### **Part-Set Prototypes for Explainable Mountain Gazelles Sex Classification**
2611.32343v1 by Tali Boneh Shitrit, Amir Kedem, Ilan Shimshoni, Anna Zamansky

Self-explainable visual models for fine-grained recognition are often hindered by rigid geometric constraints that cannot capture diverse semantic shapes in unconstrained "in-the-wild" imagery. Real-world camera-trap data introduces challenges, including non-optimal poses, lighting variations, occlusions, animal camouflage, and environmental shortcuts. To address this, we introduce set prototypes, a general architecture where both part cardinality and unconstrained spatial layouts are mined directly from data. Our joint-matching formulation places parts simultaneously on a frozen DINOv3 token grid via truncated Sinkhorn competition, allowing unsupported parts to abstain without spatial collapse. We evaluate our approach on Mountain Gazelle sex classification, a camera-trap benchmark, where our model achieves 0.878 macro-F1--outperforming prior architectures and at par with an unconstrained linear probe. The strongest prototypes localize to distinctive biological areas such as horns, neck and body, allowing ecologists to visually verify decisions before trusting the metric. This formulation generalizes naturally to diverse ecological monitoring challenges beyond a single species.

摘要：

##### **When Exact Token Importance Is Not Enough: Limits of Top-K Selection in LLM Attention**
2611.32324v1 by Irmaan Mirzanejad

Long-context language models often retain only a limited set of past token positions for an attention query. A common strategy scores positions individually and keeps the highest-ranked K. Yet attention weights are renormalized over the chosen set: two individually important positions need not form the best pair. We ask whether independent ranking suffices even when each importance score measures the exact downstream change in next-token predictions caused by deleting that position alone. It does not. A four-position construction makes the ranked pair incur 133.2 times the Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence of the optimal pair, and a parametric family proves that this multiplicative gap is unbounded. An exact exchange identity and preference reversals for every K >= 2 explain the interaction. In exhaustive frozen-model audits of SmolLM2, Gemma, and Qwen, exact-singleton Top-4 leaves at least 10% relative improvement within a fully enumerated 24-position candidate pool in 55/60, 49/60, and 60/60 cases, respectively. SwapCert, a same-budget one-swap audit, detects 157/164 such cases and returns directly checkable improving supports. A KL-margin certificate relates support fidelity to next-token stability; larger-budget, multi-layer cached-decoding experiments measure predictive fidelity at K = 128 and 256. These results establish joint support quality as an essential object of analysis for sparse attention and motivate downstream-aware support selection under inference constraints.

摘要：

##### **Refining and Aligning Clinical Reasoning with Health Guidelines via Formal Constraints**
2611.32247v1 by Xiaoyang Fan, Yufan Cai, Zhe Hou, Jin Song Dong

Clinical reasoning from incomplete or conflicting patient evidence must account for low-probability but high-risk concerns while remaining grounded in health guidelines. We introduce VerClin, an explainable framework that extracts guideline conditions, exclusions, recommendations, and priorities into executable knowledge and represents patient conversations as scoped cases. A deterministic four-valued reasoner produces traceable guideline matches while preserving uncertainty and enforcing exclusions. Multiple specialty agents then screen matched guidelines for case applicability, and a consultation model synthesizes accepted guidance through specialty recommendations and a sentence-preserving formal-constraint channel. We present accepted guidance in ascending order of the sum of its priority and trigger-strength ranks, which can surface high-risk guidance with weaker triggers. VerClin outperforms direct generation and Top-5 RAG on HealthBench Hard and HealthBench Professional. On the LLMEval-Med subset, it outperforms direct generation and ties Top-5 RAG. Component analyses further examine semantic closure, example-guided refinement, specialty screening, and constraint-conditioned delivery.

摘要：

##### **Blaming the Machine, Billing the Victim? How Mock Jurors Reassign Fault from Generative AI Systems and Respond to Liability Cliffs in Tort**
2611.32188v1 by Tin Trung Nguyen, Jiannan Xu, Bao Kham Chau, Hal Daumé, Zubin Jelveh

Generative AI complicates tort liability attribution when people blame a generative AI system that cannot itself bear legally relevant fault. We investigate where this blame goes when fault must be allocated among legal persons, and whether American discontinuous apportionment doctrines influence mock jurors' factual findings of fault. Such doctrines include Contributory negligence, Joint and several liability, and their 50% or 51% variations, all of which have a fault-percentage threshold or a "cliff" where the victim-entitled compensation (or a defendant's liability) jumps to zero (or to all compensatory damages). Our pre-registered human subjects experiment (N=199 Prolific participants) shows that as many U.S. mock jurors (36%) blame the AI system itself, the AI blame often "leaks" to the victim or to the AI user (in 27% to 73% of our AI-blaming records, depending on how leakage is calculated). Although mock jurors rarely change their blame assignment from one side of the liability cliff to the other, these participants do not acknowledge the legal doctrine or its monetary consequence when explaining their changes. Our empirical results illustrate how a narrow doctrinal exception in tort, carving out "joint and several liability" for those deploying or using Generative AI without reasonable care, might mitigate the impact of blame leakage on victims' deserved compensation without introducing significant jury nullification risks.

摘要：

##### **Benchmarking Protein Structure Tokenizers as Generation Targets for Language Models**
2611.32168v1 by Yong Liu, Zhanpeng Shi, Peicong Lin, Zhenyi Zhong, Yizhou Dang, Wendi Wu, Haoling Li, Jiayu Gu, Duo An, Shuangjia Zheng

Discrete structure tokenizers are the standard way to give a language model access to protein three-dimensional structure, yet they are selected on how accurately they can be read, their reconstruction fidelity, while the property that governs generation is how well a model can write them. We measure this property, writability, directly: holding the base model, data, recipe and compute fixed, we swap only the tokenizer and train the same 9B model to emit structure tokens residue by residue, over eight tokenizers, four compute budgets and three evaluation sets (96 cells). At 2B tokens final writability runs from TM 0.662 down to 0.378 under identical conditions, with five of the eight arms clearing the TM 0.5 mark at which a fold is usually called correct and three falling short of it. Neither codebook size nor reconstruction ceiling explains where an arm lands; the best and worst both use 8192 codewords. What does explain the spread is local structure preservation of the code space. In DPLM-2 a one-bit codeword error costs 0.019 TM where a random codeword costs 0.347, and the trained model's own errors are correspondingly local, at 0.51 of the random Hamming expectation; FoldToken4 fails both tests at the same codebook size and is 0.13 TM less writable. Three controls discipline how such a benchmark may be read. A same-recipe rerun puts the noise floor at $σ=0.0067$ TM, which collapses an apparent six-way ranking into four tiers. A reference-free geometry panel turns out to be nearly orthogonal to TM (Pearson $-0.58$), with the worst arm by TM producing the most physically valid backbones. And two pre-registered interventions are null for a reason the mechanism measurements reveal: the quantity they target saturates at 1.5B tokens while TM is still rising. All 131 evaluation cells, including raw generated token streams, are released.

摘要：

##### **The algorithmic behavioral sink: Modeling the transition to irreversible demographic collapse**
2611.32084v1 by Feng Yu

In 2025, the vast majority of major global economies reported a Total Fertility Rate below the replacement level of 2.1. This synchronized collapse has persisted despite extensive pro-natalist financial interventions, revealing a structural limitation in conventional linear demographic models. This paper proposes that the ubiquity of mobile internet and Artificial Intelligence acts as a synthetic mating disruptor, precipitating a non-linear phase transition in human pair-bonding dynamics. Treating the marriage market as a complex system governed by thermodynamic constraints, we present a Fermi-Dirac selection model, using national income distributions as the input spectrum and fitted with Japanese longitudinal data to illustrate how digital hyper-reality generates an increasingly prohibitive Marriage Energy Barrier. Our model quantifies a Tech Factor that decouples female mating expectations from local economic realities during initial courtship, a distortion we predict will be severely exacerbated as AI evolves into an emotional substitute. Projections indicate that this substitution effect is accelerating; without intervention, the world post 2040 risks entering an irreversible demographic contraction, as the sudden surge in TFR required for recovery is biologically impossible. Ultimately, this framework explains why traditional financial subsidies fail to reverse falling birth rates and suggests that digital convenience may represent a behavioral Great Filter that erodes the imperative for species persistence.

摘要：

##### **Explainable Deep Learning for Malaria Parasite Detection and Species Differentiation in Thick Blood Smear Images: A Pilot Feasibility Study**
2611.31855v1 by Haimanot Bitew, Ayisheshim Almaw, Habtamu Zegeye, Demeke Alene

Species-level differentiation of Plasmodium falciparum (PF) and P. vivax (PV) determines treatment, yet deep learning detectors rarely test whether their explanations are reliable. In this pilot feasibility study of TRACE-Mal, a research prototype combining YOLOv8n-seg detection and a six-model soft-voting ensemble, we examined whether agreement among three post-hoc explanation methods (Grad-CAM, LIME and SHAP) is associated with the correctness of species calls. the study analysed 2,847 parasite crops from 250 thick smears within publicly available Lacuna Malaria Datasets. The model achieved an mAP50 score of 0.79 and the ensemble had a macro F1 of 0.84 (PF recall 0.86; PV recall 0.74). Unadjusted analyses of crops revealed that predictions explained by greather than or equal to 2 methods were more accurate (86.2% vs 78.9%; difference 7.3 percentage points; 95% CI 3.5-11.1) compared to those explained by less than 2 methods. As the number of explanatory methods increased so did prediction accuracy (from 73.8% for no methods to 89.5% for all three methods). When performing a cluster-bootstrap analysis of 60 test images, again there was a positive consensus difference (7.3 percentage points; 95% CI 2.9-11.8). Further, incorporating explanation information into a model's prediction confidence enhanced its error differentiation ability (AUROC 0.024; 95% CI 0.008-0.041). PF and PV parasite crops have distinct morphologies. Although based on one unvalidated public data source, these findings demonstrate proof-of-concept and should not be taken as a measure of diagnostic capability.

摘要：

##### **DELTA: Diagnosing Exploration Lineages via Twin-path Alignment - What Can Contrasting Design Paths Reveal About Microarchitectural Behavior?**
2611.31767v1 by Kaushal Mhapsekar, Darsh Asher, Bita Aslrousta, Amna Hassan, Debayan Sengupta, Azam Ghanbari, Samira Mirbagher Ajorpaz

Architects have two mature instruments for performance work. Top-Down analysis tells them where a design loses its pipeline slots, and design-space exploration (DSE) tells them which configuration scores best. Neither explains why a search stops improving: why a backend-bound design rejects a prefetcher that the bottleneck label recommends, or why a capacity increase that helped in one configuration hurts in the next. This paper argues that the evidence needed to answer such questions is already produced during exploration and then discarded. Every parent-child edge in an exploration tree is a controlled intervention: one knob changes, the rest of the design is held fixed, and a cycle-level simulator measures the outcome. When the same edit helps under one parent and hurts under another, aligning the two parents localizes the interaction that decides its sign.
  We present DELTA, a simulator-grounded root-cause analysis tool that builds a Design Memory of such interventions during exploration and lets an architect query it afterwards. Each record stores the full parent configuration, the single applied edit, the measured and predicted IPC, a Top-Down breakdown from StallLens, our gem5 implementation of Top-Down, and its lineage. Unlike evolutionary LLM search, which keeps only high-scoring programs, DELTA keeps failed edges as evidence. The same memory also makes exploration efficient: on four SPEC CPU2017 workloads, the full system raises IPC by 55.5% in 22 simulated designs for $1.56 of model cost, predicts the IPC of its own proposals to within 3.3%, and keeps improving after a bottleneck-guided search stalls. A root-cause query over the recorded history led us to a usefulness-throttled variant of the Best-Offset prefetcher that improves IPC by 1.37% over the original in matched configurations.

摘要：

##### **Rapid Exploratory Maps and their potential role in supporting evidence-to-policy dialogues and processes**
2611.31693v1 by Gareth J. Hollands, Ian Shemilt, James Thomas

Producing useful evidence syntheses requires clearly formulated research questions linked to an understanding of their optimal scope. When policymakers (or other end users) commission or steer the production of systematic reviews or other evidence syntheses to fulfil a demand for evidence, these parameters are rarely specified sufficiently for production to proceed. Instead, these details are typically developed via a collaborative dialogic process involving evidence producers and users to ensure the resulting synthesis will meet users' needs. This process is informed by scoping and (more-or-less formally) mapping the existing evidence base, which can take many weeks or months, slowed by applying mostly manual processes to identify and characterise the evidence. Recent developments in artificial intelligence (AI) technologies have opened up new opportunities for more rapid, iterative, and responsive mapping of relevant evidence to support such early-stage evidence-to-policy dialogues and processes. In this article, we outline and characterise this emerging approach, termed Rapid Exploratory Maps (REMs). We also present two illustrative case studies drawn from first-hand experiences within a programme of evidence reviews production to inform government policy in England. While this approach could be transformative for the mapping process, its broader potential is as yet largely unassessed, and it confers important challenges and likely risks, as well as benefits. Determining whether REMs could usefully support more efficient ways of working with end users when developing and designing evidence syntheses will require continued development and careful evaluation.

Something changed in the agent, and the agent's history caused the change. Nothing other than a process shared with people explains that change. The change matches one theory of the human process and not that theory's rivals. Psychiatry and its neighbouring fields have an evidence standard for each part, from the diagnostic manuals' rule for induced states, from clinical test scores and from animal models. We translate each standard into a criterion that a machine study can meet. We show how to probe that alternative. Further, we audited forty-three published studies that apply psychiatric terms to a machine or induce a comparable state in one. Automating experiments does not necessarily advance scientific understanding. Research agents must identify meaningful knowledge gaps, determine what available data can resolve, and assess whether their findings advance existing knowledge. We introduce DeepQuest, a knowledge-gap-driven AI scientist framework for Alzheimer's disease research. DeepQuest transforms unresolved questions from domain reviews into traceable, testable hypotheses and prioritizes them through data feasibility screening and groupwise Elo ranking. It couples iterative experimentation with two-level auditing that separates experimental reliability from knowledge contribution relative to two frozen literature corpora. On OASIS-3, DeepQuest produces 42 feasible hypotheses and outperforms the strongest baselines in 14 of 15 evaluator-dimension comparisons across three language-model reviewers. The full framework achieves an audit-assessed scientific finding rate of 95.24%, outperforming both general-purpose executors and domain-specific research agents. This scientific finding rate remains stable across literature corpora and evaluator model families. Representative findings concern neuropsychiatric symptoms and dementia risk, longitudinal amyloid spatial stability, and neocortical tau-associated clinical impairment, with key associations supported in independent ADNI and AIBL cohorts. These results show how aligning knowledge gaps, data feasibility, and evidence auditing can turn automated research into traceable, evidence-supported scientific discovery. This critical review covers four paradigms in turn: how clinical hallucination arises and why static metrics miss it; the agentic hallucination cascade, in which multi-agent and tool-augmented systems propagate and amplify early reasoning errors; process reward modeling as step-level supervision; and neuro-symbolic grounding, in which formal logic engines, ontology reasoners, and knowledge graphs place verifiable constraints on neural output. The occurrence of echo chambers is a well-known phenomenon in social networks. Opinion dynamics models have been used to explain how echo chambers occur through agents updating their opinions. However, social network users also exchange beliefs supporting their opinions. For that purpose, we complement an existing opinion dynamics model with classical belief merge operations. Beliefs and opinions are also maintained coherent guided by values that agents share. Finally, they show that connecting opinions to beliefs and maintaining their coherence increases the number of belief echo chambers, but that they involve a reduced number of agents.

##### **DL101 The Representation Capability of Neural Network Models**
2611.24980v1 by Fernando Berzal

Universal approximation theorems (Cybenko, Funahashi, Hornik & Leshno) provide the mathematical justification for why neural networks are powerful enough to model complex, nonlinear phenomena. Shallow networks can approximate any continuous function on compact sets. Leshno's theorem clarified that non-polynomial activations are necessary for universality, which explains why ReLU, logistic, hyperbolic tangent, and other common activations work (but pure polynomials do not).
  However, approximation theorems provide no efficiency, training, nor generalization guarantees. They do not indicate how many neurons are needed for a given accuracy, whether gradient descent will find the right parameters, or whether networks trained on finite data will generalize well.
  Barron provided quantitative error bounds for particular function classes (approximation rates for functions with bounded Fourier norm), whereas Montúfar was the first to show that expressivity grows exponentially with depth (in terms of the number of linear regions). Deep networks are strictly more expressive than shallow ones with the same number of parameters. Depth is fundamentally more powerful than width, providing a rigorous mathematical explanation for the use of deep learning models.

A trajectory audit replays real admissions checkpoint by checkpoint, lets each system decide with only the evidence released so far, and rates every test, treatment and disposition for its potential harm to the patient and, separately, for clinical validity, against the documented final diagnosis and physician-written disease rubrics yet on the evidence available when the decision was made. An LLM judge scores every decision, and a physician reference standard (triple annotation and senior adjudication of a stratified sample) calibrates the rates. An oversight audit branches each trajectory at its first actionable error and measures, on the same branches, whether corrective feedback repairs the error and whether it introduces new harm. Across 300 cases from a hospital cardiology cohort, MIMIC-IV and the public ASCENT benchmark, with up to seven AI systems per source, 55.2% of hospital trajectories reaching an acceptable final diagnosis passed through a decision with moderate or severe potential harm and 24.8% through a clinically unacceptable one; the harm rates were 76.2% on MIMIC-IV and 39.6% on ASCENT. Structured corrections resolved 73.6% of first errors versus 24.8% after bare rejection, yet new potentially unsafe decisions followed 30.2% of corrections versus 19.7% of rejections. A correct final diagnosis does not certify a safe trajectory, and a successful correction does not certify a safe recovery.

##### **JET: Just-Enough Exploration Tuning for Efficient Tool-Calling Agents in Repository-Level Question Answering**
2611.22539v1 by Yoonho Choi, Taehyeon Han, Seonghyeon Lee

In repository-level question answering (QA), an agent explores a code repository with tools and should answer a question once enough information is gathered. Nevertheless, we find that LLM agents often continue to invoke tools even after the gathered information is already sufficient to answer. We call this phenomenon over-exploration. Moreover, even though existing methods improve task performance through supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on expert trajectories, SFT also propagates the over-exploration in the trajectories to the agent. To mitigate over-exploration, we propose Just-Enough Exploration Tuning (JET), a fine-tuning method that trains the agent to answer once the gathered information is sufficient. JET truncates each training trajectory at the step where the gathered information becomes sufficient, inserts a rationale that explains why the agent can answer at that step, and fine-tunes the agent with a higher loss weight on the rationale. Experiments on two repository-level QA benchmarks with two open-weight 8B models demonstrate that JET reduces tool calls by 11 to 33% on self-generated trajectories while largely preserving the score. On expert trajectories, JET makes 18 to 28% fewer tool calls than SFT while retaining most of the score gain of SFT. In both cases, JET also lowers the inference cost.

Traditional assessment and treatment approaches, while clinically grounded, often struggle with scalability, accessibility, and long-term engagement. In recent years, advances in digital technologies and artificial intelligence (AI) have enabled the development of novel, data-driven solutions for the prevention, detection, treatment, and recovery of behavioral addictions. This book presents a comprehensive survey of the management of behavioral addictions through digital technologies, bridging clinical foundations with state-of-the-art computational approaches. This book aims to provide researchers, clinicians, and practitioners with a unified, interdisciplinary perspective on the opportunities and limitations of AI-based digital technologies in behavioral addiction management,and to highlight pathways toward more scalable, personalized, and effective intervention strategies.

##### **Anticipating or Speculating? Making Futures Pay in AI Ethics**
2611.22268v2 by John P. Nelson

Philosophers have recently been trying to develop uniform decision rules for instances in which physicians' judgments diverge from the recommendations of medical artificial intelligence (AI) systems. I argue that this effort is fatally flawed because the performance and appropriate use of algorithmic recommendation systems by physicians are highly context-specific, empirical medical matters rather than epistemological ones. In such instances, general decision rules can be stipulated only to the extent that they abstract away from relevant, indeed determinative, contextual circumstances. The alleged problem of physician-AI disagreement, and others like it, play into longstanding disputes in technology ethics about the viability and utility of envisioning possible futures: is this useful "anticipation" or wasteful "speculation"? I suggest that the two may be distinguished. Anticipation explores plausible futures to help inform present actions under irreducible uncertainty. Speculation abstracts away uncertainty, developing formal solutions to stipulated circumstances whose relationship to the real present is often quite unclear. Efforts to develop crisp philosophical solutions to physician-AI disagreement, among other problems, fall into the latter category. Technology ethics should remember that fruitful futures thinking must both begin from and return to the messy, uncertain present.

##### **Not All Evidence Is Admissible: Evidence Governance for Tool-Augmented Medical Agents**
2611.22169v1 by Runxun Zhang, Onofrio Antonio Catalano, Jinqiao Wang, Jingwei Wei

Tool-augmented medical agents treat whatever their tools return as evidence. We show that this default is costly: medical tools fail silently, and a frozen reasoner cannot ignore what enters its context, so many cases it would have answered correctly are flipped to wrong once tool outputs are added. We call this evidence hijacking. $\mathcal{V}$-information explains the phenomenon: the reasoner has lost the option to ignore, so a tool output can carry negative usable information, and moving the decision of what to keep outside the reasoner restores that option. The theory further shows that part of any filtering gain comes merely from letting the reasoner see less, and only selection by content lets it see better. Building on this, we recast tool-augmented reasoning as evidence governance, which separates deciding what counts as evidence from the reasoner that answers, and propose context-independent evidence governance (CIEG), the first medical agent framework that rules, after acquisition, on whether each individual tool output may enter as evidence. Every tool output is written as a card that can be checked against the raw input; an independent checker that sees only the input and the cards decides which cards become evidence; and the reasoner answers from the admitted cards alone, with no training anywhere. On medical question answering, fundus glaucoma screening, and chest X-ray pneumonia classification, it achieves the highest accuracy among models of comparable scale, and significantly outperforms state-of-the-art tool-using agents and multi-agent systems built on the same backbone at lower cost.

In generated store data with known effects, the world model passes the checks of construction, stability, price direction and agreement with sales and with LightGBM, yet when merchants plan into high-demand weeks it states 1.33 to 1.40 times the true lift, and 1.23 to 1.42 times in eight variants of that world; LightGBM overstates it too (1.26, and 1.16 to 1.35). Both still pick the right weeks to promote, but stock ordered to the world model's lift is off by 0.44 to 0.48 of the true lift. The world model's stated response to a lasting regular-price change is 4.7 to 6.5 times the true -0.5, an error in kind that only a measured cause finds. Sanity checks for explanations test whether an explanation depends on the model; a decision needs to know whether it is right about the world, and these results show the first does not give the second. For the final tuned world models of our companion paper on nine data sets, baseline and lift add up under each model's own plan on every window, but a deeper price cut lowers the forecast in 29% to 83% of item-week steps on Dominick's. Across 703 configurations from the settings searches, checks passed were not related to accuracy (Spearman 0.02, 95% interval -0.02 to 0.05). A noisy benchmark of price episodes in Dominick's data is reported as a check of direction only.
